#10749. A 10-Point Checklist for Legacy Application Modernization

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Legacy applications often support critical business operations, but keeping them running can become increasingly difficult. Outdated infrastructure, growing technical debt, security vulnerabilities, and limited integration capabilities can make these systems expensive to maintain and harder to adapt to changing business needs.

Modernizing them involves more than upgrading technology. You need to understand what the existing application does, identify which components need improvement, and manage the transition without disrupting essential operations.

This 10-point legacy application modernization checklist walks you through the process, from assessing your existing systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, testing applications, and measuring results.

Legacy application modernization is the process of updating existing software applications, their architecture, infrastructure, or underlying technologies to meet current business and technical requirements.

Depending on the application's condition, modernization may involve moving it to a new platform, improving its code, redesigning its architecture, or replacing it with a new solution.

The goal is to improve performance, security, scalability, maintainability, and the application's ability to support future business requirements.

Modernization isn't necessarily the same as cloud migration. Moving an application from on-premises infrastructure to the cloud changes where it runs, but it may leave its underlying architecture and technical limitations unchanged.

An organization can modernize an application without moving it to the cloud or combine both approaches as part of a larger transformation.

Use this checklist to track the ten essential steps involved in modernizing legacy applications.

1. Define modernization goals: Identify business problems and establish measurable success criteria.

2. Assess existing applications: Review infrastructure, technical debt, code quality, and performance limitations.

3. Map application dependencies: Document critical workflows, integrations, and business rules.

4. Choose a modernization strategy: Select an approach based on business value, cost, complexity, and risk.

5. Develop a roadmap and budget: Prioritize activities, estimate costs, assign responsibilities, and establish milestones.

6. Plan architecture and security: Design the target environment and address security, compliance, and availability requirements.

7. Prepare data migration: Plan data transfer, integration changes, and validation procedures.

8. Modernize incrementally: Validate the approach with a pilot and implement changes in manageable phases.

9. Test and deploy: Verify functionality, performance, security, and data integrity before production rollout.

10. Monitor and optimize: Measure results, support users, and continuously improve the modernized application.

Let's examine what each step involves and how to determine whether you're ready to proceed.

Before selecting technologies or planning development work, identify what the modernization initiative needs to achieve.

Clear business objectives help prioritize investments, guide technical decisions, and establish whether the project delivers meaningful improvements.

Start by examining the problems affecting your application's performance, reliability, or business value.

For example, a legacy order management system might still process transactions reliably but require extensive manual work whenever the business adds a new sales channel.

In this situation, improving integration capabilities may be more valuable than replacing the entire application.

An application's age alone doesn't justify a major technology investment. Whether legacy modernization makes sense for your business depends on the problems the existing system creates, the cost of maintaining it, and whether smaller improvements could resolve those issues.

Once you've identified the problems, translate them into measurable objectives.

If slow performance is the primary concern, establish a target for response times or transaction processing speed. If maintenance expenses are rising, determine what reduction in operating costs would justify the investment.

Relevant key performance indicators (KPIs) may include:

These baseline measurements will help determine whether the changes deliver the expected results.

Completion check: You have a documented business case identifying the problems to address, expected outcomes, measurable KPIs, and stakeholders responsible for approving success.

A legacy application assessment provides a clear picture of your current technology environment.

Without this assessment, teams may underestimate project complexity, overlook critical components, or choose a modernization approach that doesn't address the application's actual limitations.

Begin by creating an inventory of your applications and their supporting technologies.

Document each application's purpose, business owner, primary users, operating environment, programming languages, frameworks, databases, and infrastructure requirements.

Also identify software versions, third-party dependencies, licensing restrictions, and components approaching or past their end-of-support dates.

If your organization operates several legacy applications, use a consistent assessment framework. This makes it easier to compare their business importance, technical condition, and modernization urgency.

Review the application's codebase to identify issues that make maintenance and development difficult.

Look for tightly coupled components, duplicated logic, outdated libraries, insufficient automated tests, and undocumented configurations.

Pay particular attention to areas where small modifications regularly cause unexpected problems elsewhere in the system.

For example, if payment processing, inventory management, and customer records share tightly connected code, modifying one function could require extensive testing across the entire application.

Your infrastructure assessment should also cover hardware limitations, database compatibility, backup capabilities, operating systems, and unsupported dependencies.

Analyze how the application performs during normal operations and periods of increased demand.

Review response times, database queries, resource utilization, transaction volumes, and recurring failures.

Existing monitoring data and incident records can reveal problems that aren't immediately visible during routine use.

Determine whether performance limitations originate from inefficient application code, infrastructure capacity, database design, or external dependencies.

This distinction matters because adding computing resources won't necessarily resolve problems caused by poor application logic.

Completion check: You have an application inventory and technical assessment report documenting major components, technical debt, operational risks, and performance limitations.

Legacy applications rarely operate independently. They exchange data with other systems, support interconnected business processes, and often contain business rules that aren't fully documented.

Understanding these dependencies is essential because changing one component can affect several other applications or workflows.

Document the processes that rely on the application, including the steps users follow, the data they need, and the expected outcomes.

Work with business users, application owners, operations teams, and developers familiar with the system.

Identify processes that cannot tolerate significant interruption, such as payment processing, order fulfillment, customer authentication, or financial reporting.

Don't overlook activities that happen less frequently. Month-end financial reports, annual calculations, and scheduled data exchanges may depend on functionality that isn't exercised during everyday operations.

Map how the application communicates with databases, APIs, third-party services, and other internal systems.

Include scheduled jobs, file transfers, message queues, authentication services, and reporting tools.

Some legacy applications contain years of accumulated business logic.

Rules for pricing, approvals, calculations, or customer eligibility may exist only in source code, database procedures, or undocumented workflows.

Identify these rules before modifying the underlying implementation.

Where possible, create automated tests that capture existing expected behavior. These tests can help verify that the modernized application preserves required functionality.

Completion check: Critical workflows, system dependencies, integrations, and business rules are documented and reviewed by the relevant business and technical owners.

The appropriate modernization strategy depends on the application's technical condition, business importance, budget, and future requirements.

Not every application needs to be rebuilt. Some systems require targeted improvements, while others may be better replaced or retired.

Common legacy application modernization strategies include:

Different applications within the same organization may require different approaches.

Evaluate each option against your technical constraints and business requirements.

Consider implementation effort, expected benefits, ongoing maintenance costs, required expertise, and potential disruption.

For example, replatforming may be suitable for an application that operates reliably but depends on aging infrastructure. Rearchitecting may be necessary when tightly coupled components prevent independent updates or scaling.

A complete rebuild requires particular caution. Recreating undocumented functionality can be expensive, and important business rules may be lost if the existing system hasn't been thoroughly assessed.

The right strategy should solve the identified problems without introducing unnecessary cost or complexity.

Completion check: Each application has a selected modernization strategy, documented rationale, estimated effort, and an approved decision on whether to modernize, retain, replace, or retire it.

Once you've selected a modernization approach, convert it into an actionable plan.

Your roadmap should establish what will change, in what order, who will be responsible, and how progress will be evaluated.

Prioritize modernization work according to business value, technical complexity, urgency, and dependencies.

Applications with unsupported components or significant security risks may require early attention. However, starting with the most complex application isn't always the best choice.

A smaller, lower-risk application can provide an opportunity to validate tools, workflows, and team capabilities before modernizing more critical systems.

Dependencies also affect the sequence. For example, a shared authentication service may need to be updated before applications that rely on it can be migrated.

Prepare a budget covering the full modernization process rather than development alone.

Compare upfront implementation expenses with expected operating costs over time.

A modernization approach with a lower initial cost may still require significant ongoing maintenance.

Allow contingency for unexpected issues, particularly when legacy documentation is incomplete or system dependencies remain uncertain.

Divide the project into phases with defined deliverables and approval requirements.

Assign responsible owners for architecture, development, security, data migration, testing, and business acceptance.

Establish how project risks will be reported and who can approve changes to scope, budget, or deployment schedules.

Keep the roadmap adaptable. Findings from pilot implementations or migration rehearsals may require changes to the original plan.

Completion check: Your modernization roadmap includes prioritized activities, estimated costs, responsible owners, dependencies, milestones, and approval checkpoints.

The target architecture determines how the modernized application will operate, scale, integrate with other systems, and remain maintainable.

Security and compliance requirements should be incorporated into the design from the beginning.

Choose an architecture that fits the application's actual requirements.

Possible approaches include a modular monolith, service-oriented architecture, microservices, or event-driven components.

Microservices aren't automatically the best option. Although they allow independent deployment and scaling of components, they also introduce complexity in communication, monitoring, deployment, and data management.

For smaller applications or tightly integrated business processes, a well-structured modular architecture may be easier to maintain.

Document architectural decisions covering application components, data storage, interfaces, infrastructure requirements, and operational responsibilities.

Identify security weaknesses that should be resolved during modernization.

Review authentication, authorization, encryption, secrets management, logging, and access to sensitive information.

Evaluate third-party dependencies and establish processes for vulnerability scanning, patching, and security testing.

Regulatory and contractual requirements should also influence the target design.

Security controls should be applied throughout development and deployment rather than added shortly before release.

Define how the application will remain available and recover from failures.

Establish recovery time objectives (RTOs) and recovery point objectives (RPOs) based on business requirements.

Plan backup procedures, restoration testing, appropriate redundancy, and monitoring for critical components.

Validate that the target environment can support the required availability and recovery objectives.

Completion check: The target architecture has documented design decisions, security controls, compliance requirements, availability objectives, and recovery procedures.

Data migration is one of the most sensitive stages of legacy application modernization.

Legacy databases may contain inconsistent records, outdated formats, duplicated information, or relationships that newer systems handle differently.

A migration strategy needs to explain how data will be transferred, validated, and made available without compromising business operations.

Begin by reviewing the quality and structure of existing data.

Identify missing values, duplicate records, inconsistent formats, obsolete fields, and relationships that could create migration problems.

Document how existing data structures correspond to the target system.

For example, a legacy customer database might store contact details in a single field, while the new application requires separate fields for email addresses and phone numbers.

The migration process needs defined transformation rules to handle these differences.

Determine which records must be migrated, archived, retained for compliance, or excluded.

Choose a migration approach based on data volume, application availability requirements, and acceptable downtime.

Depending on the system, this may involve a scheduled migration during a maintenance window, staged transfers, or synchronization between old and new environments.

Identify integrations that will need modification.

Where applications exchange information through APIs, files, or messaging systems, verify that the modernized implementation supports the required data formats and business behavior.

If the legacy and modernized applications will operate simultaneously, define how data updates will be synchronized between them.

Test the migration process using representative datasets before transferring production data.

Validate record counts, field values, relationships, and critical business calculations.

For sensitive or business-critical information, use appropriate reconciliation methods, including integrity checks where applicable.

Document how inconsistencies will be identified and resolved.

Also establish what happens if migration fails or the application needs to return to its previous environment.

Completion check: Data mapping rules are approved, migration rehearsals have been completed, and reconciliation procedures can verify data accuracy.

Modernizing an entire application in a single major release can increase project risk, particularly when the system supports critical business operations.

An incremental approach allows teams to validate decisions, identify problems earlier, and limit the impact of failures.

Select a manageable component or workload to validate your modernization approach.

The pilot should test an important assumption, such as whether the target platform supports the application, whether a legacy integration can be replaced, or whether an architectural change improves performance.

Define success criteria before implementation.

A useful pilot should provide evidence that the approach is technically feasible and that the team can manage its operational requirements.

Use the findings to improve estimates, identify missing skills, and refine the broader implementation plan.

Divide the application into manageable migration or development units where its architecture allows.

One approach is the Strangler Fig pattern, which gradually replaces legacy functionality while the remaining parts of the original application continue operating.

For example, an organization might modernize customer notifications first, followed by reporting and then core transaction processing.

This can reduce the risks associated with replacing an entire system at once.

However, incremental modernization also requires careful coordination. Temporary integrations, duplicated components, and parallel systems can introduce complexity.

Each phase should have defined boundaries, validation requirements, and completion criteria.

Establish repeatable processes for developing, testing, and deploying application changes.

Continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines can automate builds, execute tests, and promote approved changes through environments.

Use version control, consistent configuration management, and infrastructure as code where appropriate.

Include security and quality checks in the delivery process to reduce the risk of introducing defects during frequent releases.

Completion check: The pilot has validated the selected approach, implementation is divided into manageable phases, and repeatable build, test, and deployment processes are established.

Modernization isn't complete when the new application runs successfully in a development environment.

It must perform the required business functions, communicate correctly with connected systems, and meet operational expectations before production rollout.

Develop a testing plan based on the application's critical workflows and identified risks.

Automate repeatable tests where practical, especially for critical business processes.

Compare results against the performance baselines and acceptance criteria established earlier.

Business users should verify that the modernized application supports their actual workflows.

User acceptance testing (UAT) should include routine operations, exception handling, reporting requirements, and tasks that depend on specialized business knowledge.

Use representative migrated data during testing.

Confirm that critical records, calculations, and reports remain accurate after the transition.

Resolve issues affecting essential operations before approving production deployment.

Prepare a documented deployment plan outlining the sequence of activities, responsible teams, communication channels, and expected service interruptions.

Choose a deployment method appropriate for the application, such as a phased rollout, canary release, or scheduled cutover.

Establish measurable conditions that would stop deployment or trigger a rollback.

Rollback planning must account for data changes made after cutover. Restoring an earlier application version doesn't automatically restore database consistency, especially if new transactions have already been processed.

Rehearse deployment and recovery procedures in a suitable non-production environment.

Completion check: Required tests have passed, business owners have approved acceptance criteria, and deployment, cutover, and recovery procedures have been validated.

The initial deployment marks the beginning of ongoing operation rather than the end of modernization.

The application needs continued monitoring and maintenance to ensure that expected improvements are sustained.

Compare post-modernization results against the baseline established at the beginning of the project.

Track metrics linked to your business objectives, such as application availability, maintenance costs, response times, deployment frequency, security vulnerabilities, and user satisfaction.

Use monitoring tools, logs, traces, and alerts to identify problems that may not be visible through basic availability checks.

Review performance after deployment and at agreed intervals to determine whether the modernization initiative is meeting its objectives.

Technical improvements may introduce changes to established workflows.

Provide relevant training, updated documentation, and support resources for users and operational teams.

Ensure that administrators and developers understand the new architecture, deployment processes, monitoring tools, and incident response procedures.

Collect user feedback to identify usability issues or missing functionality.

Use production data and operational feedback to guide further improvements.

Optimization may involve updating dependencies, improving database queries, adjusting infrastructure capacity, strengthening security controls, or simplifying application components.

Once replacement functionality is stable and approved, begin decommissioning components that are no longer needed.

Before retirement, confirm that dependent applications have been migrated, necessary records are retained, and archival or compliance obligations have been met.

Removing unused infrastructure and software can reduce maintenance costs and unnecessary security exposure.

Completion check: Monitoring and support responsibilities are established, modernization KPIs are reviewed, and unused legacy components have an approved retirement plan.

Organizations without the internal resources to manage legacy modernization may need support from an external software development company.

Depending on the project, a development partner can assist with application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, system integration, data migration, and implementation.

Companies offering custom software development and application modernization services include:

Idea Maker: Provides custom software development and legacy application modernization services, including application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, and cloud migration.

ScienceSoft: Offers custom software development, legacy software modernization, application reengineering, cloud migration, and system integration services.

Itransition: Provides custom application development, legacy application modernization, system integration, software migration, and post-modernization support.

Simform: Offers digital product engineering, software modernization, cloud and platform engineering, and data integration services.

These are examples of providers rather than a ranked comparison.

When evaluating a development partner, consider their experience with your existing technology stack, ability to handle complex integrations, approach to data security, and experience delivering projects with similar requirements.

Ask prospective providers how they assess legacy systems, manage migration risks, preserve critical business logic, and validate application functionality before deployment.

The main steps are defining business goals, assessing existing applications, mapping dependencies, choosing a modernization strategy, developing a roadmap, designing the target architecture, planning data migration, implementing changes, testing and deploying, and continuously optimizing the application.

The exact sequence may vary depending on the application's complexity and business requirements.

Start by evaluating the application's business value, technical condition, dependencies, and future requirements.

Rehosting or replatforming may be sufficient when the application functions well and infrastructure is the primary concern.

Refactoring or rearchitecting may be necessary when code quality or architectural limitations affect maintainability and scalability.

Rebuilding or replacing becomes an option when the existing implementation no longer meets business needs or would be too costly to maintain.

Compare expected benefits, implementation costs, risks, and ongoing maintenance requirements before making a decision.

Common risks include undocumented dependencies, data loss or corruption, unexpected downtime, security vulnerabilities, integration failures, and loss of essential business functionality.

Projects may also experience delays or budget overruns when technical complexity is underestimated.

These risks can be reduced through early assessment, phased implementation, migration rehearsals, comprehensive testing, and validated recovery procedures.

The timeline depends on the application's size, architecture, technical debt, data complexity, integrations, and selected modernization approach.

A limited rehosting or refactoring project generally requires less effort than rebuilding a large application or modernizing several interconnected systems.

A reliable estimate requires an initial technical assessment. A pilot or proof of concept can help validate assumptions about implementation effort and potential challenges.

Measure success against the business and technical objectives established before implementation.

Relevant indicators include application performance, availability, maintenance costs, security improvements, deployment speed, and user satisfaction.

For example, if reducing maintenance effort was the primary goal, lower support costs and faster resolution of application issues may be more meaningful than infrastructure performance alone.

Modernization success should be evaluated over time rather than only immediately after deployment.

Legacy application modernization works best when it follows a structured plan based on business needs and the realities of existing technology.

The ten steps in this checklist cover the key decisions and activities involved, from assessing legacy systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, validating functionality, and maintaining the resulting application.

Careful dependency mapping, realistic planning, incremental implementation, and thorough testing help reduce risks throughout the transition.

Start by assessing your existing applications and defining what modernization needs to achieve. Those findings will help you identify which systems deserve attention, where to invest resources, and how to approach modernization without introducing unnecessary complexity.

#10745. Tiny robots powered by light can hunt down and collect bacteria

Robots small enough to operate in the microbial world could give scientists a new way to directly handle objects that are impossible to manipulate by hand. These tiny machines are around 50 times smaller than the diameter of a human hair, bringing researchers closer to the long-pursued goal of interacting directly with the microscopic world.

That capability could be especially useful for biological materials in water, including individual cells and bacteria. Precisely controlling and moving objects at this scale has been a persistent challenge. The newly developed nanorobots show that it is already possible to collect bacteria, transport them, and release them at selected locations.

One of the biggest obstacles in developing machines this small is finding an effective way to propel and control them. At Julius-Maximilians-Universität Würzburg (JMU), a research team led by Professor Bert Hecht has been developing a solution that uses the recoil produced by individual photons to move microscopic devices known as microdrones.

The devices contain as many as four plasmonic nanoantennas. These antennas absorb light with a particular color and helicity, then emit that light in a specific direction. Redirecting each photon creates a tiny recoil force, similar in principle to the recoil produced when a bullet is fired. Because the microdrones have so little mass, those extremely small forces can generate substantial acceleration and speed.

For the latest work, the researchers reduced the size of their light-powered robots even further, producing devices smaller than one micrometer. Simplifying the steering system was an important part of achieving that size while retaining propulsion based on photon recoil.

The new control method takes advantage of nanoscale antenna wires built into the robot. These wires naturally tend to align with the polarization direction of incoming light. By changing the light's polarization, the researchers can control which direction the nanorobot faces. At the same time, photon recoil continues to propel it forward, creating a steering system that works somewhat like the directional control used in larger vehicles.

"In essence, we have built a light-driven nanorobot that can track down and collect bacteria," says Jin Qin, lead experimental scientist of the study. "By simplifying the design, we reached a size at which these robots can operate directly in the microbial world - almost like microscopic cleaning devices."

The robots are also highly maneuverable. They can make extremely fast 90° turns, helping them scan broad areas of a sample in an organized and efficient way. They can also selectively capture, carry, and release substantial numbers of bacteria.

Under controlled laboratory conditions, this means the nanorobots can effectively "clean" microscopic environments. They gather bacteria from one area and deposit them at specifically chosen locations.

"This is a striking example of how light can be used not only to observe the microscopic world, but also to actively shape it," adds Bert Hecht. "The idea of tiny robotic cleaners may sound futuristic, but we are already demonstrating the physical principles that make it possible."

The robots remain fully maneuverable even while carrying larger groups of bacteria, although their speed decreases somewhat under the added load. That ability to keep functioning while transporting larger bacterial clusters points to possible future uses in microbiology, biomedical research, and precise manipulation of materials at the microscopic scale.

#10741. Pokemon VGC competitor claims he was wrongfully suspended after another player used his identity at Worlds

A Pokemon VGC competitor claims he was wrongfully suspended after another player used his tournament identity at the 2026 Pokemon World Championships.

Johnny Chiou made the claim in a lengthy post on X, saying his friend Chen-Wei Liu discovered that his RK9 account had been transferred to another email address without his permission.

According to Chiou, another player later appeared at Worlds using Player ID 5283777, a number Liu says was assigned to him and used during the 2024 Pokemon World Championships. Liu reported the issue to Pokemon support, but was later issued an indefinite suspension for deception related to his Worlds qualification and attendance.

We Demand Transparency and Justice for CHEN-WEI LIU. @playpokemon My friend, CHEN-WEI LIU, discovered that his RK9 account had been transferred to another email address without his knowledge or authorization. This raised serious concerns that his qualification for the 2026…

A Player ID is a unique number used to track a competitor’s tournament history, results and qualifications. Pokemon’s official rules say players must have their own personal Player ID and use only one number.

The supporting timeline says Liu noticed on September 4 that his RK9 profile and tournament history had disappeared. He contacted RK9 the following day and later provided the Player ID he had used at the 2024 Worlds.

The evidence package includes a photograph of a match slip from August 28, the first day of the 2026 Worlds VGC competition. It lists I-Shang Chang from Taiwan against Jin Yang Lee, with Player ID 5283777 printed under Chang’s name. Public VGC standings also list the match.

Chiou’s post refers to the player as I-Hsiang Chang, while the match slip and public standings spell the name I-Shang Chang.

The timeline says RK9 confirmed on September 17 that Liu’s account had been moved to a new email address before Worlds after an email-change request was submitted with the correct credentials. Liu allegedly replied that he never requested or authorized the transfer.

Liu then contacted The Pokemon Company International on September 20 and asked for an investigation. He followed up in October, according to the timeline, but says he received no meaningful explanation.

Pokemon pro sparks controversy after “cheating” at official VGC tournament

On October 16, Pokemon issued Liu a disciplinary notice under case DISINC-007883. The notice said the alleged violation concerned his “qualification and attendance for the 2026 Pokemon World Championships.”

It cited Section 4.3 of the Pokemon Standards of Conduct and listed:

“Deception, defrauding, or engagement of a fellow program participant under false pretenses.”

The notice suspended Liu indefinitely from Play Pokemon events, including both competing and attending as a spectator. It also said he could submit an appeal after 90 days, beginning January 13, 2027.

However, the notice does not explain what Pokemon believes Liu actually did. It does not say whether the company believes he knowingly allowed another person to use his account, misrepresented his identity, participated in a qualification scheme or misled tournament staff.

The timeline says Liu submitted a formal rebuttal on October 17 and followed up five days later. According to the material posted by Chiou, no response had arrived by November 10.

The dispute follows several other Play Pokemon controversies covered by Dexerto this year, including a former Pokemon World Champion who said he was barred from Worlds after a Pokemon Center line dispute, a Pokemon TCG veteran who said he was banned for life after calling out a judge, and a Pokemon Go champion who challenged the decision to strip his Orlando Regional title.

For now, the public record leaves two competing explanations. Liu says someone else may have used his account and tournament identity. Pokemon’s notice indicates that it believes Liu was involved in deception, but does not publicly explain how.

#10740. Scientists turn DNA into a memory device that uses 100x less power

DNA serves as the genetic blueprint for every living organism, but it is also an extraordinarily dense way to store information. A single gram can hold about 215 million gigabytes of data. Bringing that remarkable storage capacity into electronics could lead to more efficient data centers, faster processing and systems capable of handling increasingly complex information.

The challenge has been finding a way to make biological DNA function effectively alongside electronic materials. Penn State researchers have now developed an approach designed to overcome that incompatibility.

The work, published in Advanced Functional Materials and the subject of a patent application, relies on two key components. One is synthetic DNA, made from commercially available, chemically engineered molecules arranged into short genetic sequences tailored for specific electronic requirements. The other is crystalline perovskite, a semiconductor already used in technologies including solar cells, lasers and data storage devices.

"Biology and electronics are different domains," said Kavya S. Keremane, co-corresponding author and postdoctoral researcher in materials science and engineering at Penn State. "Bridging these two fields required developing an entirely new materials platform that allows them to function seamlessly together. By combining the information storage capabilities of DNA with the exceptional electronic properties of perovskite semiconductors, we created a bio-hybrid system that fundamentally changes how low-power memory devices can be designed."

Using these materials, the team created a memory resistor, known as a "memristor," that operates with very little energy. Unlike ordinary resistors, which maintain a set resistance to electrical current in devices ranging from cell phones to space shuttles and lose their stored information when power disappears, memristors can preserve a record of previous electrical activity. They can remember the direction in which current previously flowed even after the power source is removed.

That ability allows information to be stored and processed in the same place, resembling the way neurons function in the brain. Such an arrangement could support more simultaneous and sophisticated forms of data processing. According to the researchers, however, practical commercial systems would still require enough storage capacity and electrical power to become costly and inefficient without DNA's ability to pack enormous amounts of information into a very small space while consuming little energy.

"As the demand for artificial intelligence (AI) grows, we need a new strategy for low-power, high-storage devices," said Bed Poudel, co-corresponding author and research professor of materials science and engineering at Penn State.

Poudel said AI and other emerging technologies are expected to depend increasingly on neuromorphic computing, which is designed to operate more like the human brain. Such systems can evaluate multiple inputs at once while making decisions informed by previous experiences and future priorities.

"Usually, it takes more power to store more information. Our device, however, consumes 100 times less power and the storage capacity is higher than traditional storage devices, like flash drives."

To construct the device, the researchers added silver nanoparticles to a layer of customized DNA sequences -- specially designed to be of certain compositions and lengths -- that was integrated with thin films of perovskite.

This technique, called "doping," involves introducing a small amount of another material to produce specific properties. In this case, adding the silver nanoparticles allowed the DNA to conduct electricity while also helping its molecular units line up in a more orderly arrangement.

Synthetic DNA offered another important advantage over natural DNA. Unlike natural DNA -- long, entangled strands that behave like wet spaghetti when handled -- short and rigid pieces of synthetic DNA can be arranged with much greater precision at extremely small scales.

According to co-author Neela H. Yennawar, research professor and director of the Penn State Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences' Biomolecular Interactions Core Facility, molecularly engineered DNA can provide structural organization, adjustable electrical conductivity and functional control that natural DNA cannot achieve when incorporated into thin films.

"We can computationally determine exactly which sequences we need and how long they should be, and then we can rationally design them with synthetic DNA," Yennawar said. "These structures can be systematically doped with silver and other ions and engineered to interface seamlessly with perovskites -- transforming DNA from a biological macromolecule into a programmable, multifunctional nanomaterials platform."

When combined, the silver-doped DNA and perovskite formed bio-hybrid pathways that directed the flow of electrical current through the device.

The researchers found that electrons moved reliably when they applied less than 0.1 volt -- for comparison, standard U.S. outlets have 120 volts -- and the device responded predictably when the direction of the current was changed.

The carefully designed DNA structures, combined with the perovskite, also helped make the device unusually stable. According to the team, it continued operating consistently at temperatures approaching 250 degrees Fahrenheit and remained functional at room temperature for more than six weeks, substantially exceeding the performance standards of existing perovskite-based memory storage devices.

The researchers also reported that the new system could perform the same memory function as comparable technologies while consuming only one-tenth as much power. That level of efficiency could make the approach especially attractive for future electronics designed to handle large amounts of information with lower energy demands.

"Using just the DNA or just perovskite alone did not produce near as robust a result as the combination," Keremane said. "It's this combination that enables a very high memory storage density that requires very little power."

A New Direction for Bio-Inspired Electronics

The team now plans to improve the technology further and explore additional uses for bio-inspired electronic systems.

"Nature has the solution -- we just have to find it and apply it," Poudel said. "This work of integrating DNA into electronics to do amazing things gives a glimpse into what is possible."

In addition to Keremane, Yennawar and Poudel, other Penn State co-authors include co-corresponding author Luyao Zheng, postdoctoral research in materials science and engineering; Haodong Wu, doctoral student in materials science and engineering; Jiamao Zheng, who was a master's student in materials science and engineering at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State; Shashank Priya, who was a professor of materials science and engineering at the time of research; and Chiranth C. Ravi, who was a master's student in the Huck Institutes of the Life Sciences at the time of research and has since graduated from Penn State. Abhinav Gorthy and co-corresponding author Rashmi Jha, chemical engineering and materials science, University of Minnesota, also contributed.

The U.S. National Science Foundation, the National Institutes of Health, Penn State and the University of Minnesota supported this research.

Materials provided by Penn State. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10735. NASA just used satellites and debris to navigate without GPS

NASA's Starling mission has reached another milestone in autonomous spaceflight by demonstrating a system that can determine a satellite's orbital position using other objects in space as reference points rather than depending on an external navigation network.

The technology, called FALCON (Fast Autonomous Lost-in-space Catalog-based Optical Navigation), is designed to help spacecraft operate with greater independence. Such capabilities could become increasingly important as NASA pursues missions farther from Earth, including lunar satellite swarms, distributed science missions, and future human exploration.

Satellites near Earth commonly rely on GPS for navigation, but those signals may be weak, unreliable, or unavailable around the Moon and in deep space. FALCON offers an alternative by allowing spacecraft to determine where they are using what they can observe around them.

The FALCON payload is a joint flight experiment developed by NASA and EraDrive, a startup that emerged from Stanford University. The system combines EraDrive's Era-Core flight software and embedded algorithms with Starling's cameras and an onboard catalog of known satellites. Together, these tools enable GPS-independent navigation while also helping the spacecraft track activity and objects in its surroundings.

"FALCON is yet another success for the Starling demonstration mission. The results from FALCON can have far-reaching implications for on-orbit space-traffic monitoring, collision avoidance, and alternative navigation," said Roger Hunter, program manager for NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program at NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley. "The number of 'firsts' from Starling just keeps growing."

Using Satellites and Debris as Reference Points

The FALCON demonstration evaluated two related capabilities using Starling's onboard star tracker cameras. These cameras are standard spacecraft instruments that detect bright objects in space and help determine a spacecraft's orientation and position.

During position, navigation, and timing experiments, FALCON identified objects seen by Starling's cameras, including other spacecraft and orbital debris, and compared them with a publicly available catalog of known space objects maintained by the U.S. Department of War. Once the observed objects were identified and verified, FALCON used them as reference points to calculate Starling's orbit.

A separate set of experiments tested whether the spacecraft could improve orbital estimates for the objects it observed. Mission controllers loaded a catalog containing approximately 20,000 space objects and their predicted orbits onto Starling.

FALCON then compared the catalog information with observations gathered by Starling's cameras. Using those measurements, it calculated Starling's position while also refining the estimated locations of other objects in space. The resulting estimates were even more precise than the existing catalog data.

Over a period of three days, FALCON improved the known orbits of more than 200 objects without any intervention from operators on the ground.

A First for Autonomous Optical Navigation

FALCON's ability to determine its own orbit represents a first for a spacecraft navigating with optical cameras based on its position relative to other objects in space. In separate catalog updating tests, Starling also generated better onboard predictions of object positions than those supplied by ground stations.

These abilities could be especially valuable for future networks of satellites operating without GPS. Coordinated satellite systems may play an important role in supporting human activities on the surfaces of the Moon or Mars.

Precise spacecraft positioning is also essential for distributed science missions, where measurements collected from several locations in space must be accurately aligned. For space traffic management, autonomous navigation and onboard catalog updates could reduce dependence on ground-based tracking networks while improving collision avoidance.

From University Research to Commercial Technology

The FALCON experiment also demonstrates NASA's role in helping university research develop into commercial technology. The work began as a University SmallSat Technology Partnerships project before developing into EraDrive, which is now commercializing its Era-Core software and related hardware for wider use.

Starling gave the technology an opportunity to be tested under real conditions in orbit, demonstrating how advanced flight software can allow satellites to function as increasingly autonomous navigators.

Later this year, Starling, which launched in 2023, will expand the FALCON experiment using Era-Core. The mission's four spacecraft will share tracking information with one another and use those combined observations to refine their positions collectively.

NASA's Ames Research Center in California's Silicon Valley leads the Starling mission. NASA's Small Spacecraft and Distributed Systems program, based at Ames and within the Research and Technology Mission Directorate, funds and manages the Starling mission.

Materials provided by NASA. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10734. Pin vs Noon in 2026: Which AI Recruiter Fits Your Team?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Buyers are moving from pilots to purchases: 52% of talent leaders told Korn Ferry they plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026 (1,674 leaders, October 2025), and SHRM's 2026 research puts recruiting at 27% of organizations using AI, the most of any HR function. For a wider field, see our guide to the best AI recruiting agents.

Noon details come from Noon's own pages or a named third party, checked October 14, 2026.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform that assists recruiters by finding candidates across the 850M+ profiles Pin says it covers across North America and Europe, writing outreach from each person's profile signals and booking interviews. Candidate data comes from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. You search in plain language, paste a job description, upload ideal résumés or import a role from your ATS. Each ranked candidate has a fit score and an AI-written reason, and the model learns from your team's accept and reject feedback.

The Pin recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist you approve each step. In Autopilot it sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates your ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Closing and offers stay human. The agent asks clarifying questions ("Is remote considered?"), shows every action in a feed and works 24/7 alongside your team. Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, says: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

Noon (noon.ai) is an AI recruiting platform from Portal Inc. in New York whose agents source across the open web, run email and SMS outreach and book interviews. You start from a pasted job description or posting URL. Noon extracts the title, location and "non-negotiables," and its Autopilot sourcing agent keeps searching as new people enter the market. Candidates land in a ranked feed with reasoning, and thumbs-up and thumbs-down feedback calibrates the search per role.

Noon also extends further down the funnel. Its AI Interviewer runs voice screening calls, its Notetaker joins live interviews, and Inbound Screening ranks ATS applicants and flags suspected fraud. Those tools sit downstream of sourcing. Pin concentrates on the outbound workflow, from search through scheduling, and adds the team layer around it: a shared inbox, a dedupe layer so two recruiters never contact the same person, funnel dashboards and a multi-client CRM for agencies.

Pin gives you natural-language filters plus advanced filters you set yourself, and paid plans include unlimited searches.

Noon steers the search through criteria set per role. Its pages describe titles, locations, years of experience, target-company lists and must-haves, plus non-negotiables Noon says it never relaxes. You adjust by chat and feedback. We found no public description of an open-ended filter panel like Pin's, so ask for one in a demo if ad hoc searching matters.

On consistency, Pin returns a ranked shortlist with a reason for every candidate from the first search, then improves as your team accepts and rejects. Noon says its feedback loop calibrates the model per role, but it doesn't say how long that takes. Compare day one with day ten yourself (see the trial plan below).

Pin writes each message from the candidate's profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice. Its sequences run across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence, which the recruiter sends. Sequences adapt per candidate, and any reply pauses the sequence. Free is email-only. Replies go to a shared team inbox, and a dedupe layer stops two recruiters contacting the same person.

Noon writes an intro for each candidate and drops it into sequence templates you control. Sequences run on email and SMS.

The practical difference is how contact data is packaged. Every Pin plan includes contact credits (5 a week on Free, 500 a month on Solo, 1,000 per user a month on Professional, unlimited on Business), and a personal email, work email or phone number uses 1 credit. Noon says contact enrichment is unlimited. Revealing email and phone for 300 candidates a month uses 600 of the 1,000 credits included with each Professional seat.

Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in your ATS. Pin lists 120+ ATS integrations, including Greenhouse, Lever, Ashby, Bullhorn, iCIMS and Jobvite. ATS integration is an add-on on Solo and Professional and is included on Business. Teams without an ATS can track candidates in Pin's Kanban pipeline, a mini ATS from first touch to signed offer.

Noon's AI Coordinator answers candidate questions and books interviews through your Calendly availability, then handles reschedules. Its integrations page lists 40+ systems, including all six above plus Workday, SAP SuccessFactors, UKG, Loxo and Recruiterflow, and Noon says ATS sync is part of its plan. Several other Noon pages still say "20+," so confirm your ATS is live before you commit.

Pin publishes its pricing on its pricing page (checked October 14, 2026). Free is $0 (5 contact credits a week, 1 job, 1 agent, email-only sequences, 3 searches a day). Solo is $99 per user per month billed annually, for one seat, with 500 contact credits a month included. Professional is $135 per user per month billed annually or $179 month to month, with 2 agents and 1,000 contact credits per user each month, a shared inbox and 5 mailboxes per user. Business is custom, with unlimited seats, credits and agents and ATS integration included.

Noon sells one plan, quoted per team. Its pricing page lists unlimited sourcing, email enrichment, contacts, agents and seats, with no credits or per-seat fees. Its get-started page invites teams to create a free account, yet most "Start free" buttons open a demo-booking calendar, and Noon does not say what the free account includes.

Worked example: three recruiters on Pin Professional

With Noon, you won't know the number until after a sales call. Noon prices one flat plan per team and says adding seats doesn't change it, while Pin's cost is known before you talk to anyone and scales predictably per seat. Get Noon's quote in writing and compare it against these figures, keeping in mind that Noon's bundle includes screening and note-taking tools you may not need.

Noon also offers a 10x ROI guarantee on annual plans. According to Noon's guarantee terms (checked October 14, 2026), it values recruiter time at $60 an hour, relies on Noon's own measurement of time saved and requires minimum weekly use.

Pin is SOC 2 Type 2 audited by a third party, encrypts data at rest and in transit, and is GDPR and CCPA ready, with documents in its trust center at trust.pin.com. SSO/SAML and SCIM come with the Business plan.

Noon says it holds SOC 2 Type II and GDPR compliance, runs an annual third-party penetration test and supports SSO and SAML. According to its security page (updated September 3, 2026), Noon also offers data residency options for enterprise agreements and makes the SOC 2 report and pen-test summary available under NDA. The page does not mention CCPA.

Pin is built for agencies scaling outbound, and for recruiters and teams of any size that want a known price and hands-on control. It brings a multi-client recruiting CRM, per-seat pricing that scales predictably and a Business plan with unlimited seats. Pin assists recruiters rather than replacing them. Noon's homepage claims 40% faster time to hire, a vendor-reported figure.

Noon is good for teams with heavy inbound application volume that want voice screening and interview notes bundled with sourcing, and that are comfortable buying through a sales quote. The caveats are an unpublished price and free-account details, and only three reviews on Trustpilot.

When Pin is not the right fit: teams that only need high-volume inbound applicant screening.

When Noon is a harder fit: buyers who need a price before a sales call, teams that want a free plan with published details, and anyone who wants independent reviews to check (three on Trustpilot, no G2 listing found).

Choose Pin if you: - want a published price and a free plan with no credit card - want to start in Assist mode and hand steps to Autopilot as you build trust - run a team where several recruiters share candidates and need dedupe and a shared inbox - run an agency and need pipelines per client

#10732. Florida woman arrested after targeting ex’s Pokemon cards because he wasn’t giving her attention

A Florida woman has been arrested after allegedly targeting her ex-boyfriend’s Pokemon card collection during an argument, before returning to his apartment for a bong and ending up facing felony charges.

Nadia Hussain, 23, was arrested in Orlando on November 7 following a bizarre confrontation involving Pokemon cards, a cellphone, and an interrupted 911 call.

According to an Orlando Police Department arrest affidavit obtained by Guessing Headlights, Hussain had spent the night at her ex-boyfriend’s apartment before the pair got into an argument.

Her ex, Jordan Bonilla, asked her to leave, but she allegedly refused, prompting him to begin recording the confrontation.

Police say Hussain knocked the phone from his hand, struck him several times, and repeatedly demanded that he “delete the video!”

She then turned her attention to his Pokemon card collection, which was sitting on the living room table.

According to WFTV, Hussain later admitted to police that she deliberately grabbed the cards after realizing how much it upset her ex-boyfriend.

She also told investigators she had been purposefully annoying him because she felt he wasn’t showing her enough affection.

Bonilla eventually escorted Hussain to her car before rushing back upstairs and locking his apartment door.

However, the encounter wasn’t over. Hussain returned, claiming she had left her bong inside.

Despite promising not to enter, she allegedly forced her way into the apartment when Bonilla opened the door to hand it over.

Police say she headed straight toward the living room before Bonilla blocked her path. She then entered a bathroom with a second entrance to the living room, leading him to believe she was attempting to reach his Pokemon cards again.

Despite all the chaos surrounding the Pokemon collection, no theft charges involving the cards were filed.

More recently, the FBI began investigating thieves using stolen credit cards to purchase rare Pokemon cards from trading card stores.

In Hussain’s case, there is no indication that any Pokemon cards were stolen or damaged, although the confrontation has left her facing criminal charges. The allegations have not been proven in court.

#10729. IBM quantum computer solves classically intractable problem in 15 minutes

Scientists used a new error correction strategy to encode 70 logical qubits and tackle a problem considered classically intractable.

The quantum computation was completed in about 15 minutes, while leading classical computing methods would require an impractical amount of time to perform the same task.

IBM and researchers at the University of Chicago have announced a quantum computing demonstration that meets key criteria for quantum advantage. The experiment performed a calculation beyond the practical reach of leading classical simulation techniques while also providing evidence that the quantum computer produced a reliable result.

In a new paper, "Sampling hard circuits with verifiably high fidelity," the researchers describe how they achieved both goals using a newly designed form of encoded quantum circuit. The work represents one of the largest demonstrations of logical quantum computing reported so far.

The circuits and experimental results have also been made publicly available through the Quantum Advantage Tracker.

Why Quantum Results Are So Difficult to Verify

Researchers have long used a benchmark called random circuit sampling (RCS) to explore whether quantum computers can outperform conventional machines.

In simplified terms, RCS challenges a quantum computer to generate patterns that become so complicated that a classical computer cannot efficiently reproduce them. That makes the benchmark useful for testing the limits of classical simulation.

But it also creates a major problem. Once the quantum calculation becomes too difficult for a classical computer to reproduce, verifying that the quantum machine actually produced the correct result becomes increasingly difficult as well. Eventually, checking the answer can itself become infeasible unless researchers make strong assumptions about how the quantum computer behaves internally.

The IBM and University of Chicago team approached this verification problem by developing a more structured alternative to RCS.

The researchers showed that their method preserves the same computational hardness criteria associated with RCS, meaning the problem remains extremely difficult for classical computers. At the same time, the added structure allows errors to be detected during the quantum computation.

"Verification remains one of the biggest challenges in firmly establishing experimental quantum advantage," said Bill Fefferman, Associate Professor at the University of Chicago. "This experiment develops techniques to better characterize the fidelity of hard quantum states under noise, increasing confidence that the quantum computer is solving a computationally hard problem."

Soumik Ghosh, PhD student in Fefferman's group at the University of Chicago, added, "Beyond strengthening experimental validation, advances in verification have the potential to unlock practical applications for the next generation of quantum computers."

The experiment also included one of the world's largest-known demonstrations of quantum error correction.

The researchers operated 70 logical qubits. Unlike individual physical qubits, logical qubits are encoded in a way that helps protect quantum information from errors and noise.

Using those logical qubits, the team carried out 2,415 logical two-qubit operations and 468 logical "T gates," two measures that reflect the complexity of the quantum circuit.

The encoded design significantly improved reliability. Effective logical error rates were 10 times lower than the underlying physical error rates, allowing the circuit to maintain unusually high fidelity even while performing a large number of quantum operations.

IBM Says Quantum Advantage Has Entered a New Stage

"We are now firmly in the quantum advantage era," said Jay Gambetta, Director of IBM Research and IBM Fellow. "We have demonstrated a quantum computation beyond the practical reach of classical computers that establishes, with statistical confidence, a lower bound on how faithfully it was executed. This milestone gives scientists, developers, and businesses a new foundation for trusting quantum computers as they scale to problems far beyond what we can achieve classically."

The researchers found that many leading classical simulation methods would face prohibitive runtimes when attempting the same task.

The IBM quantum computer, by comparison, completed the computation in approximately 15 minutes.

A Step Toward Larger, More Trustworthy Quantum Computers

Speed alone is not enough for quantum computing to become useful at larger scales. Researchers also need ways to suppress errors and establish confidence that a quantum system produced a valid result.

This experiment advances both goals at once by combining large-scale logical quantum computing with a method for evaluating the reliability of a calculation that is already beyond practical classical simulation.

Error correction and trustworthy verification are considered essential for scaling quantum computers toward more difficult problems, making the new demonstration an important step toward that goal.

#10727. Scientists discover Earth has a new moon that’s smaller than most cars

Scientists have discovered that Earth has temporarily gained a second moon, although the tiny asteroid is expected to leave our planet’s orbit within the next few months.

The space rock, officially identified as 2026 QQ8, measures just 3 to 6 feet across, making it smaller than most cars.

Despite its size, astronomers have classified the object as a temporary mini-moon due to its gravitational relationship with Earth.

The asteroid reportedly entered Earth’s gravitational influence in April 2026 but wasn’t discovered until late August, when Hungarian astronomer Kriztián Sárneczky identified it. Its stay is expected to end in late 2026 or early 2027.

According to reports from The Independent and AccuWeather, asteroid 2026 QQ8 is expected to complete its temporary journey around Earth before returning to interplanetary space.

The object typically approaches Earth once every decade or so, but its latest encounter brought it close enough to become temporarily associated with our planet.

In August, the asteroid made its closest reported approach to Earth, passing approximately 333,700 miles away. For comparison, Earth’s permanent Moon orbits at an average distance of roughly 239,000 miles.

Despite being classified as a mini-moon, 2026 QQ8 is far too small to be seen with the naked eye. Its limited size also makes it impossible to observe using typical amateur telescopes.

Astronomers have also indicated that the asteroid poses no danger to Earth. Even if its trajectory brought it into the planet’s atmosphere, the small space rock would likely burn up before reaching the surface.

While the discovery of another moon might sound unusual, temporary natural satellites are not unprecedented.

Scientists think they’ve discovered a new dwarf planet in our solar system

Mysterious “space object” finally identified after crash landing in farmer’s field

A new ‘moon’ has been trailing Earth for decades and no one noticed until now

Astronomers observed an early example of a temporary mini-moon in 1991, and similar objects have been identified in the decades since.

Some researchers believe Earth could have as many as six mini-moons at any given time, according to BBC’s Sky at Night Magazine, although their small size makes them difficult to detect.

Unlike Earth’s permanent Moon, these objects only remain near the planet temporarily before continuing their journeys through space.

The discovery comes after astronomers identified another unusual companion to Earth, known as 2025 PN7, which had been traveling alongside our planet for decades before researchers noticed it.

Unlike temporary mini-moons such as 2026 QQ8, 2025 PN7 is classified as a quasi-moon because it orbits the Sun rather than Earth. Scientists believe the object has been following a similar orbital path to our planet for roughly 60 years and could remain nearby for several more decades.

#10725. How to Build a Typed AI Pipeline With Jev and Clairic.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A support email lands: "Order NW-47790, the tent poles snapped on the first night, I paid $389 and I want every cent back today or I'm disputing the charge."

To act on that, your code needs answers to a handful of small questions. Is it a refund request? How urgent is it? Should a person handle it? What's the order number? How much money is involved? It's the same problem whether you're sorting a support inbox or building a custom chatbot that has to work out what each customer is asking for.

The usual move is to send the whole email to a chat model with a prompt that ends "respond in JSON", then validate whatever comes back. It works, but you're paying a model built to write essays to fill in five fields, and you're parsing its guess at a schema.

System One models are built for exactly this kind of job. In this tutorial we'll build a small support inbox pipeline with two of them: Jev from TypeSafe makes the decisions, and Clairic from Cloudflare reads the values. Plain TypeScript then turns their answers into an action. No prompt engineering for output formats, and no JSON repair.

The name comes from Daniel Kahneman's Thinking, Fast and Slow. System 1 is the fast, intuitive kind of thinking you use to read a face or a sign. System 2 is the slow, deliberate kind you use for long division.

A System One model is an AI model that understands natural language but doesn't generate text. You give it some input (the state) and a set of typed questions, and it gives back typed answers with probabilities. There's no free text to parse, because the model can only answer in the shape you asked for.

That's different from asking a chat model for structured output. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema shows how to constrain a chat model and validate its JSON with Zod. With a System One model, the type is the interface: a yes/no question comes back as a probability, a pick-one question comes back as one of your options, and a date comes back as a date.

Jev and Clairic are both System One models, but they do different jobs, and that's what makes them a good pair.

Jev can't hand you an order number, and Clairic has no way to pick from a list of options or answer yes or no. Give each one the job it's built for.

If you've read SitePoint's Jev and LangChain tutorial, you've seen Jev deciding which model handles an agent's request. Here we'll use it on the incoming text itself.

Five made-up emails for a made-up outdoor gear store go in. For each one:

Both models read the same email, so they run at the same time.

Put the three values in your environment:

No SDKs are needed. Both models are a single fetch call.

Here's the test inbox. Each email is there to test a different path through the rules:

Jev takes a state (the email) and a set of named questions. We'll ask three, one of each type:

All three questions go in one request. Jev reads the email once and answers every question in parallel, so adding a question costs very little:

We pin jev-1.14.0 rather than the jev-latest alias. Your thresholds get tuned against one version's probabilities, so you want to choose when that version changes.

For the angry tent email, Jev came back with:

(Trimmed: the real response also includes the probability of every option and level.)

Clairic takes the same state, plus a set of typed fields instead of questions:

reference_date anchors relative dates like "the 14th" or "last Monday". In production, pass the date the email arrived.

Each answer comes back with three things worth using:

For the "where is it?" email, Clairic read the deadline like this:

"The 14th" became a full date, worked out from the reference date. Text fields like order_number also come back as character spans of the original email. The value is copied from the input, not generated, so it can't invent an order number that isn't there.

The two models don't depend on each other, so send both requests together:

The time for each email is whichever model is slower, not the two added together.

This is the part a chat model usually ends up doing badly: the business rules. Here they're ordinary code you can read, test, and change without touching a prompt:

Notice that the model's confidence sits right next to the business rule. A $18.95 refund is only automatic if Clairic is sure about both the order number and the amount. Raising the refund limit or the confidence bar is a one-line change, and you don't need to call either model again to see what it would do. Save the answers and replay them.

Every email went where it should. The whole run used 2,703 input tokens on Jev and 2,292 on Clairic, about US$0.0003 in total. At that rate, 10,000 support emails would cost roughly US$0.70.

The interesting part of the run wasn't the right answers. It was the two wrong ones.

"From last Monday." The run used 2 November 2026 as the reference date, which is a Monday. "Last Monday" means 26 October, but Clairic answered 2026-11-02, the reference date itself. Its confidence on that answer was 0.54, compared with 0.99 or higher on almost every other value in the run.

"A couple of weeks ago." Clairic turned this into 2026-10-19, which is a fair reading but still a guess. Its confidence was 0.43.

Neither date changed any action, because the rules only trust values at 0.9 or above. That's the habit to build: a value under your bar counts as unknown. A chat model returning JSON would have handed you "2026-11-02" with the same certainty as everything else.

One more catch: Clairic copied the order number as #NW-48213, hash included, because that's how the customer wrote it. Text values are spans of the input, so normalising them is your code's job. That's one line of code.

System One models handle the reading and the routing. They don't hold a conversation. Once an email is routed, something still has to write the reply, ask a follow-up question, or explain a returns policy in the customer's own words. That's where a chat model earns its cost.

The split that works is: System One models decide, code applies the rules, and a chat model only gets the cases where words matter.

We built a support inbox triage pipeline from two small models and some ordinary code:

Each email took about half a second and cost less than a hundredth of a cent. Most importantly, the two answers that would have been wrong came back flagged as unsure.

The full code is about 160 lines with no dependencies. Try adding a fourth Jev question (does the customer mention a photo?) or an estimate field in Clairic (how many days until the trip?), and see how little the rest of the pipeline has to change.
