#10676. This new qubit could be 100 times less error-prone in superfluid quantum computer breakthrough

Researchers at the University of Surrey have proposed a new kind of qubit that could help address one of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing: keeping error rates low as systems become larger.

The concept uses superfluid helium, a form of helium with unusual quantum properties, to create a qubit that may be less sensitive to some of the disturbances that affect today's leading quantum computers.

Why Quantum Computers Struggle With Errors

Quantum computers rely on qubits to store and process information in ways that conventional computers cannot. Many current systems use superconducting circuits, but these are highly sensitive to electromagnetic noise and stray electrical charges - similar to the static electricity that can make hair cling to a balloon.

Even very small disturbances can disrupt the fragile quantum information stored in a qubit. As more qubits are added, controlling those errors becomes increasingly difficult, making scalability one of the central challenges in quantum computing.

In a study published in npj Quantum Information, researchers from Surrey's Quantum Sciences Group describe a different approach built around superfluid helium-3 - an unusual form of liquid helium that can flow without friction.

Their proposed device, called the Superfluid Helium Oscillator Quantum (SHOQ) device, would use charge-neutral superfluid helium. Because the helium carries no electric charge, the design could be naturally protected from certain types of electromagnetic noise.

A Qubit With Much Lower Predicted Error Rates

According to the researchers, this is the first reported design for a qubit based on a superfluid. Their calculations suggest the SHOQ device could have error rates about 100 times lower than conventional superconducting qubits.

Dr. Priya Sharma, Daphne Jackson Fellow in Hybrid Quantum Systems at the University of Surrey's School of Mathematics and Physics, and lead author of the study, said:

"We are not the first to think about the individual components behind this idea, but what we have done for the first time is bring them together in a microfluidic device and work out the specific details that could enable the device to function as a qubit.

"The maths tells us that it should work. We have taken what we already know about superfluid helium and quantum technologies and turned that into an educated design, with the parameters and specifications needed to build one. The next step is to make a prototype and put those predictions to the test."

Rather than replacing existing quantum computing hardware, the researchers say the SHOQ device could potentially be integrated with superconducting quantum technology.

That could allow different types of qubits to perform different jobs within the same system, depending on their strengths.

One longer-term possibility is to use the SHOQ device as a form of quantum memory. In that setup, a superfluid based qubit could store quantum information while other hardware carries out calculations.

Dr. Eran Ginossar, Associate Professor at the University of Surrey's Department of Physics and Advanced Technology Institute, and co-author of the study, said:

"We don't necessarily need one type of qubit to do everything. Combining different quantum technologies could allow us to take advantage of the strengths of each.

"Superfluid helium gives us a fundamentally different type of quantum hardware to explore. If the predicted performance can be demonstrated experimentally, it could eventually work alongside existing superconducting technologies as part of a larger quantum system."

The team now plans to build a prototype to find out whether the predicted performance can be reproduced in a real device. That effort is being supported by an IAA Commercialisation Fellowship awarded to Dr. Priya Sharma.

The SHOQ device would need to operate at extremely low temperatures, but researchers have already reached the necessary conditions in previous experiments involving superfluid helium-3.

The project was led by the University of Surrey in collaboration with Professor Jens Koch at Northwestern University in the United States. Koch was one of the researchers involved in developing the transmon - a superconducting qubit design that is now widely used in quantum computing.

Materials provided by University of Surrey. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10671. Scientists recreate a bizarre quantum effect that stops a particle cold

A particle does not have to pass through a magnetic field to be influenced by it. That surprising feature of quantum physics has now been recreated by researchers at the University of Oxford, who used a hybrid quantum computer to demonstrate how an invisible magnetic influence can bring a simulated particle's movement to a complete stop.

The experiment reproduced a phenomenon known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect, in which a charged particle traveling around a region containing magnetic flux experiences a measurable change in its quantum state, even though its path never takes it through the magnetic field itself.

The Oxford team achieved this using a quantum system that combines qubits, the basic units of quantum information, with quantum oscillators, which represent vibrating motion. Their findings, published in Nature Physics, demonstrate how this combination could help scientists investigate fundamental interactions between matter and fields that are increasingly difficult to simulate using conventional computers.

A Strange Quantum Effect Predicted in 1959

In everyday physics, a charged particle responds to the electric and magnetic fields it encounters. Quantum mechanics, however, reveals that particles can behave in ways that have no straightforward counterpart in our ordinary experience.

In 1959, physicists Yakir Aharonov and David Bohm predicted that a charged particle could be affected by magnetic flux even if it traveled entirely outside the region containing the magnetic field. Rather than experiencing a conventional magnetic force along its path, the particle would undergo a change in its quantum phase.

Quantum phase describes an aspect of a particle's wave behavior that influences how different possible paths interfere with one another. Although the phase itself is not directly visible, differences in phase can produce measurable effects.

The prediction became known as the Aharonov-Bohm effect and was eventually verified in experiments involving real electrons. It remains one of the clearest demonstrations that the quantum world operates according to rules that go beyond the familiar laws of classical physics.

Researchers are now exploring how this phenomenon behaves in more complex theoretical systems known as lattice gauge theories.

These theories describe how matter interacts with fields associated with fundamental forces. They represent space as a grid, with matter located at individual points and fields associated with the connections between them. Such models are particularly important in particle physics and high-energy physics, where scientists study the basic particles and interactions that make up the universe.

The challenge is that the mathematics becomes extraordinarily demanding as these systems grow more complicated. Even powerful conventional computers can struggle to calculate how all the interacting components evolve.

Quantum computers offer a different approach. Instead of attempting to calculate every aspect of a quantum system using classical computing methods, scientists can construct another quantum system that follows the same underlying rules. They can then observe its behavior directly, an approach known as quantum simulation.

How Scientists Built the Quantum Experiment

The Oxford project began in 2022, when lead author Dr. Sebastian Saner, Dr. Oana Bazavan, and their colleagues started developing a way to simulate lattice gauge theories using a hybrid quantum system. They collaborated with Dr. Alejandro Bermudez of the Instituto de Física Teórica in Madrid.

Their experimental design relied on two different quantum components that performed separate functions.

The first consisted of qubits encoded in the internal states of trapped ions, which are electrically charged atoms held in place using electromagnetic fields. These qubits represented the gauge fields in the theoretical model.

The second component used quantum oscillators, created by the vibrations of those same trapped ions. These oscillators represented matter.

By combining the two, the researchers constructed a simple loop that served as a basic building block of a lattice gauge theory. Two oscillators represented matter at two different locations, while two qubits represented the fields connecting those locations.

Although the arrangement was small, it was sufficient to reproduce an important interaction between matter and magnetic flux.

Quantum Entanglement Creates a Surprising Solution

The researchers next placed the two qubits into an entangled quantum state. Entanglement is a phenomenon in which quantum systems become linked in ways that cannot be fully explained by classical physics.

Within the team's simulated lattice gauge theory, this entangled state represented magnetic flux passing through the center of the loop.

Initially, the researchers had planned to introduce the magnetic flux as a fixed background condition. However, creating the necessary interaction would have required capabilities that their quantum hardware did not possess.

Encoding the flux directly into the qubits provided a practical alternative. More importantly, it allowed the magnetic flux to become part of the evolving quantum system rather than remaining an unchanging external influence.

"For us, the exciting step was to encode the magnetic flux in a gauge field that was itself dynamical," says Dr. Saner. "Rather than having matter evolve in a fixed background, the matter and gauge field become part of the same quantum dynamics."

What began as a technical workaround ultimately enabled the researchers to explore a more interesting version of the phenomenon, one in which matter and the field could participate together in the quantum dynamics.

A Quantum Effect That Stops Particle Motion

With their simulated loop in place, the scientists examined how a matter particle moved between locations through a process known as quantum tunneling.

Quantum tunneling allows particles to move between states in ways that would not be possible under ordinary classical rules.

When the researchers ran their simulation without magnetic flux, the particle could tunnel freely around the loop.

Introducing the simulated flux produced a strikingly different result.

The particle had two possible paths around the loop, but the magnetic flux changed their relative quantum phases. As a result, the two paths interfered destructively, meaning their quantum contributions canceled each other out.

This interference completely suppressed the tunneling, leaving the simulated particle frozen in its original state.

The outcome demonstrated the Aharonov-Bohm effect within a lattice gauge theory containing a dynamical field. Rather than simply reproducing the familiar behavior of a particle moving around a fixed magnetic influence, the experiment showed how the phenomenon can emerge when matter and the field are both represented by interacting quantum components.

A New Tool for Exploring Fundamental Physics

The Oxford findings were published alongside a related study by researchers at the University of Maryland, led by Professor Norbert Linke.

The Maryland team also used a hybrid quantum system, but their experiment focused on simulating the Yukawa potential, a mathematical description of an interaction relevant to nuclear and particle physics.

Although the two research groups developed their methods independently, they later coordinated the submission of their studies to Nature Physics.

Together, the experiments demonstrate how hybrid quantum computers could become valuable tools for investigating fundamental interactions that are difficult to reproduce using classical simulations.

By combining different types of quantum components, researchers can build systems in which matter and fields are represented in ways that more closely reflect the physics they want to study.

The Oxford experiment provides an example of that potential. A phenomenon first predicted in 1959 and later observed with electrons has now been recreated in a quantum simulator where the field itself participates in the dynamics, offering scientists another way to explore the unusual rules governing the quantum world.

Materials provided by University of Oxford. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10667. Physicists discover “astonishing” property of light that defies expectations

Scientists have discovered a surprising ability of light that allows it to develop spinning patterns as it moves through empty space, without relying on special lenses, mirrors, or materials. The finding reveals that light possesses a hidden geometric structure that can influence its behavior in ways researchers previously thought were difficult to achieve.

The discovery comes from an international team led by researchers at the University of East Anglia (UEA) in the United Kingdom, working with scientists in South Africa. Their experiments demonstrate that carefully prepared light can be controlled through its own internal geometry, offering a new approach to manipulating how light spins and twists.

The findings challenge long-standing assumptions about how different properties of light interact. They could eventually lead to more sensitive medical tests, faster and more secure communications, and advanced quantum technologies that use light to transmit and protect information.

Why the Direction of Light's Spin Matters

One of the most intriguing aspects of the discovery involves chirality, a scientific term describing the difference between left-handed and right-handed structures.

Human hands provide a familiar example. Although they are mirror images of each other, a left hand cannot be perfectly superimposed on a right hand. Many molecules have a similar property, including compounds used in medicines. Two versions of the same molecule can have nearly identical chemical structures yet produce very different effects in the body.

Scientists can distinguish these molecular forms by studying how they interact with light that has a particular rotational behavior. Traditionally, producing and controlling such light has often involved specialized materials, carefully designed surfaces, or powerful lenses that focus beams extremely tightly.

The research demonstrates that appropriately prepared light can develop this behavior naturally as it travels, without requiring those additional optical components.

"Our work shows that light can naturally develop this handed behavior all on its own," said Dr. Kayn Forbes from UEA's School of Chemistry, Pharmacy and Pharmacology.

"You just have to prepare it in the right way.

"Most people think of light as traveling in straight lines. But scientists can also create structured light -- light whose brightness, shape, and direction are carefully arranged.

"One extreme example is light that twists as it travels, forming a corkscrew shape known as an optical vortex. Each twist can carry information, making this kind of light valuable for high-speed internet, secure communications, and advanced sensors.

"Light can also spin as it travels, depending on how it is polarized. This spin can be left-handed or right-handed -- another form of chirality."

Scientists Discover How Light Develops a Hidden Spin

Light can exhibit different kinds of rotational behavior. An optical vortex, for example, has a wave pattern that twists around its direction of travel, much like a corkscrew. Light also has a property known as polarization, which describes how its electric field oscillates. Certain polarization patterns produce a form of rotation called spin.

Physicists have long studied the relationship between these two properties. However, the interactions responsible for the newly observed effect were generally considered extremely weak and difficult to detect without specialized experimental conditions.

The researchers found that the situation changes when a beam of light is prepared in a particular balanced configuration.

Under those conditions, the beam can initially have no spin, yet distinct regions with spinning behavior emerge as it travels through free space. The effect does not require the light to pass through engineered materials or additional optical devices.

"It starts off with no spin at all," explained MSc student Light Mkhumbuza, who carried out key experiments. "But as the beam travels forward, spinning regions appear and separate out -- almost as if the spin was hiding and then revealed itself."

Rather than creating spin through an external optical component, the researchers demonstrated how the beam's existing internal structure can determine where spinning regions develop during propagation.

To explain this unexpected behavior, the researchers turned to topology, a mathematical field concerned with properties that remain unchanged when objects are continuously stretched, bent, or reshaped.

Dr. Isaac Nape, from the University of the Witwatersrand in Johannesburg, South Africa, offered a simple way to understand the concept.

"To explain it, imagine a mug and a doughnut," he said. "You can morph one into the other without tearing it, because they both have one hole. That hole is a topological feature."

The researchers found that structured light possesses its own topological characteristics, encoded in the arrangement of its polarization.

This underlying pattern acts like a mathematical fingerprint. Even as the light beam changes shape while traveling, its topological structure remains, influencing how different regions of the beam evolve.

As a result, the arrangement of the light itself can determine where its left-handed and right-handed spinning patterns become visible.

The discovery gives scientists a way to guide this behavior by designing the beam's initial structure, rather than depending on specialized materials to produce the desired effect.

"This gives us a completely new tuning knob for light. By adjusting its topology, we can decide how and where chirality appears," said Dr. Nape.

Potential Breakthroughs in Medicine and Communications

The ability to control light through its geometry could eventually make several important optical technologies simpler, more affordable, and more versatile.

One particularly promising application involves medical diagnostics and pharmaceutical research. Because many biologically important molecules have left-handed and right-handed forms, carefully structured light could help scientists identify these differences with greater sensitivity. Such tools could be valuable for evaluating drug safety and detecting biological markers associated with disease.

The same principles could support smaller optical sensors capable of identifying chemicals or biological substances without requiring large, sophisticated laboratory instruments.

Communications technology could also benefit. By encoding information into different twisting and spinning patterns, researchers may be able to increase the amount of data carried by individual light beams. This could contribute to faster optical communications, improved information security, and future quantum networks.

Other potential uses include manipulating microscopic particles, cells, and molecules with light, as well as developing more resilient quantum systems. Topological properties may help protect delicate quantum information against certain forms of noise and interference.

"The implications are wide-ranging," said Dr. Forbes. "This work could lead to simpler and more sensitive medical tests, especially in drug development.

"It could also be used to pack more information into laser beams -- boosting data capacity for communications, including future quantum networks.

"And because the effect doesn't rely on fragile materials or precision-engineered surfaces, it could be easier and cheaper to use in real-world technologies.

"This research could lay the foundations for a new generation of light-based technologies, by showing that light's behavior can be controlled using its own internal geometry," he added.

These applications remain possibilities for future development, but the underlying discovery provides researchers with another way to design and control the behavior of light.

A Hidden Property of Light Finally Revealed

For decades, scientists have relied on increasingly sophisticated optical equipment to produce and manipulate unusual forms of light. The findings suggest that some of these behaviors can instead arise from carefully designed structures already encoded within a light beam.

By showing how topology governs the emergence of spinning regions during propagation, the team has revealed a previously underappreciated connection between light's geometry and its physical behavior.

The discovery also illustrates how much remains to be learned about something as familiar and extensively studied as light.

"For something so familiar, light is proving to be far richer, stranger and more powerful than anyone imagined," said Dr. Forbes.

"And astonishingly, this new behavior has been there all along -- just waiting to be seen."

The research, 'Topological Control of Chirality and Spin with Structured Light', was published in the journal Light: Science & Applications.

Materials provided by University of East Anglia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10666. Mysterious 3,300-year-old sword found at bottom of lake in “excellent” condition

A 3,300-year-old sword was found at the bottom of a small lake in Italy, and it looks like it is ready to be used.

Over the years, plenty of pretty old items have washed up on shores around the world. However, some of the most unusual and sought-after discoveries are still hidden deep below the earth’s surface.

In Central Italy, experts have uncovered a sword from the Bronze Age that appears to be at least 3,300 years old. The sword had been submerged in Lake Mezzano, which can be found in the province of Viterbo and Southern Etruria, and was brought to shore in October.

“The artifact, preserved in excellent condition, still featuring a sharp edge, represents an important addition to our understanding of the communities that frequented the lake basin over three thousand years ago,” said Italy’s Superintendence of Archaeology, Fine Arts and Landscape for the Province of Viterbo and Southern Etruria.

They noted that it is the third sword found at similar sites since the 1970s, and that it is of “extraordinary interest” for experts.

“The new discovery further confirms the exceptional scientific significance of Lake Mezzano and the importance of this site for the study of the protohistory of central Italy,” the Superintendence added.

“The new evidence contributes to enriching our knowledge of the populations who inhabited Tuscia over three thousand years ago, opening up further research avenues into the economic, social, and cultural dynamics that characterized the region in prehistoric times, well before the emergence of the Etruscan civilization.”

They also noted that research “will continue in the coming years” as they want to uncover further items linked to “ritual and cult practices connected to water.”

This isn’t the first age-old sword that has been dug recently. In May, a six-year-old boy uncovered a 1,300-year-old sword from the Viking age during a field trip in Norway.

Six-year-old boy accidentally discovers 1,300-year-old Viking age sword on school field trip

Scientist accidentally discovers ancient giant tooth while studying endangered frogs

Scientists discover ancient “dinosaur fish” may live for over 400 years

Elsewhere, archaeologists in Turkey believe they’d uncovered the real grave of a professional wrestler who lived between 475 and 300 BC. Ancient coins had been found buried in the grave too.

Also, in early October, scientists in Dublin, Ireland, uncovered “ancient DNA” in a 1,000-year-old book linked to “disease outbreaks in animals” from the past.

#10664. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10662. Deer rescued after spending 10 days with Halloween pumpkin stuck on its head

A deer in Michigan has finally been rescued after spending 10 days with a plastic Halloween pumpkin stuck on its head, following a search involving animal rescuers and local residents.

The young buck was first spotted in Livonia, near Detroit, with a bright orange pumpkin-shaped container covering its nose, preventing it from eating or drinking properly.

The unusual sight sparked concern among residents, who began sharing it on social media to help rescuers track down the animal.

The Detroit Animal Welfare Group (DAWG) spent more than a week searching for the deer before finally locating it on Sunday, November 8.

The rescue operation took place in Hines Park in Wayne County, Michigan, where volunteers pursued the deer through dense woods, thick brush, and muddy terrain.

According to the rescue group, the team was forced to cross a 20-foot-wide river four separate times while attempting to get close enough to safely tranquilize the animal.

The water was “up to our thighs” and “freezing cold,” making the search particularly challenging.

As darkness approached, the rescuers were preparing to abandon their efforts for the evening when another sighting gave them one final opportunity.

“We were getting ready to leave because dark was setting in and darting in the dark is not ideal; there was a sighting,” the group explained.

The team managed to safely tranquilize the deer and remove the plastic pumpkin from its head, bringing the 10-day ordeal to an end.

Bear finally freed by rescuers after getting head stuck in giant stove pipe

15-year-old stranded alone at sea survives for 62 hours while holding onto bodies of his family

The deer was suffering from mild dehydration after struggling to eat and drink, but received treatment and quickly recovered.

Once the tranquilizing medication was reversed, the animal returned to its feet and was released back into the wild.

According to the rescue group, the buck stopped to groom himself for several minutes before walking away, appearing to glance back at the volunteers who had freed him.

The Detroit Animal Welfare Group have since posted about another deer with its head stuck in a pumpkin container, which has apparently been removed by a local vet.

Commenters appealed for people to be more careful with the plastic decorations, suggesting they snip the handles off to lessen the chance of animals getting caught in them.

#10660. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (2026) review

I have a friend who first played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time back in the day, when the game was only just released and he was home from university for Christmas. This is objectively the best of all times to play any video game: not Christmas specifically, but dropped out of the regular world and faced with a few weeks filled with nothing pressing to do whatsoever. Years later, though, my friend told me that the magic of the game didn't just come down to the playing of it, but the way that the playing of it unfolded.

Here's what he meant. He'd play all day, curtains drawn, family ignored, working through dungeons and pelting across Hyrule Field on Epona. Then in the evening he'd call his girlfriend, who was back with her own family for Christmas. She always wanted to know what he'd got up to that day. And not in East Sussex; she wanted to know what he'd got up to in Hyrule.

So as he played he took regular breaks to tell someone else about what he had encountered, and what he had discovered, and what he had yet to overcome. It is my bright delight today to be able to tell you that, around thirty years later, having just revisited the game in Nintendo's new remake, Ocarina of Time is still worth telling people about. It's still an adventure so surprising, so satisfying, so moving that I need to talk about it.

Here's the thing, though. Despite the game being a cast-iron masterpiece, this reaction was something of a surprise to me. Or rather, it was a surprise precisely because the game was a cast-iron masterpiece. In truth, a couple of weeks ago I wasn't really aching to head back to Ocarina of Time after all these years. I kind of dreaded having to slog through an echoing, once-great game and summon a thousand words of measured reverence afterwards.

Thankfully I had forgotten two things. The first is that I am an idiot, which means that I had failed to realise that any chance to replay a Zelda should be leapt at. And secondly, it turns out that I'd forgotten exactly what kind of masterpiece Ocarina of Time is. And along with that last point - and we'll get to this properly in a bit - I hadn't realised that there's a secret ingredient to this specific remake that has proved absolutely essential to its greatness, and that has nothing at all to do with the code running on the Switch 2.

Let's deal with remake duties here. This is more or less the game that people played back in 1998. The beats of the adventure, with Link travelling between different eras of Hyrule in order to defeat Ganon, remain the same. You still start in Kokiri Forest and you still ride Epona over the fence at Lon Lon Ranch in one of the great heart-racing, skin-prickling moments in all of art. If you're stuck in a dungeon, you can almost always use an existing guide from the N64 game to work your way through it. The game world is still broken up into little chunks that fade out and in as you move between them. And each game still begins with that unskippably evocative intro that has Link and Epona dashing over a misty hill with the moon behind them, Zelda meets Susan Cooper.

Changes though? For one thing the whole game's been updated with modern graphics, and has ditched any and all fixed cameras for a modern system of movement on one thumbstick and camera on the other. One example: Hyrule Castle Town, in my memory at least, always had a central camera and you would pivot about its streets as if you were a horse on a circus merry-go-round.

Now it's pure Frozen territory, cobbled paths racing off in every direction and you can explore them with the camera rushing behind you. The game's spaces feel less like elaborate machines that you were a mechanical part of, and more like video game spaces. Yes, from the way I've phrased that I now see that something of a trade-off has taken place here. I loved that sense you got in early 3D games that the whole thing was a clever practical trick that was forever almost about to collapse in on itself, but anyway! The new Ocarina is a game you can show kids who weren't alive in 1998 and they will feel at home more quickly.

Sometimes the graphical update is really wonderful. The route to Zora's Domain, which I barely remember in the original Ocarina, is now a lovely scramble over cliffs and arcing crags. A bold smattering of 1990s Nickelodeon colours and glossy textures, meanwhile, makes Jabu Jabu's Belly a cross between a pre-millennium rave and a modern soft play.

My favourite locations of all, however, are two of the mid-game dungeons. The Water Temple is now a drowned Art Nouveau palace that you could easily imagine Captain Nemo knocking around in. And the Forest Temple, which is my absolute favourite Zelda dungeon of all time, is a parquet-floored murder mansion that's been overtaken by trees. Dark wood-panelled corridors and weird bits of church sticking out of the foliage. It's the kind of place you'd hear a weird crime podcast about.

Trade-offs, however. My wife, a huge fan of the original game, would wander in and out as I played, and often felt the game she remembered was a little more characterful in its visuals. The moment you fight Dark Link, for example, has lost a little of its glitchy liminality, although the staging, with an old tree in the middle of a vast smoky room, is still wonderfully strange. Towards the end of the game, with the trip into Gerudo territory, you could also occasionally be forgiven for wondering if you'd stepped into one of a dozen third-person Unreal games in development at the moment. It's pleasant to look at, but it sometimes doesn't look specifically like Zelda anymore.

All that, and some strange character models that inevitably I learned to love, don't really matter very much, however. Not least because a handful of clever tweaks elsewhere mean that Ocarina now races along at an astonishing clip. And all this with a careful paring back of intrusions from Navi the fairy and the owl, who were both there in the original game to help move you along swiftly in the first place.

These tweaks are all pretty great. Link now has a jump button, which removes the original game's contextual jumps from the equation, and makes the thundering jump attack even more pleasing to pull off. Even better, in a loan from the 3DS remake of Ocarina, you can swap out usable items using a trigger mini-menu, and you can change boots and whatever you had on your head with some of the directional face buttons.

This doesn't sound like much, but it's quietly transformative in the way that the best updates are. Take the Water Temple. This is the stuff of memes in terms of difficulty and the patience required in navigating an environment that can be flooded up to three different levels. But now, as on the 3DS, you can enjoy it without having to slip into the menu every few steps to add your iron boots or remove them.

And this in turn means that a dungeon I had been really dreading turned out to be a dungeon that revealed itself via experimentation. I would change the water level and see what options I now had, think about the relationship between spaces I could reach and spaces I could not, all without needing to muddle about in menus and interrupt the flow of things. Flow is the word in fact: a simple UI and item-management tweak has given a legendary Zelda dungeon its perfect pace and sense of revelation.

Alongside tweaks like that which I noticed - oh, it's easier to equip the Hookshot - are no doubt hundreds of little things I didn't notice. I suspect there's a lot more gentle direction prompting built into places like the Water Temple, while elsewhere, in the Forest Temple, for example, I suspected that some of the backtracking had been removed. Individual item placements have been changed around perhaps, and there are generous tweaks like a wallet that holds more rupees from the off. For me at least, all of these tweaks had several things in common: they improved the game without removing or altering anything that was particularly meaningful about the original, and, also, I struggled to confidently spot them.

I did try to spot them for a while. And I measured up what I was playing against my memory, which is always wonky in the first place. Hyrule Field feels smaller now, but it's still thrilling to race across. I remember Epona entering the game sooner, or maybe I just remember the idea of a horse in a Zelda game being a huge, attention-holding deal. I thought a favourite boss involving oil paintings came much later, but again, I had just shifted it to the third act because of its original impact on me.

But this gives way over time to the most important thing about Ocarina of Time, I think, and the reason why this remake has such an emotional impact on people like me. This isn't just another Zelda with an ingenious overworld. It's a Zelda game that's even more preoccupied with time than ever before. And this is because time unfolds within the game, via a central mechanic that takes Hyrule seven years into the future, transforming Link from a child to an adult - and then allows you to go back again.

To play a game like this, a game that is about this stuff, after a period of almost thirty years away, gives your imagination and memory a kind of IMAX treatment. I realised that not only is this a game about the passage of time and the way that the world looks different when you're young to when you're a bit older - I had never really realised that adult Link goes through variations of the same dungeons that young Link went through, but now they're all just a bit harder and more consequential - it's also the kind of game that a lot of players have knowingly or unknowingly measured parts of their lives by.

This time, when I reached the first Fairy Fountain, for example, I remembered that my wife had walked down the aisle to this music - about half the registry office recognised the tune with a shiver of laughter, incidentally. Then there's the Water Temple, which has become cultural shorthand for a challenge that is really not screwing around. How many awful things had I faced in life by first conjuring the spirit of Link about to enter this place.

Players can change camera rotation speed and toggle motion-control aiming. Targeting can be controlled by holding a button or toggling a button, Ocarina can be controlled by the mic or by buttons, controller vibration can be switched on or off.

There's that first arrival in Hyrule Field, which is both a promise of everything that games were about to become in 1998 and that fleeting moment in childhood where the boundaries suddenly change. This game doesn't just send me back into my memories, then. It puts me back in conversation with all the people I have known who have ever loved it. I play it now and think I'm talking to my friend who played it at university, or my wife who loved it long before we met.

Was it always this good? Were the dungeons always so complex and challenging, but also so playful, so coherent? Were the bosses always driven by interesting ideas and drama and humour rather than difficulty? Was the world always so rich with minor characters who linger in the mind - the accordion player in the windmill, the gravedigger who has his own tour he'd really like you to try out? It's impossible for me to say at this point. This is Ocarina as it's always been, as timeless and intricate, but it also has a vibrancy and clarity that makes it feel like it was just created last Wednesday. If you're new to Zelda and you're coming to this game fresh, you're going to love it. Someone, somewhere, is about to play a game so special for the first time that they need to call up their friend and tell them all about it. But if you're returning to this game? Well, somehow you'll find that you're still coming to it fresh.

My dad always said that you should read Don Quixote three times in your life, and I only recently realised that he meant that the book changes because you change. So. When I got to the Deku Tree back at the start of Ocarina a few weeks ago, and I completed the dungeon and watched the impossible vision of the Deku Tree dying of weariness? I remembered that the first time I'd been here, almost thirty years ago, I couldn't help but identify with Link, freshly gifted a sword and shield, one dungeon down, ready for whatever came next. This time, I think, I was closer to identifying with the Deku Tree.

And here's the important part: this wasn't a depressing thought. It just meant that I had taken the long route to reach this point, and this astonishing game had taken the long route too. And yet we arrived at this point together, and together we were transformed. And that's really why any of this video game stuff matters.

A copy of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time was provided for this review by Nintendo.

#10659. AI can act exactly like a human, but we still don't think it's conscious

Even when artificial intelligence behaves exactly like a human, people are reluctant to consider it conscious, according to a new study from Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich (LMU).

As artificial intelligence becomes more advanced, reports of AI systems misleading users, working together, or appearing to pursue independent goals are becoming increasingly common. These behaviors often lead people to describe AI as though it can think, feel, or make decisions for itself. But does behaving like a human actually convince people that a machine has consciousness?

New research from LMU suggests that people make a clear distinction between displaying intelligent behavior and possessing a conscious mind, even when humans and AI act in precisely the same way.

How Scientists Tested Perceptions of AI Consciousness

Published in the journal Cognition, the study was led by Dr. Louis Longin of LMU's Chair of Philosophy of Mind, working alongside Dr. Bahador Bahrami, Professor Ophelia Deroy, and other researchers.

"Whereas previous studies have typically asked general questions about whether AI actually has mental states, our study is the first to directly compare how people attribute the same mental states to AI and humans behaving in exactly the same way, under identical circumstances," says Longin.

"We may be using mental terms to refer to AI, but this may only be because we lack better words," says Ophelia Deroy, philosopher and a senior author of the study. "After all, AI is trained to act and speak like a human, so these descriptions seem natural."

To investigate how people judge consciousness, the researchers conducted experiments involving nearly 1,100 participants. One group read brief descriptions of artificial agents that showed different levels of responsiveness to their environments. For example, some agents reacted to sounds or recognized the emotional states of others.

Another group encountered the same situations, except the characters were described as humans rather than AI systems. Participants then evaluated how conscious or aware each character seemed to be of its surroundings.

A Surprising Gap Between Human and AI Consciousness

The experiments revealed that responsiveness influenced how participants judged both humans and artificial intelligence. Characters that reacted more actively to their surroundings were generally considered more conscious and more aware.

However, a striking difference emerged when participants evaluated consciousness specifically. Even the most responsive AI was rated as less conscious than the least responsive human. In other words, identical behavior did not lead people to assign the same level of consciousness to humans and machines.

The results changed considerably when participants were asked about awareness instead. For this characteristic, judgments of humans and AI followed largely similar patterns, suggesting that people interpret awareness and consciousness in fundamentally different ways.

"There is a growing worry in public discussion that people will see AI behaving in a human-like way and start treating these systems as if they had human-like mental states," says Longin, lead author of the study. "We found something much more nuanced: People are quite willing to say that an AI notices things and is aware of its surroundings. But they clearly draw a line when it comes to the term 'consciousness'. People consistently attribute less consciousness to AI than humans -- even when their behavior is identical."

Although people sometimes describe AI as conscious when discussing its actions, the findings indicate that they still distinguish between artificial systems and human minds.

The researchers believe the difference between consciousness and awareness offers an important insight into how people understand artificial intelligence.

Awareness is a relatively neutral term that can describe an agent's ability to detect and respond to information in its environment. Consciousness, however, carries deeper implications about subjective experience and what it means to have an inner mental life.

Because awareness focuses more directly on observable behavior, people appear comfortable applying it to both humans and AI. Consciousness carries a different meaning, leading people to judge the two much less similarly.

The findings also highlight why the language used to discuss artificial intelligence matters, particularly as AI systems become increasingly capable of producing convincing human behavior.

"Our study teaches us lessons for how we communicate about AI," says Bahador Bahrami, who is also a senior author of the study and an expert in social neuroscience. "Companies and journalists often reach for hyped descriptions of AI models, and credit them with intentions, plans, or even moral conscience and hesitation. What our study shows is that everyday judgments are much more discriminating: People do not simply put AI and humans on the same mental scale. But the boundary also depends on which terms we use. For more neutral descriptors such as 'awareness', the distinction can largely disappear. That makes our choice of language important."

#10657. Eight years ago the idea of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel had me captivated, but can Bethesda recapture the magic?

Eight years ago, I sat at my desk enthralled. Bethesda had just released the first trailer for The Elder Scrolls 6, a then mysteriously nameless, locationless sequel to one of the biggest games of all time, and I reckoned I'd just found the secrets to both. It was going to be set in the region of Hammerfell, I reckoned. And the city of Sentinel was a crucial factor in reasoning why.

Admittedly, I'm maybe a little more horrified at my level of Elder Scrolls obsession than I am chuffed at any apparent clairvoyance. Ultimately it was a bit of a punt, launched off the back of a day's worth of fanatical hyperfocus and exactly the right amount of the old Gamer Network office's weapons-grade filter coffee. "There's not really a lot to go on here", I wrote, sagely, "but, if we had to, we'd plant our flag in Hammerfell as the location of The Elder Scrolls 6."

Indulge me a bit and let's retread the reasoning. First, three assumptions were required in order to make any kind of guess at all: The Elder Scrolls 6 would not be set in all of Tamriel; not set outside of Tamriel; and would be set somewhere vaguely close in the timeline to the events of Morrowind, Oblivion and Skyrim - so as to make that bit of scrabbly rock and ruined castle, visible in that now infamous first teaser, at least somewhat traceable with contemporary descriptions in the lore.

Then came a lot of narrowing down. Not Morrowind, Cyrodiil, Skyrim or the Summerset Isles, I decided, as we'd been to all of those too recently (I'll leave it up to you whether another eight years since Morrowind lets that one still count as remotely "recent", but still). And then not Valenwood, Elsweyr, or Black Marsh, thanks to the topography of the land in the trailer and the type of environment on show. That left High Rock and Hammerfell, the former of which we ruled out because of its relative size - on the small side for a presumably beefy new TES game - and more importantly, the architecture that seemed at odds with the sandy stone just barely visible in the structures of that first teaser.

And finally: Sentinel. The city was perfectly positioned - along a coast with the sea to the right, dry mountains in the foreground, warm stone ruins to the left, no visible land beyond. Now, that setting is all but confirmed - "all but" as, technically, nothing's set in stone yet, pardon the pun. All we've since been shown is a sand dune, an Elder Scroll, the name Sentinel, and a brief quote from a Moth Priest to a Hammerfell High Queen. But we can pretty much lock it in.

To the Elder Scrolls obsessive who spent that day eight years ago scurrying down endless YouTube rabbit holes and lingering on the dustiest unofficial wiki pages, the thought of a game set there ought to be tantalising. Already, even with that paper thin new teaser, there's enough to start picking at. The new trailer's quote is dated just four years after the events of Skyrim, for instance, with both that territory and Cyrodiil located right next door, making for some lovely opportunities for crossovers and references to wider events. And the quote itself is curious. As our guides team have already speculated, the notion of "A future lost. A victory undone. Sands of time scattered by fate" gives off the faintest whiff of a time-forking Dragon Break. That being the timeline-branching, multiple-player-choice-enabling thing that happened at the end of The Elder Scrolls 2: Daggerfall, a game which took place - aha! - in the Iliac Bay between High Rock and Hammerfell. Surely, the same bay we're looking at in that original TES 6 trailer.

It keeps going! The "victory" mentioned in that quote could refer to the victory won by the people of Hammerfell against a Thalmor invasion, in a conflict that - ooh! - took place around the same time Skyrim's main story was happening, and just a few years before that quote is dated. Could we get branching timelines? A tying together of the overarching Thalmor plotline that's been simmering away until now? A load of varying endings, as Bethesda experimented with so openly in Starfield? I'm off again. Fire up the Unofficial Elder Scrolls Pages!

There's such magic here, far beyond the apparent mastery Bethesda has over theory-baiting teasers for suckers like me. These games, for instance, also have an uncanny knack for joyful stupidity. There are Oblivion NPC barks out there that landed right as YouTube's golden era hit liftoff, and in turn defined an entire generation's sense of humour. I was once in a Slack channel here called #reeblereeblereeble, which was just myself and two other writers punting Imperial Watch taunts at each other to lighten the mood every couple of weeks, or whenever someone seemed a bit stressed. When I leave a gathering of old school friends I still catch myself bidding them "farewell!" with Wood Elf gusto, and giggling at the thought of being immediately squished into mulch.

And none of that is to mention the design itself, which has at times been more or less unparalleled. The clockwork, systemic magic of Morrowind is extraordinary, the first hour alone a lesson - at least as important as good old over-cited Mario 1:1 - in instructional design. Within a walkable radius of the musty little settlement of Seyda Neen Bethesda gave the player every tool necessary for understanding - and subsequently playing with, then outright breaking - its entire world. With Oblivion it brought the role-playing open world to the masses. With Skyrim it expanded what our very concept of the masses means.

By all accounts I should, then, be absolutely drooling at the thought of The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel. But for some reason the excitement is muted. It's been like this since long before this second teaser, frankly, and for much of that time I've put it down to Bethesda itself.

Bethesda Game Studios has not been on a good run. Fallout 76 might've finally recovered from its nightmarish launch, but time hasn't been hugely kind to Fallout 4, which longer-running fans have come to generally settle on as decent, but something of a slight step back from the wilder, more playful tendencies of Fallout 3 and Obsidian's New Vegas. The same could be argued of Skyrim, a wonderful game in its own right that did, nevertheless, sand down some of The Elder Scrolls' signature RPG elements into something smoother, almost closer to a frictionless modern action game (a topic on which Elder Scrolls fans have some rather strong opinions, though that's a whole other debate). And then there's Starfield, a perfectly alright game that, by virtue of that, is sort of a disaster. Its mere adequacy has only served to harden the belief, amongst long-running fans, that Bethesda has lost its magic touch.

The natural question, then, is can Bethesda recapture the magic? Again for some time I've been worried the answer might be 'no'. I worried about the BioWare-ification of the studio: the failed (albeit very well recovered) live service punt, the wonky new IP, the consolidation beneath changing owners, the shifting goalposts, the creeping corporate-ification and apparently inevitable entropy of big, creative RPG studios over a long enough period of time. The Bethesda of today is not the Bethesda of Morrowind, Oblivion or Skyrim. It's probably not even the Bethesda of Starfield. And worryingly, it's shown for some time now that it seems to care more for broad appeal - that mirage of financial success - over having caché with its long-running fans. That kind of blinkered thinking always comes back to bite.

But that's not it. I worried about that, but then I learned to stop worrying about that. The reality is Bethesda can absolutely recover the magic, just as any studio can - including BioWare for that matter, had it been given a clear, EA nonsense-free chance - for the obvious reason that no two development cycles need ever be the same. Development studios are not individuals, they're not sportspeople who go in or out of form, or see the talent fade as their legs start to give out. They're massive organisations full of all kinds of people. And so just as people leave, other people can join. There are always systemic issues at work, but you can overcome those if you can hire well, design well, plan well - if your owners are smart enough to let you, if you're given enough runway, and your leaders know what they're doing. Good game development, like just about anything done with a large team, is ultimately about making good decisions consistently over time. This is entirely possible for Bethesda to do, with the right people in charge to make them. That goes for the people above Bethesda's top brass, too.

Will Bethesda deliver? Yes, no, maybe. In a way it's besides the point. Instead, I've realised the thing that gives me pause, before burrowing giddily down into another warren of silly Tamriel backstory, is that the world this Elder Scrolls game will launch into will just be so thoroughly different to the last. Every video game from a western, triple-A publisher is life-or-death now. Every sales figure, metascore, or stray quote is another algorithmically driven hate-narrative, a collective Emperor's thumbs down. And every ultimate owner of these games now seems to be properly set on being really impossible not to loathe.

Bethesda can't make games that are just alright, because today alright means doom. It takes too long, for one - we're already well into GTA 6 territory here, 15 years on from Skyrim and counting, with master of whispers Jason Schreier suggesting at least another two to go. And by virtue of that, its games must surely cost too much. The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel will sell gangbusters, which will mitigate that. But at this level of spending, the level of the stratosphere, a sales hit alone arguably isn't enough. The world demands something more.

You take that long and cost that much and your game has to shake the mountains and rumble the earth. Move the industry or revive it. Sell physical consoles that are likely to cost into the low thousands. Rewrite a narrative of failure, incompetence, arrogance, waste, and decline. This is not me declaring those things necessary; it's the way of the industry now. The sands have shifted. A game like The Elder Scrolls 6 is now a symbol. In this case one of its owner, Xbox.

That company has no other cards to play but this one, as evidenced by CEO Asha Sharma jangling her Todd Howard-shaped keys every time the heat from all that delicious bloodletting gets a little much. Sentinel has to not only be successful, then, but widely adored. Anything else, after hundreds of millions on its development, billions on wider acquisitions, thousands upon thousands of layoffs (and, let's not forget, a boycott derived from still blood-splattered links to what a UN commission calls a genocide), will be a failure. Not a failure for Bethesda's developers, by any stretch, but for the company at large after the weight of context Xbox has dropped on top of them. A failure to flip over the table at the dying moment of a losing game. A failure for those who are all too desperate to change the story.

All this - all the perpetual, seemingly endless turmoil of making video games, the cynicism in the air of so many gaming spaces, the terminally compromised corporate ownership of the games themselves - is what makes excitement so hard to muster. I look at The Elder Scrolls now and can't help but feel a little tinge of anger or sadness. Anger that yet another route to open joy - and to unserious teen memories and, frankly, a tiny little part of a personal identity, no matter how hard I try to resist that - has become wound up with such unsavoury ownership. All this, reduced to yet another PR tool and distraction technique to shift eyes and words away from bleak and pointless cruelty. And then sadness, at the idea I might escape into it, whenever it finally arrives, only to find myself in exactly the world I wish I could just briefly leave behind. Bethesda's developers can recapture the magic. But can the world of video games let them?

#10656. Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S
