#10654. People are taking an unapproved weight-loss drug. AI found something surprising

An experimental weight-loss drug that has not yet received regulatory approval is already attracting users willing to obtain unapproved versions online. Now, scientists are using artificial intelligence to investigate their experiences, uncovering unexpected symptom reports that differ from those documented in clinical trials.

The drug, retatrutide, belongs to a new generation of GLP-1 medications designed to help regulate appetite and body weight. Although it remains under investigation and is not approved for use, people are purchasing unapproved formulations that are not intended for human consumption and discussing their experiences on internet forums.

Researchers at the University of Pennsylvania analyzed nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments from approximately 40,000 users to investigate these accounts. Their study, published in JAMA Network Open, provides the first systematic examination of potential side effects reported by people claiming to use unapproved retatrutide.

One finding stood out. Among users whose reported symptoms could be classified, increased appetite was the most common complaint. That was particularly unexpected because retatrutide is designed to suppress hunger, and its Phase 2 clinical trials primarily identified digestive problems as the most frequent side effects.

AI Uncovers Unexpected Weight-Loss Drug Reports

"Our goal is to identify hypotheses in online conversations that we could test. AI helps us find recurring patterns in people's self-reports, including experiences that may be missed by conventional drug-safety monitoring," says Sharath Chandra Guntuku, Research Associate Professor in Computer and Information Science (CIS), Director of Penn's Computational Social Listening Lab and senior author of the study.

However, the researchers emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as proof of previously unknown side effects. Reddit users represent a very different population from clinical trial participants, and their accounts cannot establish whether the drug caused the symptoms they described.

There is also considerable uncertainty about what these individuals are actually taking. Products sold outside regulated medical channels may differ from the genuine experimental medication in their ingredients, purity, and dosage.

"We can't say that retatrutide is causing these symptoms, or even that Reddit users are actually taking genuine retatrutide, since the drug is not legally available, and gray market formulations vary," says Lyle Ungar, Professor in CIS and a co-author of the study.

Rather than confirming new risks, the research offers scientists an opportunity to identify questions that warrant more rigorous investigation.

"This is the first glimpse into the experiences of a population that is otherwise outside conventional drug safety monitoring because they are taking unapproved versions of a drug that has yet to finish clinical trials," says Neil Sehgal, the study's first author and a doctoral student in CIS.

How Researchers Analyzed 150,000 Reddit Posts

The project builds on previous research by the University of Pennsylvania team. In that earlier investigation, scientists used large language models (LLMs), the type of artificial intelligence that powers many modern chatbots, to examine more than 400,000 Reddit posts discussing semaglutide and tirzepatide.

That analysis revealed reports of possible side effects, including menstrual irregularities, that may not have been fully documented in clinical trials or official drug safety records.

"We wanted to apply the same methodology to gray-market retatrutide," says Sehgal.

Retatrutide is particularly interesting because it works differently from existing GLP-1 medications. Semaglutide acts on one hormone receptor involved in blood sugar control and appetite regulation, while tirzepatide targets two. Retatrutide activates three receptors, potentially producing a different combination of effects throughout the body.

To investigate what people were experiencing, the researchers used AI to examine nearly 150,000 Reddit posts and comments. They identified approximately 14,000 users who claimed to have personally taken an unapproved version of retatrutide.

The AI systems then extracted descriptions of symptoms that users associated with the medication and translated those descriptions into standardized medical terminology. This allowed researchers to compare reports even when different people used different words to describe similar experiences.

After excluding expected effects such as weight loss and reduced appetite, the team identified 7,823 users who reported at least one symptom that could be medically classified.

"A researcher might be able to examine a few thousand Reddit posts by hand, but looking at nearly 150,000 would be an enormous undertaking," says Sehgal. "AI makes it possible to analyze these conversations at scale, identifying patterns in what people are sharing that would otherwise be difficult to recognize."

The most striking difference involved hunger.

In retatrutide's Phase 2 clinical trials (the drug is currently in Phase 3 trials, the final step before FDA approval), participants most commonly experienced gastrointestinal complaints, including nausea. These effects are familiar concerns with medications that influence appetite and digestion.

But among Reddit users who reported taking unapproved retatrutide, the pattern looked different. Increased appetite emerged as the most frequently described classifiable symptom, despite the substantial weight-loss effects demonstrated in clinical testing.

The researchers also compared the new findings with their earlier analysis of semaglutide and tirzepatide.

Compared with those earlier Reddit reports, discussions involving unapproved retatrutide contained proportionally more mentions of appetite-related changes, sleep disturbances, increased heart rate, and muscle-related symptoms. Reports of nausea and other digestive complaints were proportionally less common.

These differences could reflect how retatrutide interacts with the body. However, they could also result from differences between the groups of people discussing the medications, their other drug use, or the contents of the unapproved products.

"Retatrutide works differently from existing GLP-1 drugs, so it's possible that its effects on the body are different, too," says Jena Shaw Tronieri, Senior Research Investigator at Penn's Center for Weight and Eating Disorders and a co-author of the study.

"But the people discussing it on Reddit also may be different," she notes. "Users willing to obtain an unapproved medication on the gray market likely differ from other clinical populations. Some may be taking multiple experimental peptides, and we can't verify exactly what they're taking. We need more research to understand what, if anything, these differences in self-reported symptoms mean."

Can AI Detect Drug Safety Concerns Earlier?

Beyond the findings about retatrutide, the study demonstrates how AI could potentially improve the early detection of drug safety concerns.

Traditional monitoring systems depend heavily on clinical trials, reports submitted to regulators, and information collected by healthcare professionals. People obtaining experimental medications through unapproved channels may not be represented in those systems.

Social media offers another source of information, allowing researchers to identify recurring experiences that might otherwise escape attention. However, these reports must be independently investigated before they can be considered reliable evidence of a drug-related problem.

The Penn team has already seen an example of how this approach can help guide further research.

In their previous investigation of semaglutide and tirzepatide, researchers identified online reports of menstrual irregularities. Those findings subsequently encouraged another scientific team to examine similar symptoms using the FDA's database of reported adverse drug events.

The follow-up study, published in Obstetrics & Gynecology, also detected disproportionate reports of certain menstrual changes. Although those findings did not establish causation, they provided additional reasons to investigate the potential connection.

The researchers hope their retatrutide findings will lead to similar independent studies, particularly as the drug advances through clinical development.

"That's what we hope will happen with this study," says Sehgal. "Other researchers can take what we find on Reddit and look for the same patterns in other datasets, using entirely different methods. Whether those signals hold up or not, the goal is to build a better understanding of what people are actually experiencing."

The research was conducted through the University of Pennsylvania's School of Engineering and Applied Science and Perelman School of Medicine. Benjamin Rader of Boston Children's Hospital also contributed as a co-author. Funding was provided in part by the University of Pennsylvania's Penn Medical Communication Research Institute. Tronieri disclosed receiving an investigator-initiated grant from Novo Nordisk on behalf of the University of Pennsylvania, along with consulting fees from Currax Pharmaceuticals, LLC. No conflicts of interest were reported by the other authors.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania School of Engineering and Applied Science. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10653. Man rushed to hospital after drying his hair too “aggressively”

A man in the UK had to be rushed to hospital after drying his hair too “aggressively” because he was running late for work.

Everyone has been there at some point or another: your alarm hasn’t gone off, or you’ve slept through it, meaning you’re running late to get to school, work, or even a job interview. As a result, you might end up rushing through things and skipping steps that you’d take if you had the time.

Well, in the case of 21-year-old Samuel Johnson, he woke up 45 minutes later than planned for work, leaving him with just 15 minutes to get ready.

However, after being just 10 minutes late to work, the 21-year-old found himself in discomfort. The pain didn’t subside throughout the day, leading to Johnson needing to rush to hospital for a check-up.

After being checked over by doctors, the 21-year-old noted that he felt like he’d “given himself whiplash” and discovered he had a trapped nerve. How that happen? Well, he dried his hair too “roughly” while rushing.

“I got the towel caught round the back of my head, so I went to pull it off. But instead of pulling it off, the towel got caught, and my head went flying all the way down with the towel,” he told the Daily Star.

“I told the triage nurse I was in a rush this morning, and I dried my hair too aggressively and I was really embarrassed. The doctors sent me home with painkillers. When I woke up the next day I couldn’t move at all, it was so bad, I just felt like a rock.”

Marcus Hunefalk-Franzen, a 60-year-old tarot card reader and actor, said the incident happened back in 2015.

#10652. In Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert doubles down on its maximalism in audacious, faintly sinister style

And so we are back in Pywel: the super-sized and often super-pretty realm where the deserts are blood-red, the grass is a lurid shade of green, and Kliff and his merry gang of Greymanes seem to be pissed all the time. Yet what's this? Peace seems to have broken out across the region, which means our grizzled action hero is in a reflective mood.

At the outset of Charting the Unknown, Crimson Desert's recently released DLC, I'm tasked with taking Kliff out for a quiet spot of moonlit fishing. The problem is, I seem to have forgotten how to actually fish - and if memory serves, perhaps I never learned because the system was so poorly explained in the base game. So I flail around for a full 15 minutes before eventually relenting and consulting Reddit on how to snag a bite.

You might reasonably assume that these types of basic explanatory wrinkles had been ironed out in the countless updates since Crimson Desert's release in March. Certainly, developer Pearl Abyss has gone to great effort elsewhere to streamline what was, at times, a frustratingly clunky experience (those menus at launch, oh god) while adding even more stuff, such as all-new cutscenes (to improve the story's legibility) and the ability to ride wolves and tigers (because why the hell not). But for all the tinkering and wholesale additions, Crimson Desert has retained its essential character: the menus remain a total faff and the narrative, which I started afresh some weeks ago, is still bafflingly incoherent (maybe even more than I initially appreciated).

It's into this churn of ongoing development that Pearl Abyss has produced Charting the Unknown, a DLC which adds even more elements to a game already buckling under the weight of mechanical overload. Now you can build ships and sail them around Pywel's azure-blue waters. You can dive below the waves and swim with the fishes. You can also fall in love, get married, and - should things not pan out - get divorced. The everything game has become, well, even more everything in quintessentially audacious style. On that note, just wait until you see some of its supermassive Evangelion-esque maritime bosses.

Though don't get too excited. Like the base game, the DLC is a mixed bag. First, shipfaring lacks any kind of drama. Islands are so spread out as to make the actual journey painfully boring: impressionistic oceanic voyaging à la Wind Waker this is not. The diving, though, is beautifully executed. Underwater locales are notably serene, free from many of the irritating distractions that plague ground traversal.

As for the new narrative threads involving all three playable characters - grizzled Kliff, Bayonetta-alike Damiane, and gentle giant orc Oongka - they're equally uneven. Kliff's is the most interesting, transplanting the player to a desert cloaked in a thick orange sandstorm that, without the aid of a working compass, must be mapped out using beacons resembling luminescent dongs. It's pleasingly disorientating, a rare example of Pearl Abyss pushing a kind of intentional design friction, rather than accidentally creating it by inadequately explaining how to interact with the game.

Alas, there are also bugs, one of which was game-halting: Damiane's steed wouldn't spawn when I needed to follow Kliff on horseback. It was a reminder that for all the visual splendour, Crimson Desert can often feel like it's being held together by the code equivalent of gaffer tape.

Everything controls about as well as you'd expect if you've played the base game, i.e. not very well at all. Kliff may be able to run on water and soar through the sky like a cross between Matrix star Neo and Jesus, but he remains a bastard to control for even the most basic tasks. Why can't I lock onto the object a foot away? Why is he doing a three-hit combo somersault through the air when all I tried to do is pull out his lantern? These issues are compounded by the new romance and socialising options that appear every time you target an NPC: Crimson Desert becomes even fiddlier as it introduces these additional layers.

As the hours pass by - as I run, gallop, and bound through the arresting landscape in a manner that is comparatively frictionless compared to the rest of the game - my mind begins to numb. For all the extraneous mechanical gubbins, missions remain brainlessly simple: cavort from one waypoint to another and do a few simple button presses, or crack a few skulls to chug the narrative along. But then perhaps a certain numbing is the point of Crimson Desert? At a time when the real world is markedly inhospitable (diminishing job prospects, the ongoing cost-of-living crisis, the looming threat of geopolitical meltdown), Pearl Abyss' epic swaddles the player like a swords-and-sorcery comfort blanket. It is a safe space to disappear into for hours. Leave your worldly woes at the start screen.

The impression this mash-up of a game appears to want to impart is generosity: here, Pearl Abyss seems to say, is a product capable of accommodating your every wish, will, and whim. It appears to have left no gameplay stone unturned in its ultimate quest to provide the player with total satisfaction - a kind of blissed-out contentment at the thought of never having to leave this almost divine realm.

But rather than generous, perhaps there is something nefarious about Crimson Desert's maximalist design philosophy. It strikes me again that this is the most uniquely modern title I've ever played, as if the Grand Theft Auto and Assassin's Creed franchises, countless MMORPGs (the genre in which Pearl Abyss cut its teeth), and many hyper-compulsive mobile titles were squished together in an attention-devouring final form. One so mightily captivating, so singularly consuming, that the player keeps playing until their lifeforce is depleted and they can play no more.

That seems to be the way these "forever" games curve: toward the player's surrender - of mind, body, and time. And at least until Grand Theft Auto 6 is released in a few weeks, Crimson Desert and its new DLC seem to be the purest expression yet of this type of thing. Wading through its sheer mass of hit-or-miss distractions (which could expand even more), the design ethos begins to grate. As if I'm stuck in an elaborate, endlessly bifurcating maze of hamster wheels, always busy, though never enjoying any discernible pay-off. And so, as its presentational lustre wears off further, and its eye-popping massiveness becomes less of a novelty, Crimson Desert begins to hit differently: much less high-fantasy heaven and more like hell.

#10650. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#10648. What we've been playing - "Sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island"

A few of the things that have us hooked this week.

Well, hello! I am once again wearing the Bertie hat in your usual host's absence, so joyous welcomes to our regular feature in which we talk about what we've been playing in recent times. This week, Marie has been revisiting Zelda - although perhaps not the one you might expect - while Dom is rediscovering Vice City of the 80s. Kelsey is playing some very weird cards, Connor is back in WoW - possibly forever - and I'm equally smitten with a different RPG.

But enough about us, what about you? What have you been playing this week? Do tell all! And don't forget we have an entire archive of previous What We've Been Playings for your leisurely perusal, just in case you forgot what you were playing a year or a month or a week ago. Bye!

Once again, I am finally playing a game from my backlog that I should've played years ago, and that's the delightful albeit dark card game Inscryption. There's so much more to this than you'd first expect, and I'm surprised the community has been able to keep the game's best bits under wraps for so long. Ultimately, nothing could've prepared me for Act 2, and my only real qualm with the whole experience is that I wish Act 2 had its own endless roguelike mode; you'd never be able to pull me away from it.

The game falters a little at the start of Act 3, I'll admit, and I've found myself very frustrated by what is an otherwise relatively easy time. This is down to me missing some rather useful earlier cards that I've since learnt would've made this final stretch much breezier, but oh well. I will still take down the Uberbots one way or another for the love of the game!

I've sunk every free hour I have into World of Warcraft: Forever since its release on Wednesday night. As of writing I am Level 19, waiting for an escort quest to come up in Westfall. So far things seem largely the same as in the original MMO, though the little changes and improvements I've seen have firmly planted a smile on my face.

This includes the new weather system. Like many others I was caught in a snow storm in Dun Morogh this week, a seemingly random environmental transformation that drastically limits view distance and blankets your screen with the cold stuff. It adds a lot, I think, to the fantasy of the game. I'll be playing this all weekend and for the foreseeable future.

I'll let you guess why I'm playing this right now of all times. Ahead of Grand Theft Auto 6's launch, I've made it my unofficial job to internalise the map of Vice City. I'm spending time driving around the strip, familiarising myself with the docklands, going for a few rounds of golf up in Leaf Links, and sniffing out the warehouses on Prawn Island. I'm immensely curious how similar the map is in GTA 6, and can't wait to see what's changed in the interceding 40 years in-universe. It's even better having V-Rock blast Iron Maiden and Twisted Sister at me; over the past few evenings, I've been playing this extra loud so it drowns out the sound of local fireworks for the sake of my poor, terrified dog. I don't think he's a fan of Bruce 'the air raid siren' Dickinson's voice - but it's better than all the apocalyptic banging.

The cold winter nights are drawing in, which calls for blankets, a warm drink, and a nice peaceful game before bed. I don't have an exact reason for going back to Tears of the Kingdom. Perhaps it's me diving into Ocarina of Time that fuelled my curiosity about returning, but what's surprised me the most is there's still so much I didn't see or do in my first 190 hours with it.

I started again, cleared my save file, and it turns out I've completely forgotten most things about the game - which is by no means a complaint! I'm rather enjoying making the little discoveries that are either brand-new to me, or ones that make me go 'AH-HA, I remember you' (in the case of Gloom Hands, the reaction was 'NOPE, NOPE, NOPE, NOPE.')

I've also had another surprise. I play in handheld mode mostly, and ToTK was all looking a bit blurry on the Switch 2's screen. So I got the upgrade pack and suddenly everything became beautiful again - Link was in focus, the individual blades of grass in Hyrule Field were clearly whipping in the wind, and the crispy snow around Rito village almost sparkled. I don't often get upgrade packs for games I already own, but this one was worth it.

I suspect this won't be the last time you see me yapping about Queen's Domain here. I've already enthused about it elsewhere on the site this week, but I've managed to squeeze in a few more hours since then, and it continues to enthrall. Here's a small moment from my most recent go: I'd been fumbling about the Tower of Knowledge for a while at this point, dying slightly more than I'd like to those jabby gargoyle gits and lumbering chimera things, so I thought I'd have a little wander away from the violence and have an explore. That was when I noticed the gap on the bookshelf, wide enough to slot in the mysterious tome - the Ratty Bug Compendium - I'd been wondering about ever since I picked it up elsewhere some five hours earlier.

Moments later a secret passage had opened, leading out to a balcony overlooking the ocean. Treasure! Exciting! But just as I'm about to go back inside, I notice a little outcrop on the exterior of the tower, seemingly reachable with my sword's magic dashing power. So over I go, discovering an otherwise inaccessible route into an inner chamber. There, I find a lever, which I excitedly pull, only to be rewarded with... nothing at all. Until five minutes or so later, when I discover a secret panel has now opened up on the other side of the tower, granting me access to the treats within. In isolation, it's not a remarkable gaming anecdote, but Queen's Domain is just crammed with these little incremental secrets and surprises, full of clever design that makes exploration feel so rewarding. I've already spotted more enigmas waiting to be cracked, and if Queen's Domain keeps distracting me like this, I might never escape its pull.

#10647. MIT scientists build a tiny robot that swims using living muscle cells

A swimming robot does not necessarily need a bulky motor or powerful mechanical parts to move through water. MIT researchers have demonstrated that a single, carefully engineered layer of living muscle cells can generate enough force to propel a tiny robot forward.

The engineers developed an extremely thin swimming robot that uses living muscle tissue to move its fins. Described in a new study published in Advanced Functional Materials, the device has a flexible gel framework roughly the length and width of a stick of gum. The gel forms two fins, each coated with a layer of muscle cells far thinner than a human hair. These cells have been genetically modified to contract when exposed to light.

By directing light onto either fin, the researchers can trigger muscle contractions that make the fin flap and push the robot through water. Adjusting which fin receives light and how frequently it is illuminated allows the team to control both its speed and direction.

In experiments, the remarkably thin robot successfully swam and turned through a simple underwater maze. At maximum speed, it covered approximately four times its body length per minute. Although that is much slower than an Olympic swimmer, who can travel up to 65 body lengths per minute, it is comparable to the swimming pace of a cow shark moving leisurely through the ocean.

"It takes a lot of force to move through water versus air," says study author Ritu Raman, associate professor of mechanical engineering at MIT. "The robot's quite strong, given its size. "

A New Generation of Robots Powered by Living Muscle

The device represents the first demonstration of a very thin, two-dimensional robot that can move from place to place using living muscle tissue.

Most existing biohybrid robots rely on relatively large, three-dimensional structures made from laboratory-grown skeletal muscle. Producing these structures typically requires millions of cells. Raman explains that thinner designs could reduce manufacturing costs while potentially improving movement efficiency.

"Currently, biohybrid robots from our group and others' are built from bulky, 3D chunks of lab-grown skeletal muscle that require millions of cells to fabricate," says Raman, who notes that thinner, less bulky designs such as the team's new bot could be cheaper to build and could move more efficiently. "We believe that biohybrid robots powered by living muscle could one day perform delicate jobs like exploring environments too fragile or unpredictable for conventional hardware, because living tissue is soft, responsive to its surroundings, and can heal itself."

The MIT research team also included first author Maheera Bawa, along with Arielle Berman, Laura Schwendeman, Ferdows Afghah, and Seanbiron Johnson.

From Artificial Muscle to a Swimming Robot

The new swimming robot builds on an earlier achievement by Raman's laboratory. Last year, the researchers created an artificial muscle disk inspired by the human iris, demonstrating how a thin layer of living cells could produce precisely controlled movements.

For that project, the engineers patterned a gel disk with grooves extending outward from its center and forming concentric circles. They then placed living muscle cells on the surface, where the cells grew into a thin layer following the patterned channels.

When illuminated, the muscle tissue contracted in different directions, causing the disk to stretch and compress. Its movements resembled the way the iris of the human eye changes shape as the pupil expands or narrows.

The experiment established that researchers could grow extremely thin muscle layers in intricate arrangements and use light to control their movements along multiple directions.

"People hadn't seen this muscle architecture engineered from scratch before," Raman says. "And the cells were moving in multiple directions. But they only moved about 100 microns. From a robotics perspective, their movements were tiny."

For the latest project, the researchers wanted to dramatically increase the amount of movement and force generated by the muscle cells. Their goal was to produce contractions powerful enough to propel a robot through water.

They determined that achieving this would require a better foundation for the living muscle tissue.

The earlier artificial iris had used fibrin, an exceptionally soft gel, as its underlying structure. However, the team found that fibrin could quickly shrink under the forces exerted by contracting muscle cells, limiting the effectiveness of the resulting movement.

To overcome this problem, the engineers investigated three characteristics of the gel foundation: its chemical composition, its stiffness, and the dimensions and geometry of the grooves used to organize muscle growth.

"For engineering any type of tissue, it's known that these are knobs you can tune," Raman says. "And we wanted to optimize all these parameters to support live muscle cells."

Engineering a Stronger Muscle-Powered Robot

Finding the right supporting material required testing gels with different formulations, stiffness levels, and surface patterns.

The researchers compared several groove shapes, including narrow channels with square profiles and longer channels with curved bottoms. They discovered that muscle cells aligned more effectively inside square-shaped grooves.

This alignment was important because muscle cells that grow in the same direction are more likely to merge into fibers. Those fibers can then form stronger muscle tissue that contracts in a coordinated way. The square grooves therefore provided a more effective structure for producing force.

The team also replaced fibrin with gelatin methacrylate (GelMA), a material commonly used to grow and engineer biological tissue.

By adjusting the formulation of GelMA, the researchers produced supporting gels with varying degrees of stiffness. They then examined how muscle cells developed on each surface and measured their ability to generate force.

Stiffer gels produced the best results. On these firmer surfaces, muscle cells grew in better alignment and generated stronger contractions.

The engineers next investigated how thick the gel should be. They determined that a film measuring approximately half a millimeter offered an effective balance. It was sturdy enough to support a single layer of muscle cells while remaining sufficiently lightweight to prevent the contracting cells from detaching from the surface.

The team further improved muscle performance through a form of exercise. Repeated pulses of light stimulated the cells to contract, strengthening the muscle tissue before it was incorporated into the swimming robot.

With the gel structure optimized and the muscle cells strengthened, the researchers assembled their swimming device.

The finished robot consisted of a thin gel framework divided into two fins. Both sides of the gel were patterned with square-bottomed grooves and covered with living muscle cells. As the cells developed, they fused into organized muscle fibers capable of contracting together.

Each fin could be activated independently using light, giving the researchers a simple way to control the robot's movements.

"You can think of the robot as having two independent muscles," Raman says. "If we shine a light on just one, only that muscle moves. If shining on both, they both flap."

To test the device, the researchers placed it inside a large petri dish filled with water. They then moved a light source by hand, illuminating the fins to guide the robot through a maze positioned inside the dish.

As the muscles contracted, the fins flapped and propelled the robot through the water. By changing the light stimulation, the engineers successfully controlled its movement and helped it navigate the obstacles.

Although the robot demonstrated the ability to swim and steer, its current shape remains relatively simple. The primary objective of the experiment was to establish that an exceptionally thin layer of engineered muscle tissue could generate enough force for swimming.

The researchers now plan to refine the robot's body to improve its speed. Beyond demonstrating a new approach to biohybrid robotics, the technology could eventually be useful for monitoring aquatic environments or operating in locations where conventional rigid machines might cause damage.

"Our next goal is to optimize the body design to enable faster swimming," Raman says. "But even at slow swim speeds, one could imagine a muscle-powered swimmer being used for purposes like environmental monitoring in aquatic environments."

The Office of Naval Research provided partial funding for this research.

#10646. Man installs outdoor cooling system to stop gaming PC overheating his room

A man has spent two months building an outdoor cooling system to stop his gaming computer from making his room too warm.

The builder explained that he decided to take advantage of freezing winter temperatures by installing part of his PC’s cooling system outside his house.

With temperatures dropping to -4°C (25°F), they mounted a radiator and two fans outside their house, connecting them to the PC through a wall.

Explaining how the system works in a Reddit post, they said it pumps liquid outside to cool it before sending it back into the computer, keeping temperatures down without heating the room.

He also revealed that the project was partly motivated by wanting to overclock his computer, having previously reached the temperature limits of his existing cooling system.

However, getting everything working took some effort, including having to install sensors and write software to prevent the liquid from becoming too cold, which could cause condensation to form on the PC’s components.

Several problems were encountered along the way, including an electrical fault and accidentally flooding the computer after forgetting to close part of the cooling system.

The result, they say, is a CPU that now runs at around 40-45°C (104-113°F) with the outdoor system activated, compared to 60-65°C (140-149°F) using conventional cooling after a separate processor modification.

More importantly, his room no longer heats up during gaming. In fact, the cooling system might be working a little too well.

“When the fans in the pc are running, […] it becomes a freaking air conditioner and my feet start to freeze.”

#10643. Despite promising a heady mix of Vampire Survivors and Warhammer fan-service, Warhammer Survivors is somehow less than the sum of its parts

You know my favourite thing about Vampire Survivors? The slow build of strength, the swelling power fantasy, the edging. Developer Poncle has proved itself incredibly adept at goading players into ever more ludicrous setups, demonstrating that with some patience, a canny eye for build-crafting, and some very rudimentary knowledge of your surroundings, even an old man armed with garlic can be an extinction level event for the amassing undead. Warhammer Survivors sadly never reaches those heights.

Created by Warhammer 40,000: Boltgun 2 studio Auroch Digital, Warhammer Survivors is - as its name suggests - an officially-licensed attempt to transplant developer Poncle's winning Vampire Survivors' formula to the Warhammer universe. Cue 40+ characters ripped from Warhammer lore getting the action-roguelike treatment, crossbows instead of clock lancets, rocket launchers instead of fire wands, bomb Squids instead of skeletons… you get the idea.

But this fusion doesn't really work. While Vampire Survivors was always a bit of an uphill battle from the start, it still hooked you with a thread that pulled you forward, tempting you with power-ups and other intriguing toys to play with en route. If hour one was a slog, hour five sparked curiosity, and hour 10 was a romp. In Warhammer Survivors, however, hour 10 feels like just as much of a slog as hour one. And even though Warhammer Survivors offers two alternate universes - the grimdark future of 40K and the old-school fantasy of Age of Sigmar, each a full game in its own right - this does little to alleviate the grind.

It's all so slow. Characters move slowly, upgrades come slowly, unlocking the mode that makes things move a bit faster comes slowly. Every inch feels like a mile, and the moreish power creep - tantalisingly propulsive in Vampire Survivors - is out of reach for so long per run here, it's discouraging. That's not to say there isn't any resolution to all the tension. Every now and then, you'll roll the right dice, appease the right gods, make the right moves. Your miniature pixel heroes will overcome the odds, and burn through the writhing mass of Orks, Skaven, Tyranids, Nurgle, or whatever other bastards are trying to get you down today.

The Survivors genre - sometimes known as 'bullet heaven' now - lives and dies on the strength of the over-powered nonsense that emerges as all your weapons and evolutions start to harmonise with each other in brutal synchronicity. Warhammer Survivors seems reluctant to let you get to this level, though. Even 30 hours in (!), some runs will end before they ever really get started - no matter how many skips, rerolls, or other chance-altering upgrades you've committed to your save file.

And because this is really two games glued into one, you have to do all the requisite grinding and levelling on both sides. With massive maps and impossibly slow character movement (especially on some of the bulkier characters), collecting all the items per location is more of a chore than it is fun. This wouldn't be so bad if these items were just curios, but in most cases they're pretty significant quality-of-life enhancers. Why are you hiding them from me, Auroch!?

It's almost impressive how Warhammer Survivors manages to feel like a step back from Poncle's original in many ways: yes, there's more content, but there appears to be a lack of understanding of why this genre immediately took off and spawned a myriad of imitators. The very worst weapons are the only ones available at the beginning, so unlocking new characters and armaments is necessary rather than a fun bonus. Lore is paper thin - in Vampire Survivors, we had a blank canvas and the lack of lore didn't really matter, but Warhammer is steeped in its own mythos, so relegating it to merely set dressing feels remiss. Some of the synergies between items and their evolutions don't really make sense. The maps are awkward and fussy to navigate. The locked requirements to take on stage-end bosses are even more cryptic than in Vampire Survivors. In almost all the ways that matter when it comes to fun, Warhammer Survivors pales in comparison to its forebear. This wouldn't be so egregious if the game wasn't trying to emulate it quite so completely, but it is - and as such, all the actually decent design choices hidden behind layers of frustration are marred.

As a Warhammer and Survivors fan, I've found myself floating back to the game, even if - sometimes - my eyes roll back into my head within minutes of my first run. But it's not a complete disaster: on those occasions where the stars do align, Warhammer Survivors finds its way to a power fantasy worthy of both franchises. You'll grab a Flamer early doors, then pick up the passive Glue to reduce incoming damage. You'll gain the Heavy Flamer, the Boltgun, and the projectile-multiplying Dice Cube, eventually unlocking the Heavy Bolter - slicing through enemies and all but melting the face off that pesky Prophet of Gork.

I just wish there wasn't so much friction in actually getting to that point. I imagine Warhammer Survivors is the sort of game I'll keep on my harddrive (and on multiple devices; it costs a fiver and it's on Game Pass) for a while. If you can endure the teething pains and engage with the wobblier systems, there's the usual Survivors payoff waiting for you as the screen fills with pixelated blood and countless helpless enemies fall to your blades. Maybe the stress of the early game even makes this catharsis even better. So even with its qualms, Warhammer Survivors is still an enjoyable, lo-fi stress reliever to snack on in between other, meatier games.

#10641. Cambridge scientists create a $10 chip that could put a medical lab on your wrist

A new optical sensor costing just $10 could bring laboratory-level precision to wearable health monitors, potentially allowing people to track blood glucose without needles. The technology could also transform food testing, industrial quality control, and environmental monitoring.

Researchers at the University of Cambridge and GlitterinTech (a start-up founded by the same research group) have developed a new type of optical spectrometer that combines exceptional accuracy with a remarkably small size and low cost. Measuring just centimeters across, the device could be incorporated into portable electronics and wearable health technology. Its potential applications range from monitoring important health markers in real time to identifying materials and detecting changes in chemical composition.

Optical spectrometers work by analyzing light to reveal information about the substances it interacts with. They are essential tools in medicine, manufacturing, chemical testing, and environmental research. However, making these instruments smaller has traditionally meant compromising their performance. Compact spectrometers often have limited measurement ranges, reduced resolution, or lower accuracy, making them better suited to identifying substances than performing precise measurements.

The researchers believe their new design could overcome these limitations. Known as a convolutional spectrometer, it uses a mathematical principle called the convolution theorem to analyze light in a fundamentally different way. According to the team, this approach delivers performance that surpasses existing types of spectrometers in several key areas, including designs based on light dispersion, Fourier transforms, and computational reconstruction.

A New Way to Analyze Light With Extraordinary Precision

Traditional spectrometers generally separate incoming light into its component wavelengths or use complex calculations to reconstruct its spectrum. The new device takes another approach by performing a mathematical operation called convolution directly on the incoming light.

To accomplish this, the researchers arranged optical components with repeating spectral responses in a sequence. These components can include unbalanced Mach-Zehnder interferometers or micro-ring resonators. By adjusting them in proportion to one another, the system can shift its response to different wavelengths in a controlled, linear fashion. Fast Fourier transforms are then used to accurately recover the light spectrum.

"The key insight was to go back to the mathematics and ask whether there was a fundamentally cleaner way to retrieve spectra," said Dr. Chunhui Yao, a lead author of the study. "By using the convolution theorem directly in the optical domain, we avoid many of the limitations that have held miniaturized spectrometers back. This gives us high precision, strong noise tolerance, and very low computational overhead, all in a compact and low-cost system."

The researchers built the spectrometer using a silicon nitride photonic platform and integrated it with onboard electronics. It operates across a broad range of near-infrared wavelengths (1200-1700 nm) and can collect and process measurements in less than a second.

Another important advantage is its ability to expand its measurement capabilities without major hardware changes. Because the system uses repeating optical patterns, its spectral bandwidth can theoretically be extended almost without limit. Adding more optical components in sequence can also increase its resolution exponentially.

A $10 Sensor That Could Transform Food Testing and Healthcare

To evaluate the device outside purely theoretical measurements, the researchers tested it on a variety of materials and substances. The results demonstrated its potential for applications that normally depend on larger, more expensive laboratory instruments.

When used to identify plastics, pharmaceuticals, coffee, flour, and tea, the spectrometer achieved a 100% classification success rate in the reported tests. It also measured concentrations in water-based and organic solutions with accuracies of approximately 0.01%, exceeding the performance of commercial benchtop spectrometers in those experiments.

The healthcare demonstrations were particularly promising. Under conditions designed to reflect real physiological environments, the device accurately measured several human biomarkers, including skin moisture, blood alcohol, blood lactate, and blood glucose.

The researchers also demonstrated noninvasive glucose tracking over an extended period in one participant. Although further testing would be needed before the technology could become a practical medical product, the finding suggests a possible route toward monitoring blood sugar without repeatedly piercing the skin.

"These biomedical demonstrations are particularly exciting," said Prof. Qixiang Cheng, who led the project. "What makes this work stand out is not just performance in the lab, but technical readiness. Dr. Chunhui Yao's contribution was crucial in translating a mathematical concept into a fully packaged, robust system that operates reliably across temperature extremes and real-world conditions. That combination is what opens the door to practical deployment."

The sensor also demonstrated impressive resistance to temperature changes. It continued operating reliably between -20°C and 80°C, an unusually wide temperature range for a miniature spectrometer. This durability could be especially valuable for wearable devices, factory equipment, and sensors operating outdoors.

Why a Simpler Design Could Be a Major Breakthrough

The new spectrometer's advantages extend beyond its accuracy, size, and price. Its internal design also reduces the amount of computing power needed to analyze incoming light.

Many existing miniature spectrometers depend on complicated calibration procedures and demanding algorithms to reconstruct spectral information. These methods can become unreliable when measurements contain noise, and they often require substantial processing resources.

The Cambridge team's convolution-based approach avoids much of this complexity. Because the measurements follow a linear mathematical relationship, the system can recover spectra rapidly and consistently while using relatively little computational power.

"This is a beautiful example of how elegant engineering can unlock real impact," said Prof. Richard Penty, who contributed to the photonic system design and integration. "The architecture is simple, scalable, and manufacturable, yet it delivers precision that rivals or exceeds much larger instruments. Dr. Chunhui Yao played a central role in bringing together the photonic design, system integration, and experimental validation that made this possible."

Its relatively simple construction could also make the technology easier to manufacture on a large scale, potentially bringing sophisticated optical measurements to devices where conventional spectrometers would be too bulky or expensive.

Bringing Laboratory-Grade Measurements to Everyday Devices

The researchers envision a future in which accurate optical sensing becomes a routine feature of everyday technology rather than something largely confined to specialized laboratories.

In industrial settings, inexpensive spectrometers could be installed throughout manufacturing facilities to continuously check material composition and product quality. Similar sensors could monitor food freshness, identify substances, and analyze environmental conditions on a much larger scale than is currently practical.

Healthcare could see equally significant changes. Compact sensors might eventually enable wearables to measure hydration levels, detect signs of alcohol intoxication, track biomarkers related to physical fitness, or continuously monitor glucose levels for people managing diabetes.

"Our vision is to make spectrometry as ubiquitous as temperature or motion sensing," added Prof. Qixiang Cheng. "This work shows that high-quality spectral information doesn't have to be confined to the laboratory -- it can be embedded directly into the technologies people use every day."

As photonic technology becomes smaller and more advanced, the convolutional spectrometer provides a promising example of how mathematical insights and practical engineering can make sophisticated scientific instruments simpler, cheaper, and more accessible. By combining high precision with a device that costs around $10, the researchers have opened a potential path toward a new generation of smart sensors capable of measuring the hidden chemistry of our bodies and surroundings.

Materials provided by University of Cambridge. Original written by Michael Shuff. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10640. World of Warcraft: Forever's magical opening week reminds us all about the power of the MMO, and the threat it poses to your free time

World of Warcraft: Forever is a time vampire. Since its release last Wednesday, I have spent 78 hours in Azeroth. Some of that I was AFK, sure. But for many of those hours, I was lost, happily, in the rolling hills and vast planes of Blizzard's MMO - now enhanced, beautified, and given a new social energy that makes the old feel wonderfully new.

The original World of Warcraft is so beloved, so influential, that I wasn't convinced Forever could build upon it without cheapening the overall experience; without creating glaring clashes between its new additions and legacy elements that, in some instances, are now over two decades old. And while there's still plenty I haven't seen after 78 hours and 35 levels, I can speak to one truth: WoW Forever is stunning work.

Perhaps it's best to start with WoW: Forever's most obvious changes: its new lighting and weather effects. People love to speak about getting lost in MMOs, being consumed by their digital worlds, but as much as I've enjoyed World of Warcraft in the past, its ageing graphics tech never managed to ensnare me that way. Forever's new visual elements, though, have gone a long way toward pulling me in.

As I look out to sea, waves roll across the ocean. Inland, water flows fast along the rivers of Stranglethorn Vale. These are largely cosmetic additions, sure, but they make Azeroth feel so much more real. Dun Morogh's snowstorms - likely the first exposure many players will have to Forever's new weather effects - transform a peaceful, picturesque landscape into something hostile at a moment's notice. It's the same for Tanris; its vast desert now blighted with sandstorms that bring raging life to an otherwise fairly barren zone.

World of Warcraft; Forever's enhancements go beyond visual, of course. Before launch, Blizzard described Forever as an opportunity to address imbalances, and to show a bit of love to underserved playstyles and classes. That's why I decided I'd play as a druid and work toward creating a balance caster: a ranged DPS druid far slower to level than a feral one, and far less useful as a magic DPS compared to a mage. In other words, an unoptimal choice for the truly silly.

I actually levelled a balance druid to max in Classic World of Warcraft some years back and it was an arduous process. In Forever, though, adjustments made to the spells and talents have made it much less frustrating so far. You do more damage, for starters, and alterations to the talent tree and unlockable skills mean you don't run into the sort of mana problems that crippled balance druids previously. From what I've seen, all the ugly duckling class specialisations - like Retribution Paladins and Enhancement Shamans - have received similar changes.

My interest had been similarly piqued before launch by the talk of WoW: Forever's new dungeons. I still need more time to understand how these additions affect the wider leveling process, but already I can see they've been crafted with care, fitting nicely in the gaps between the original dungeons on a progression level.

Hall of Thanes is a Ragefire Chasm equivalent for Alliance players, serving as a solid introduction to dungeons for newcomers before they head off to The Deadmines and beyond. Here, you delve into the bowels of Ironforge, navigating tombs and tunnels in search of treasure and tackling foes beneath the seat of dwarvish power. It's simple and clean like all early game Classic dungeons are, made with such faithfulness to the original vision that I could be convinced it was always present.

It's also a lovely narrative introduction to one of the world's many antagonists, the Dark Iron Dwarves. In the original World of Warcraft, players would first encounter them across a scattering of quests before being thrust towards Blackrock Mountain. Now, though, they show up early and immediately plant intrigue. What's the deal with these drilling machines? What about the fire elementals they're with?

Forever's new dungeons also introduce some well-crafted quests. Anyone who's played Classic WoW can recount the frustration of dungeons that, thanks to some awkward design, often require two separate completions to finish off. Blackfathom Deeps is a prime example; there are never enough twilight pendants to go around, forcing players to go back in for a second run if they want to tick it off. As such, many, myself included, don't bother. World of Warcraft: Forever, though - with some exceptions - appears to favour a one-and-done approach to dungeons. In the new Excavation Site: Wetlands, for instance, there are just enough raptors hidden in tall grass for everyone to complete their hides in one go. A little thing, but meaningful.

But beyond all this, perhaps the most wonderful thing about World of Warcraft: Forever is how full it feels. It's lively, bustling with action and excitement. Some of this is just that new MMO energy, but Blizzard's new mega server approach, which vastly increases the number of players you'll see doing quests compared to a regular server, is undoubtedly a significant factor too.

Every major city is packed with people having their own adventure, each quest zone is littered with heroes doing their bit to save the world. And this opens the door to social interactions, the beating heart of any MMO. The sight of dozens of players jumping off a zeppelin or flooding onto an elevator in unison is a joy, and grouping with strangers for an escort quest or an elite quest is a curative for digital hermits.

At one point, I find myself drawn into a conversation with a mage during a quest for troll ears. The mobs were hotly contested, and Horde players were quick to snatch tags. The few minutes I spent there, talking about what's going on and how we're finding the game were lovely, and World of Warcraft: Forever's set-up encourages so much more of this kind of thing.

So, while I probably have plenty more sleepless nights left to go before I max out my druid, World of Warcraft: Forever has made a strong early impression. It's taken the original game and expanded on it in genuinely meaningful ways, leaving the things that worked untouched while enriching the elements needed some love. It's a wonderful point to jump into the game if you're a new player, then, and a refreshed classic full of twists for the returning veteran. Whatever the coming months and years may bring, this opening week has been magical.

#10639. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10637. ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

#10635. Scientists overcome a hidden obstacle to more powerful quantum computers

Silicon spin qubits could play a major role in the future of quantum computing because they can be produced using the same semiconductor manufacturing methods that make today's computer chips. However, researchers have faced a significant challenge. When multiple silicon qubits are controlled at the same time, their operations tend to become less accurate. Building practical, large quantum computers will require scientists to control many qubits simultaneously while keeping errors to a minimum.

Researchers at the RIKEN Center for Emergent Matter Science and their collaborators have now made important progress toward solving this problem. In a study published in Nature Physics, the team demonstrated highly accurate control of several silicon spin qubits operating simultaneously. Their experiments used an array of five qubits connected to a shared microwave control line. By identifying a major cause of errors during simultaneous operation, the scientists developed a technique to reduce those errors and maintain precision.

Leon Camenzind, a RIKEN research scientist and co-first author who supervised the experiments, explains the significance of the results: "This shows that high-fidelity control can be maintained even when several qubits are operated at the same time and represents an important step toward scaling up silicon-based quantum processors."

Scientists Identify a Hidden Source of Quantum Computing Errors

Unlike conventional computers, which store information as bits representing either 0 or 1, quantum computers use quantum bits, or qubits, that can exist in combinations of both states. Silicon spin qubits store this information using the spin of individual electrons confined within silicon. Performing reliable quantum calculations requires extremely precise control over these electron spins.

Microwave signals provide that control, but operating multiple qubits simultaneously can create unwanted effects that interfere with their accuracy. The researchers traced much of this problem to a phenomenon called the AC Stark shift.

This effect occurs when microwave control signals alter a qubit's effective energy levels, producing changes in the phase of its quantum state. Those phase shifts can disrupt the intended operation of individual qubits, particularly when several are being controlled at once.

To counteract the problem, the team created a calibration method that compensates for these unwanted shifts. Rather than separately calibrating every possible combination of qubits, the technique relies on calibrations performed between pairs of qubits. This makes the approach more practical for systems containing larger numbers of quantum bits.

"Rather than requiring an entirely separate calibration procedure for every possible combination of qubits, our method uses calibrations between pairs of qubits to compensate for the unwanted phase shifts," explains Yi-Hsien Wu, the first author who carried out the experiments as part of his PhD research.

Quantum Qubits Achieve Accuracy Above 99.99%

The researchers combined their calibration technique with an improved device design and carefully adjusted microwave control pulses. Their results showed that exceptionally high accuracy could be maintained even when multiple qubits were operating simultaneously.

When the five qubits were tested individually, each achieved single-qubit gate fidelity well above 99.99%, with the highest approaching 99.999%. Fidelity describes how closely a quantum operation matches its intended result.

The team then tested how well the system performed when several qubits were active together. With as many as three qubits operating simultaneously, fidelity remained above 99.99%. Even when all five qubits were controlled at once, accuracy stayed at approximately 99.9%, approaching the levels needed for practical fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Although the calibration method greatly reduced errors, it did not eliminate them entirely. The researchers found that the remaining loss of accuracy was linked to drive-induced decoherence, an effect caused by the greater microwave power required to operate multiple qubits simultaneously.

Unlike the unwanted phase shifts corrected by the new technique, decoherence involves the loss of the delicate quantum coherence needed for reliable operations.

According to Camenzind, "This is a limitation, but further improvements to device design could help mitigate this source of error."

A New Path Toward Larger Quantum Processors

The findings show that controlling several silicon spin qubits at the same time does not necessarily require a major sacrifice in accuracy. By correcting a key source of errors, the researchers have demonstrated a promising approach for operating more qubits through shared microwave control lines.

This is especially important for the development of large quantum processors. Giving every qubit its own independent control system would make increasingly complex devices difficult to manage. Shared control lines could offer a more practical way to expand these systems while maintaining the precision needed for quantum calculations.

"By addressing a key source of control errors through pairwise calibration," says Camenzind. "Our study supports the use of shared control lines as a practical approach to scaling up quantum processors. Combined with silicon's compatibility with established semiconductor manufacturing, we believe that this advance could help pave the way toward larger quantum processors capable of performing increasingly complex computations."

The broader objective is to develop quantum computers that can reliably perform calculations despite the errors and disturbances that affect quantum systems. Such machines, known as fault-tolerant quantum computers, will require precise, dependable control over large numbers of qubits.

"Our long-term goal is to build a fault-tolerant quantum computer based on silicon spin qubits, and reliable parallel control is an essential step toward that goal," adds Seigo Tarucha, project manager of a silicon quantum computing project under JST's Moonshot Goal 6.

#10634. Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.
