#10630. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

#10628. Google Photos now lets you turn off background music in Memories

In an update, Google Photos is making it possible to fully disable background music in Memories.

Memories in Google Photos are a fun way to relive some highlighted moments in your photo library, but it can be a little frustrating to see a video pop up only to have the default background music to play over those memories.

Google is now rolling out an option to disable background music entirely.

This new global setting can be found under Photos settings > Preferences > Memories with a “Background music” toggle being added. We’re not seeing this in v7.99 on Android, but Google says that it is on its way to the app.

We are introducing a new setting across all platforms that allows you to disable background music added by Photos to Memories. When turned off, this feature prevents extra music tracks from playing during your Memories. If your Memory includes a video, you’ll still be able to hear its original audio.

Google adds that this is a global setting that applies to all Memories, including the end-of-year recap.

Beyond adding the option to remove background music, Google says that the variety of background music options has also been updated, so you might start hearing new tracks if you do opt to leave the setting turned on.

#10626. Google will fix Nest devices that didn’t adjust for Daylight Savings, breaking alarms and more

Google says it will roll out a fix for Nest devices that were impacted by problems with Daylight Savings adjustments due to changes in Canada.

A number of Nest Hub and Nest Thermostat users on Reddit noticed this week that their devices were showing the wrong time following the Daylight Savings time change.

This isn’t just a display bug, with real impacts including late alarms, delayed or broken automations, and altered schedules, among other impacts. The problem is specific to certain parts of Canada, user reports show, as changes in the country left some provinces skipping Daylight Savings changes while others kept the status quo. Google previously issued guidance on how to handle these changes on Android phones.

On Nest devices, though, adjusting time zones isn’t quite as easy, so this was a bigger headache.

Google is working on a fix, though, the company confirmed to Android Authority. In a statement, Google says that it is aware of the problem and working to resolve it.

Google is aware of an issue affecting a small subset of devices in some provinces in Canada where daylight savings is no longer observed. We apologize for any inconvenience and are actively working on a fix.

Google also replied to some affected users to confirm a fix is coming but, as of November 4, the fix does not appear to be in place.

#10624. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10623. A tiny supercurrent could unlock quantum information without destroying it

One of the biggest obstacles in quantum computing is surprisingly simple to describe: how do you read information without changing or destroying it? Unlike the bits used in ordinary computers, quantum bits, or qubits, are extraordinarily sensitive to measurement. Trying to determine their state can cause that state to collapse, potentially erasing the very information scientists want to retrieve.

Researchers have now proposed a way to overcome much of this difficulty by using an unusual electrical signal produced by the qubit itself. Their approach focuses on a special type known as an "Andreev qubit," which can generate an oscillating electric supercurrent through its own quantum behavior. By monitoring this current, scientists may be able to determine the qubit's state without directly measuring it or significantly disturbing the information it contains.

The research, supported by FAPESP, was published in Physical Review B Letters. It was led by José Carlos Egues, a full professor at the São Carlos Institute of Physics at the University of São Paulo (IFSC-USP) in Brazil, and Yugui Yao of the School of Physics at the Beijing Institute of Technology in China. If experimentally confirmed, their method could simplify quantum computing by removing the need for extra qubits used in certain measurement techniques.

Why Measuring Quantum Information Is So Difficult

Conventional computers store information in bits that have one of two values, 0 or 1. These values can be checked repeatedly without changing the information. Quantum computers work differently because their qubits can exist in superpositions, meaning they can combine the states 0 and 1 in ways that are not possible with conventional bits.

This unusual ability is central to the potential power of quantum computing. However, it also creates a major vulnerability. Quantum states can be disrupted by interactions with their surroundings, including the instruments scientists use to examine them.

"In the conventional formulation of quantum mechanics, a projective measurement causes the system to assume one of the states associated with the measured quantity. Thus, if a qubit has been prepared in a certain superposition of 0 and 1, its direct measurement tends to destroy that superposition and, consequently, the information it contained," says Egues.

To minimize this problem, physicists developed techniques known as "quantum nondemolition measurements," or QND. Many of these approaches use an additional quantum system called an ancilla. Rather than measuring the original qubit directly, researchers transfer information to the ancilla and measure that auxiliary system instead.

The term "ancilla" comes from a Latin word meaning "slave" or "servant." In modern scientific usage, it describes something that performs a supporting function. Here, the ancilla acts as an intermediary, allowing researchers to obtain information while preserving the original qubit as much as possible.

Although effective, these procedures come with practical disadvantages. Scientists must prepare the auxiliary qubits, connect them to the qubits being studied, transfer information between them, and perform repeated measurements. These additional steps require time and equipment, and the complexity becomes increasingly significant as the number of qubits grows.

"Our strategy is different. Instead of transferring the information to an auxiliary qubit, we seek to obtain it from a signal produced by the qubit under study itself," says the researcher.

A Special Quantum Bit That Produces Its Own Signal

The proposed technique relies on the unusual properties of an Andreev qubit. Its name honors Soviet physicist Alexander Andreev (1939-2023), who identified a distinctive quantum process in 1964 that occurs where normal materials meet superconductors.

Superconductors are materials that can conduct electricity without electrical resistance under appropriate conditions. At the boundaries involving these materials, special quantum states known as "Andreev states" can form. In the system studied by the researchers, these states are confined to an extremely small region between two superconducting materials.

These quantum states can represent the basic values 0 and 1, along with superpositions of those values, making them suitable for storing quantum information.

"In the system under consideration, this region is a quantum dot - that is, a nanoscale structure in which electrons are spatially confined - formed in an indium arsenide [InAs] nanowire connected to superconducting materials on both sides. It's in this environment that the states characterizing the Andreev qubit are formed," Egues explains.

The key to the new approach is an electrical phenomenon called a "supercurrent." Unlike the current flowing through ordinary electrical conductors, a supercurrent moves without resistance.

The researchers found that when the Andreev qubit exists in a superposition, the supercurrent develops a component that oscillates over time. These fluctuations arise from quantum interference between the different states making up the superposition.

Crucially, the oscillations are not random. Their behavior contains information about the qubit's underlying quantum state, potentially allowing scientists to determine what that state is without directly measuring the qubit.

"It's precisely that property that we use to read the qubit. It's that supercurrent that lies at the heart of our proposal," the researcher points out.

How a Tiny Electrical Current Reveals Quantum Information

To understand how the technique works, it helps to consider how physicists describe a qubit's state mathematically.

One common representation is the "Bloch sphere," a geometric model in which every possible pure state of a single qubit corresponds to a point on the surface of a sphere. Two angles determine the location of that point.

The first angle describes how much each of the two basic states contributes to the superposition. The second describes their relative phase, which specifies how the quantum components are aligned with one another. Together, these two measurements provide the information needed to identify the qubit's state.

According to the new research, both pieces of information can be extracted from the supercurrent. Its steady component and the size of its oscillations reveal the relative contributions of the two basic states. Meanwhile, the timing of the oscillations provides information about their phase relationship.

"Our study showed that this information is encoded in the supercurrent itself. The supercurrent has a constant component and a fluctuating component. The value of the constant component and the amplitude of the oscillation reveal the proportion of each state (0 and 1) in the superposition, and the moment when that oscillation begins reveals the phase relationship between them. Thus, by measuring how the current varies over time, it's possible to reconstruct the initial state of the qubit," Egues explains.

This means the electrical current effectively carries a signature of the quantum information stored in the system. By observing how that signature changes over time, researchers could reconstruct the qubit's original state.

A Supercurrent That Oscillates Without Voltage

Another surprising feature of the proposed system is that the electrical current can oscillate without an externally applied voltage.

Ordinarily, producing an oscillating supercurrent in this type of superconducting system requires a voltage. This familiar phenomenon is called the "AC Josephson effect."

The researchers predict something different in their Andreev qubit. Here, the current's oscillations arise naturally from the qubit's own quantum evolution. No outside voltage is required to generate them.

"Our system generates a supercurrent that varies over time like an alternating current but without voltage. It's the very evolution of the qubit's state that produces that oscillation," says Egues.

This behavior is important because it transforms quantum activity occurring on an extraordinarily small scale into a detectable electrical signal. The current provides a connection between the microscopic world of quantum states and the larger physical systems scientists can measure.

That connection is what makes the proposed reading technique possible.

Detecting Quantum States With an Extremely Sensitive Sensor

The researchers envision placing the Andreev qubit inside a closed superconducting circuit, allowing its supercurrent to circulate continuously.

Next to the circuit would sit a SQUID (superconducting quantum interference device), a highly sensitive instrument capable of detecting extremely weak magnetic fields.

Whenever an electric current flows, it produces a magnetic field. Because the supercurrent in this system oscillates, the magnetic field it generates also changes over time.

The nearby SQUID could detect these tiny magnetic variations and use them to determine the behavior of the circulating current. Since the current contains information about the qubit's quantum state, the researchers could then reconstruct that state without measuring the qubit directly.

Egues compares the arrangement to two compasses that interact through a magnetic field.

"We can think of the qubit as one compass and the SQUID as another compass. The current in the circuit produces a magnetic field, and the SQUID 'senses' that field. Since the coupling between the two is very weak, the effect of the measuring instrument on the qubit tends to be very small."

The weak interaction between the sensor and the qubit is especially important. It offers a way to gather information while minimizing the disturbance that normally accompanies quantum measurements.

Reading Quantum Information With Almost No Disturbance

In quantum physics, observing a system is not necessarily a harmless process. A measuring instrument interacts with whatever it is attempting to measure, and that interaction can change the system's behavior.

This fundamental difficulty puzzled the earliest researchers studying quantum mechanics. It remains an unavoidable feature of quantum measurements today.

The proposed technique does not eliminate this effect entirely. The SQUID still interacts with the circuit, creating a small amount of feedback that can influence the qubit. However, the team's simulations suggest that this disturbance can be reduced to a level so small that it becomes effectively negligible.

"Our method doesn't eliminate the fundamental problem of quantum measurement. But it can approach the limit of a non-destructive readout and, at the same time, avoid the use of auxiliary qubits - the ancillas - employed in other non-destructive quantum measurement protocols," Egues notes.

The approach could also remove another complication found in some existing techniques. Because researchers would not need to restore the qubit to its original state after every measurement, they could make repeated observations as the state changes.

This would allow scientists to follow the qubit's quantum evolution over time, rather than obtaining information only through separate measurement and reset cycles.

Simulations Achieve Up to 99.85% Accuracy

To investigate whether the method could work outside ideal theoretical conditions, the researchers performed computer simulations designed to account for imperfections that would arise in real experiments.

These calculations included measurement noise, as well as the small amount of feedback introduced by the detection coil. The goal was to determine whether the qubit's state could still be reconstructed accurately despite these disturbances.

The results were encouraging. Depending on the number of measurements used, the simulations produced accuracies of 99.52%, 99.81%, and 99.85%.

According to the researchers, these values fall within the standard thresholds used in quantum error correction schemes, which are designed to manage errors in quantum information processing.

The findings suggest that the method could provide highly accurate quantum state reconstruction without the additional complexity of auxiliary qubits.

However, the technique has not yet been demonstrated experimentally. The results come entirely from theoretical analysis and computer simulations, so researchers still need to establish whether a physical device can achieve comparable performance.

Could This Make Quantum Computers Simpler?

Despite the lack of experimental confirmation, the researchers emphasize that their proposal does not depend on technologies that have yet to be invented.

The calculations use realistic properties drawn from existing quantum systems, and SQUID sensors are already established tools for detecting exceptionally weak magnetic fields and electric currents.

What remains to be built is the specific arrangement of components required to implement the proposed measurement technique.

"All the numbers we used are realistic. In principle, this can be achieved. In fact, we used parameters from existing systems in our calculations. What doesn't yet exist is that geometry, that setup that we proposed," he argues.

This makes the approach different from theoretical concepts that cannot be realized until scientists develop entirely new materials or manufacturing techniques. In principle, the necessary components already exist, although assembling them into the proposed configuration and demonstrating the predicted behavior remain important challenges.

The research offers two potentially significant advances.

From the standpoint of fundamental physics, it predicts that a qubit can produce an oscillating supercurrent resembling the AC Josephson effect, but without requiring an external voltage. Instead, the oscillations emerge from the qubit's own coherent quantum evolution.

From a technological perspective, the study shows how those oscillations could serve as a measurable signature of the qubit's internal state. By analyzing the electrical current, researchers could recover quantum information while causing very little disturbance to the system.

If future experiments confirm these predictions, the method could make reading Andreev qubits substantially simpler. Eliminating auxiliary qubits and repeated resets could reduce the equipment and procedures needed to retrieve quantum information.

More fundamentally, the work points toward an intriguing possibility: information hidden in the microscopic quantum world may be accessible through an electrical current produced by the quantum system itself, offering a promising new way to observe delicate quantum states without destroying them.

#10622. Microsoft says it now sucks less to use a Windows PC with 8GB of RAM

Using a computer with 8GB of RAM in 2026 is rough, to say the least, but as RAMageddon rages on, Microsoft says it is making improvements to using a Windows PC with less memory available.

In a blog post this week, Microsoft says that Windows PCs with 8GB of RAM are now 20% more responsive and 40% faster (in multitasking) in lab testing.

These are just a few of the updates Microsoft says it has made, with the company still working to get more out of memory compression and work with third-party developers to improve memory usage.

With RAMageddon’s effects only set to get worse in 2027 and problems thought to continue through 2030, this is all good news for end users. It’s also good seeing as Apple’s MacBook Neo triggered an avalanche of new 8GB Windows laptops designed to rival the lower price point.

#10620. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

The results through the main lens are perfectly fine, and I’m actually rather enjoying Samsung’s saturated colors as the Fall foliage cranks up, but I’m still constantly underwhelmed.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

To say that’s disappointing is an understatement, but if I can make one plea to Samsung, it’s to at least change the camera layout next time around. “Core identity” excuses aside, constantly needing to readjust my grip on the phone just to avoid taking a picture of my finger is frustrating, and having a new layout also opens the door to improvements on the table wobble and the whole Qi2 debacle. If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.

#10618. The first Snapdragon phone with Android Linux Terminal support is here

The Redmagic 12 Pro is the first Snapdragon-based Android phone to support the Linux Terminal introduced in Android 16 for Pixel phones.

Android power users who want a new Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6-powered phone like the Redmagic 12 Pro now have another reason to be excited about the latest Qualcomm processors: the Android Linux Terminal.

Thanks to tipster Dylan H, who wrote in asking about compatibility. After enabling the Android Linux Terminal and running a few commands to check compatibility, I found out that the Redmagic 12 Pro does, indeed, support the Linux Terminal app. I’ve written Qualcomm asking about wider Snapdragon support for Linux Terminal and will update this article if I receive a response.

Google introduced the Terminal app as a power user feature in the Android 16 update for Pixel phones, and Samsung added support in One UI 8.5 for Exynos-powered Galaxy S26 models. So far, however, Snapdragon phones have not appeared on the compatibility list.

this is super cool! i guess my first question is if it is subject to the Android 32 subprocess limit that effectively cripples the terminal on Googlebooks.

Linux Terminal support has been slowly building on Google platforms. Googlebooks sort of support it, and now it appears that more Android phones will support it as well. The implementation on the Redmagic 12 Pro seems slightly different from what’s on Exynos-powered Samsung phones, as you cannot adjust the disk size (it’s locked at 132GB available), but you can adjust the amount of memory available for the terminal to use.

By default, Terminal has access to 1GB of memory, although the Redmagic 12 Pro lets you increase that to 10.6GB. You can also adjust the display resolution and enable Keep Awake in advanced settings. The Redmagic 12 Pro also supports the GUI feature, so programs like GIMP should work if you can get them installed (I kept running into 404 errors using the standard installation command).

The Redmagic 12 Pro goes up for preorder on November 12 and includes several freebies and a launch discount before November 17. See our full review to find out if it’s right for you.

#10614. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

#10613. How ‘key person risk’ derails companies—and poses problems for Nvidia amid the Jensen Huang scandal

When companies put all their faith and trust in one powerful leader, backup plans when things go south are hard to come by. Just ask Nvidia. The world’s second most valuable company (down to the third most valuable as of this writing) is in the throes of a major PR scandal that hasn’t just shaken trust in the tech company’s leader, but in the entire entity.

Over the weekend, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang admitted to having an extramarital affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang (no relation to Jensen), an executive from Singapore-based tech company Megaspeed. Nvidia announced it would launch an independent investigation into the CEO’s relationship over reports that he was the “target of a foreign intelligence effort.”

As the company grapples with the controversy and its potential legal ramifications, concerns over the leader’s judgment loom large, especially given he’s been one of AI’s most vocal advocates, and as China and the U.S. continue to battle it out to keep pace with one another in the AI race. While Nvidia’s stock was trending down ahead of the midterm elections, since the revelation of Huang’s affair it has plummeted, now sitting 22% below its October peak.

Turns out, there’s a phenomenon that explains the dangers of putting all your eggs in the basket of a single, all-powerful leader.

What is Key Man (or Person) Risk?

The ordeal is a prime example of something called key man (or person) risk, which refers to when a company leans too heavily on one leader, making that leader’s actions—their successes and failures—crucial for the entire company’s survival. That seems to be precisely the case for Nvidia, where tech mastermind Huang has become not just an asset but seemingly irreplaceable. That’s especially true because there is no clear successor for the powerful CEO.

As an analyst at Bernstein explained to Reuters, “Nvidia is not an ordinary company, and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he is Nvidia” and “clearly sets the long-term strategy and direction.”

Of course, all businesses have leaders. But according to Wright People PR, companies should seek to mitigate key person risk by cross-training employees and focusing on succession planning and team collaboration. 

Why the risk is growing

Still, as a handful of tech leaders become more and more powerful, key person risk seems to be accelerating. Dan Ives, partner and senior managing director of Yorkville Ives & Co., told CNN that it “has never been more of a blessing and a curse” than it is right now. 

“We’ve always had powerful CEOs but never in history have we been at a point where such an important industry is run by people you can count on two hands,” Ives explained.

In other words, it’s not just Huang and Nvidia. Many of the world’s Big Tech companies have similarly given outsize power to their leaders—Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg, Anthropic’s Dario Amodei, Tesla and Space X’s Elon Musk, to name a few.

The phenomenon can also show up in politics, as it recently has following last week’s blue tsunami in the U.S. midterms: GOP leaders are increasingly backing away from President Donald Trump as the Democrats’ blowout win highlights just how disillusioned they are with the policies of a Republican party that’s been remade in his image over the last decade.

As for Nvidia, the company is busy trying to downplay the controversy while surveying the damage, stating in a November 8 press release: “The Board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders,” adding it “has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised.”

#10611. A viral TikTok trend is turning ordinary photos into something unexpectedly emotional

Looking back through old photographs often happens in a family living room, turning the pages of a loved one’s photo albums. But on TikTok, the nostalgic ritual is its own trend.

Users are showcasing their life progress by looking back to their childhood, comparing their baby photos to recent ones. While the captions vary across videos, it’s the music and lyrics that bring them all together: Pink Floyd’s “Time.”

The trend uses the photo-carousel feature on TikTok as a template: In the first photo, with the user at a young age, the lyric “The sun is the same, in a relative way” is highlighted. The recent image continues with “but you are older.”

For many people, the trend appears to be a way to show that despite the passage of time, their key attributes remain the same. Take content creator Eugenia McQuarrie, who posted a photo of herself at a beauty salon with rollers in her hair. The photo is followed by a present-day image of McQuarrie wearing hair rollers as an adult.

One viewer commented, “diva since day one.”

Another user had a similar take on the trend, first posting a photo of himself as a kid wearing a toy firefighter hat, then cutting to his grown-up self in a real-life firefighter uniform.

According to trend-tracking agency Masterhooks, in the span of weeks this particular trend has amassed nearly 9 million views.

Part of the appeal is the versatility to make the trend one’s own, keeping the Pink Floyd soundtrack but posting unique videos and edits.

One user shows a video of her dancing in her living room as a child, then cuts to her dancing at a party as an adult. “Still the same vibe, how cute,” one viewer commented.

For others, nostalgia didn’t reach as far back as most. Ballet content creator Katelyn Power posted a video of her dancing ballet four years ago, then cut to her most recent performance of the same dance.

One viewer commented, “Pink Floyd and ballet? I am in heaven.”

#10609. Introducing M-suite: Fast Company’s exclusive community for moms in senior leadership

What is M-suite? 

M-suite is a community and content series focused on women navigating senior-level careers and motherhood. We launched in late September of 2026.  

The community’s home base is our WhatsApp chat, designed to fit into the busy lives of our members. Membership also includes a members-only newsletter, a private LinkedIn group, and virtual events where members can connect and learn from one another.  

M-suite hosts regular virtual events, plus pop-up in-person gatherings in M-suite hubs and at events that are already frequented by our members. Our events are limited to M-suite members only.  

M-suite is also a content series within Fast Company. We cover news, ideas, and workplace issues shaping the lives and careers of working mothers. Our M-suite section is not limited to M-suite members because we think these issues are bigger than motherhood alone. Non-moms, colleagues, managers, and leaders need to be part of the conversation, too. 

What M-suite isn’t 

M-suite isn’t a forum for debating birth methods, breastfeeding, or parenting styles. We support all approaches to motherhood and want to keep the conversation focused on the intersection of motherhood and ambitious careers.

#10606. Social Security 2027: Benefits checks could get ‘Bernie Bump’ after midterms blue wave. Here’s by how much

One of the little talked about potential effects of the blue tsunami, which swept Democrats into Congress in the midterm elections, is if and how it will impact the country’s ailing Social Security system.

A recent report from the Social Security trustees estimates the retirement trust fund will run out of money and become insolvent as early as the fourth quarter of 2032, in about six years—which, if not addressed, would automatically trigger a cut in benefits to tens of millions of Americans.

Vermont Senator Bernie Sanders has been trying to rally his colleagues to come up with a way to avoid that crisis. When the 120th Congress reconvenes in January, Democrats will gain control of a number of committees, and Sanders, an independent who caucuses with the Democrats, expects to head Social Security.

“As the next chair of the Social Security Subcommittee, a top priority will be to expand Social Security benefits by at least $2,400 a year & extend Social Security’s solvency for generations,” Sanders posted on X. “How? By scrapping the cap & demanding that the wealthy pay their fair share of taxes,” he added.

Under Sanders’s previous “Social Security Expansion Act” proposal, Social Security recipients would receive an extra $200 per month—a so-called Bernie Bump.

That bump would not only raise benefits but also expand the annual cost-of-living adjustments (COLA), which are meant to keep pace with inflation and will increase payments by 3.6% in 2027.

“To pay for it, the earnings cap would be removed and include capital gains and dividends,” Robert Laura, a financial planner and founder of the Retirement Coaches Association, explained to Fast Company. “For 2027, earnings over $192,300 aren’t taxed for Social Security at all, implying the wealthiest Americans aren’t paying their fair share.”

However, according to Laura, completely scrapping the cap isn’t likely. But a more aggressive phased approach could be possible. Laura says a more realistic scenario would be a plan that moves the cap closer to $500,000 over three to five years—which wouldn’t solve Social Security’s problems, but could be part of a larger, multifaceted overhaul.

“The Bernie Bump is alive and well, but I wouldn’t make any plans to spend that extra money just yet,” Laura tells Fast Company.

That’s also because although Democrats will control both chambers of Congress, a majority isn’t the same as 60 votes in the Senate—and any bill would still need the president’s signature.

Some 71 million Americans—54 million retired workers and 9 million of their survivors and dependents—receive monthly Social Security benefits.

#10603. Open enrollment is squeezing small businesses. Nearly 70% want outside help

With businesses now overseeing open enrollment for their workers, many employers admit they’re struggling to find ways to finance and organize increasingly complex benefit plans. Yet rather than decide to simply cut those programs as too costly and laborious to continue, a majority of small business owners are considering turning to outside experts’ help to make them more effective.

Employers’ commitment to benefits, and accompanying awareness that their plans are getting too difficult to operate alone, were two major findings in a recent survey of 759 small businesses by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce (USCC) and JustWorks, an HR tech company. Nearly 70% of participating entrepreneurs said they recognize they need help in running their programs, and “wish they had outside” support in structuring, operating, and budgeting them. That desire for expert third-party assistance stood at 75% among Gen Z and millennial owners, with 64% and 54% of Gen Xers and boomers considering the idea, respectively.

Little wonder so many entrepreneurs are considering outside help in orchestrating benefits programs—especially with annual open enrollment activity underway. In addition to managing the often wide array of options included in many company plans, business owners are also forced to find new budgeting adjustments to fund their rising costs.

Health care insurance is the main example of that. While premium increases to all companies this year are slated to surpass 9%, they’re hitting small businesses particularly hard.

Despite rising costs, owners expand benefit coverage

According to a recent report by the payroll services platform Gusto, employee medical costs to entrepreneurs have surged by 31% since 2022, and now represent $8,743 per worker. Entrepreneurs with staff of two to five people have endured 35% hikes in premiums over the same period, and now pay about 6% more than businesses with 25 to 50 employees.

Yet rather than pull back or eliminate benefit programs, many small business owners have found a variety of hacks to not only maintain their plans, but expand them to more staff members.

Crafty budgeting and financing decisions allowed entrepreneurs surveyed by the USCC to increase health insurance coverage from 41% of workers in the second quarter of 2025 to 46% in Q3 this year. Similarly, paid vacation days increased from 30% to 40% over the same period, while dental and vision plans expanded from 21% to 31% of staff. Retirement programs rose from 22% to 27% of employees participating.

The difficulty of finding ways to free up funds to pay for benefits programs—and the hard decisions necessary to redirect limited finances to staff plans—is one reason why a majority of business owners are considering asking for outside help to manage and operate programs.

Another motive is that many entrepreneurs are interested in using efficiencies gained from expert assistance to expand their support to workers even more.

“I would love to offer programs that help with tuition/student loans, support with homebuying/child college savings, and offset childcare or elderly care costs,” Joanne Sanders, a survey participant and president of Ewise Marketing and Communications in Georgia, told USCC pollsters.

Benefits pay off in better staff productivity and retention

There’s more than altruism behind those generous ambitions. Studies have shown that employee productivity, well-being, and loyalty all tend to strengthen at businesses offering benefit plans.

According to recent data from Lincoln Financial, 74% of “employers who offer voluntary benefits cite positive effects on recruitment, retention, and performance.” That figure rises to 86% at companies providing supplemental accident, critical illness, and hospital indemnity insurance options that often save employees from financial ruin from health calamities.

Despite the difficulties of financing and operating benefit plans, most USCC respondents said they believe what they provide is effective in improving staff lives, finances, and loyalty. Around 80% of small business owners reported their “employees are satisfied with the current offering,” and 57% said staffers “rate their total compensation package as good.”

But with packages growing more complex and costly every year, it’s not surprising most small business owners want outside help to ensure they can keep offering benefits that everyone can afford.

“Health insurance premiums climb nearly every renewal cycle, often outpacing general inflation,” Stevie Straka, vice president of people at the Ann Arbor, Michigan-based cybersecurity company Blumira, said in a report on the USCC survey. “(T)hat forces a tough annual trade-off between absorbing rising costs, shifting more of the burden to employees, or scaling back coverage.”

Those difficult decisions are why more employees now involved in or preparing for open enrollment may find their bosses getting expert help organizing and managing workplace benefit programs—and keep them running well into the future.

—Bruce Crumley

#10601. Google’s latest smart home products make the perfect gifts, and they’re on sale at Best Buy

Google Home is one of the most user-friendly smart home ecosystems, and Google has built out an extensive collection of cameras, speakers, and displays. That positions them as great gifts to expand someone’s smart home.

It’s pretty common to get one or two smart home devices and call it a day, but ecosystems like Google Home begin to come alive when there are several devices working in harmony with each other. For instance, you can start out with the Google Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen), as many do. It makes A/C and heat controls easy, and even saves you money along the way.

But adding something like the Google Home Speaker with Gemini built-in means you get even more control of your home’s indoor environment. You could go a step further and add a Google Nest Cam (indoor or outdoor) or a Google Nest Doorbell, whether wired or battery. It’s a bit pricier, but I love the Google Nest Cam with Floodlight as a smart home gift option for someone, since it provides more utility.

In any case, adding a couple more foundational pieces to your smart home makes it more useful. And as Google adds features with Gemini for Home, users get access to smart alerts and notifications that those cameras didn’t provide a year or two ago, all within the Google Home app.

If you’re gifting for someone who doesn’t have the beginnings of a smart home setup, Google’s Nest products are a good start. You could start with the Google Home Speaker, depending on their needs. If they like listening to music and want a smart home assistant at the ready, Google’s latest speaker delivers.

The Google Home Speaker has a 360-degree speaker that throws audio in every direction, filling the room. It controls other devices as that smart home fills out, and it can even act as part of a stereo setup for the Google TV Streamer.

Right now, the Google Home Speaker is at its lowest price on Best Buy, sitting at $79.99 – $20 off. It comes in some great colorways, too, like Berry and Jade. Of course, there’s Porcelain and Hazel for more toned-down looks.

Another great smart home starter gift is the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) or Nest Thermostat. The difference between the two comes down to look and capabilities, but both work very well.

The Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) works with the majority of HVAC systems and converts them into one manageable system through the Google Home app. Over time, it learns your heating and cooling habits to form better schedules that save you money and stress.

Right now, the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th) gen is down to $219 from $279 at Best Buy – $60 off. That’s the best price we’ve seen from the store, and it’s a good deal overall.

Best Buy is also offering a couple of bundles if you want to go further. You can add Nest Cams to your cart with the Nest Learning Thermostat for $100 off the order. If you want to bundle a couple of items into a gift for someone –or yourself – that’s the way to go.

Of course, you can’t go wrong with the Nest Doorbell cameras (wired or battery). Go for the battery edition if you’re limited on wiring options or want to use it at an apartment. Wired is the better choice for homes, since it makes 24/7 recording possible for more security.

You can also check out Best Buy’s other offers on Google’s smart home products. It might be worth bookmarking as deals appear during Black Friday.

#10600. In an age of mistrust, communicating more isn’t enough. Influence comes from building networks

Serbian President Aleksandar Vučić’s loss in recent elections was startling for a number of reasons. First, during his 14-year reign, Vučić had come to rule the country with an iron hand. Second was the scale: The opposition Student List candidates won 52.65% of the vote, while Vučić’s United Serbia party took only 39.2%.

But even more important, which you couldn’t see from all the big crowds and headlines, is what my friend the Serbian activist Srdja Popović calls the student’s “analog strategy.” Named Student in Every Village, it eschewed high-tech targeting databases and AI-generated graphics for old-fashioned door-to-door campaigning. 

This is part of a larger phenomenon that leaders need to recognize and act upon. As trust in institutions has diminished, traditional channels, such as carefully crafted press releases, slick promotions, and announcements from behind a podium are increasingly ineffective. Today, organizations need to build more visceral, on-the-ground networks to gain traction.  

The “Leaderless Revolutions”

In the early 2000s and 2010s, a series of popular revolutions brought down powerful authoritarian regimes. First came the color revolutions, which overthrew the remnants of communist dictatorships in Serbia, Georgia, and Ukraine. Then the uprisings of the Arab Spring sprang up across the Middle East, bringing similar pressure on hard-line despots.

Many referred to these as “leaderless revolutions” because they had no charismatic figurehead (like a Gandhi, Martin Luther King Jr., or Mandela). It seemed as if the streets just spontaneously filled with self-organizing networks of protesters, empowered by technology, to demand change. The brittle 20th-century-style regimes hardly knew what hit them. 

In 2013, political scientist Moisés Naím published The End of Power, in which he argued that because of increasing mobility and technological change, along with declining poverty, the power of institutions was diminishing. Power hadn’t ended exactly but, as he put it, power was becoming “easier to gain but harder to use or keep.”

While there was some truth to all of this, much of it was a mirage. I was there, in Kyiv, Ukraine, during the Orange Revolution in 2004, and it was clear even then that the protests were designed and planned by well-trained activists. Yes, technology played a part. Online message boards and text messaging were critical communication tools, but none of it just happened on its own. 

As Srdja recently put it to me, “There are two kinds of movements: spontaneous and successful.” Something else was going on.  

Saddleback’s Secret

When Rick Warren first arrived in Orange County, California, in 1979, he saw the opportunity to build a new kind of church. He had spent three months going door-to-door and found that while many residents identified themselves as Christians, they found church services boring and irrelevant. So he began to tailor his services and programs to meet their needs.

Today, his Saddleback Church is one of the largest congregations in the world, with 20,000 people attending services every week. Yet looks can be deceiving. What makes Warren such a powerful force isn’t those massive weekend events, but the thousands of small prayer groups that meet during the week.

The heart of the Saddleback movement is the groups of six to eight people who meet each week, build strong ties, and support each other in matters of faith, family, and career. It is the strong bonds, combined with the loose connections between those small groups, that give Saddleback its combination of massive reach and internal coherence.

My experiences in the Orange Revolution were similar. Yes, there were enormous crowds, but we largely operated in small groups, sometimes coordinating with other small groups, going online and texting to give and receive information. Later, as I got to know and work more closely with Srdja, I learned that these movements were designed that way—in no way leaderless, but very much decentralized. 

As I wrote in Cascades, transformational change is driven by small groups, loosely connected, but united by a common purpose. Decentralization is not the absence of leadership. It is a different form of leadership. The job of a leader is no longer to plan and organize tasks but to help those groups connect and inspire them with a sense of shared values and shared mission.  

The Disinformation Wars

When the COVID-19 pandemic hit in 2020, Dr. Anthony Fauci became a household name. Signaling institutional authority, he stood behind a podium, wore a white lab coat, and talked passionately about the “science” of the disease and its spread. He asked people to change their behaviors, wear masks, and distance themselves from their neighbors.

Yet many didn’t trust him. He felt distant, and they were skeptical that someone who felt so remote from their lives could have their best interests at heart. It was as if Fauci were a minister at the pulpit, but congregants never made it to the service. They were still in their prayer groups, reading from an entirely different hymn book, written by other people they saw in white lab coats just like Fauci’s on social media and YouTube but saying very different things. 

Recently, a team of researchers at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology looked into how we share information—and misinformation—in our little “prayer groups.” What they found was troubling. Their model suggests that when we’re surrounded by people who think like us, we share information more freely because we don’t expect to be challenged. We’re also less likely to check our facts, because we know that those we’re sharing with are less likely to inspect the facts themselves.

In Naím’s 2023 follow-up to his earlier book, The Revenge of Power, he showed how many of those same players that had been disrupted in earlier revolutions, like autocrats, corporations, and criminal networks, adapted the same tools that the movements used, such as the internet and social media, to discredit their opponents, reconstitute their power, and disrupt institutions. 

That’s what leaders are struggling with today. The old institutional channels are clogged. Messages must compete with myriad sources of information, ranging from good-faith institutions to nefarious players, rumor, and innuendo, before they can reach each of those small groups.

All change is local, now more than ever

In the 1950s, sociologist William Whyte made an observation about the spread of air conditioners. Back then, units were installed in windows, and he observed that these didn’t propagate evenly, but in clusters from building to building. People weren’t buying air conditioners after just hearing about them. They bought one after visiting a neighbor who had one running on a hot day.

Yet this was also the beginning of the era of mass communication. New electronic media, like television and later the internet, were measurable in ways that traditional channels were not. Increasingly sophisticated marketers divided audiences, tracked metrics, and built complicated mathematical models in an effort to make persuasion more predictable and accountable.

Yet the human brain never changed. We evolved to manage relatively small social networks that max out at about 150 people, a phenomenon known as Dunbar’s number. Beyond that, our capacity for trust diminishes greatly. Decades of research have shown that we rarely interact with information directly, but rather filter it through our peer networks. 

What recent events in Serbia, Hungary, and other places have shown is that this process can be countered, but as General Stanley McChrystal has put it, “It takes a network to defeat a network.” While it is true that institutions, nation-states, and oligarchs today have the capacity to broadcast messages that permeate every facet of our lives, it is becoming increasingly clear that people are no longer listening. 

It’s no longer enough to communicate a vision to shape opinions. Leaders today must learn to shape networks.
