The open-source project reportedly violates Synaptics' patents, but its creator begs to differ.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A hobbyist building on open-source driver for DisplayLink hardware received a cease-and-desist from Synaptics, resulting in a lengthy written argument and a threat to sue from the company. According to Phoronix, the Vino driver was “clean-room reverse-engineered” and partly developed using AI and written in Rust. However, even though the person behind the open-source driver, Mike Lothian, did not break the copyright of the code behind the driver, Synaptics argued that his work violates its patent on the DisplayLink technology.

The story began in July 2026, when Lothian reached out to Łukasz Spintzyk, a software engineer working at Synaptics, to help solve a few bugs he’s been encountering. However, Michal Lukaszek, the Strategic Engineering Partnerships Lead at the company, replied a couple of weeks later, saying that the company is concerned that Lothian is attempting to implement the DisplayLink protocol itself, which is a violation of its patents. This resulted in a cease-and-desist letter from Synaptics’ legal department asking him to take down the Vino GitHub repo, among other things.

Synaptics claims that Lothian’s work infringes on two patents: GB2484736 and GB2485613. The former, called Image generation, is about sending screen updates over a general-purpose data network like USB or Ethernet using a specific architecture and compression method, while the latter, called Display control device, details hardware that works with compressed data and only decompresses it as needed. Lothian responded, asking for complete details as to how his work infringes on its patents, but received no response, prompting him to follow up three weeks later.

This was when Synaptics decided to escalate, telling him that it would be billing Lothian “to recoup the cost and effort required to create such documentation of your infringing acts.” Andrew Bateman, the company’s Assistant General Counsel, also added, “By open sourcing our DisplayLink technology for whatever personal reasons you may have as a ‘hobbyist,’ you will be single-handedly eviscerating DisplayLink's competitive advantage in this crowded market by educating our competitors and other third parties as to how we implement our proprietary technology, which we have gone to great lengths and great expense to prevent.”

“If you'd like to notify the media or otherwise publicize our response, I encourage you to do so. We welcome any and all free publicity to let the world (in particular, our competitors and our shareholders) know that Synaptics aggressively defends and protects its DisplayLink IP from all threats of infringement.”

Lothian eventually took the GitHub repo containing the Vino driver offline, but it seems that the fight isn’t over yet. The hobbyist claims that Synaptics’ patents are invalid, saying, “In both cases the independent claims as filed contained no network or transport limitation. The claims were granted only after being amended to add delivery of the display data over a ‘general-purpose data network’ (and, for GB2485613, a ‘transport protocol’ with an ‘unpackaging’ step). That added feature was not new.”

He further argues that even though the company says that DisplayLink isn’t part of the Open Invention Network, Synaptics itself is an OIN licensee. Some say that an OIN license requires that any subsidiary acquired by the original licensee and all its patents would also fall under the company’s OIN license, although Synaptics apparently disagrees with this.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Despite the escalation, Lothian says that he remains open to discussing a sensible resolution. But unless the two parties come to an agreement, it looks like they’re getting ready for a legal face-off. You can see the complete email thread between Lothian and Synaptics in this Linux Kernel Mailing List page and the attached email thread (PDF).

Lothian said that he used a Claude subscription to help out with his cleanroom reverse-engineering efforts, revealing the headache that advanced AI tools pose for tech giants. A user has created a free Adobe Creative Suite clone in Rust using Claude, and was also reportedly working on an open-source office suite and Autodesk tools. Aside from that, we’ve also seen a lot of reverse-engineered games running on platforms they’re not designed for, forcing Anthropic to enforce some limitations on Opus 5.5. There was also concern that the widespread use of AI in chipmaking could result in the inadvertent spread of a copied design or an infringed patent across thousands of chips before anyone catches it.

#10595. ‘They don’t understand hackers’: Cybersecurity pros explain why everyone panicking about AI-powered hacks is wrong

As head of counter-adversary operations at CrowdStrike, Adam Meyers has a front-row seat to the threats facing companies. Yet even for him, a recent trip to South Korea was out of the ordinary. As he was preparing to sleep on the flight from the United States, Meyers received a message from colleagues informing him that six Korean banks had been hacked and that he would likely face questions about the incident upon landing.

By the time his flight touched down, his team at CrowdStrike had pinpointed the server used by the attacker, recovered records of their activity, and even identified a suspect. The alleged hacker had inadvertently left personal information behind while using AI tools to conduct the attacks.

The incident, which CrowdStrike subsequently investigated, involved Artex, an open-source AI-powered penetration-testing tool, alongside LLMs from a variety of providers. The scale and speed of the attack illustrate why artificial intelligence has fueled growing fears about the future of cybersecurity.

But Meyers isn’t panicking. “I thrive on chaos,” he says. And in a conversation with Fast Company at the company’s Fal.Con conference in Barcelona, he argues that much of the alarm surrounding AI-powered hacking is misplaced. “Fundamentally, this is nothing new. It’s just speed and volume,” he says. “AI hasn’t come up with new exploit paths or new vulnerability classes, right? It’s all the same stuff we’re already aware of.”

That doesn’t mean the technology is harmless. But Meyers believes the cybersecurity industry risks confusing the capabilities of increasingly sophisticated AI models with the actual threat they pose to organizations.

He’s not alone. Ciaran Martin, the former head of the U.K.’s National Cyber Security Centre, argues that much of the discussion around AI-enabled hacking has become detached from the realities of cybercrime. “To some extent, we’ve lost our mind in 2026 because of the extent of the capabilities,” he says.

Martin believes companies developing frontier AI models have focused heavily on what their systems can theoretically accomplish without paying enough attention to the motivations and constraints of actual attackers. “They don’t understand threat actors. They don’t understand hackers. They don’t understand the economics and the sociology and the politics of hacking,” he says.

The result, Martin suggests, is an industry increasingly alarmed by suspicious activity, even as the growing volume of automated attacks doesn’t necessarily translate into more successful compromises. That disconnect is putting pressure on security teams, which must sift through an ever-growing number of potential threats.

Eerke Boiten, a cybersecurity professor at De Montfort University in the U.K., sees a similar challenge. AI is accelerating the discovery of vulnerabilities, he explains, but the ability to exploit them isn’t necessarily advancing at the same pace. That leaves already overwhelmed security teams struggling to determine which threats warrant their attention. There is, however, a potential upside: The same AI capabilities that help attackers identify vulnerabilities could also help defenders fix them and assess how difficult they are to exploit.

When every vulnerability becomes an emergency

For Meyers, the challenge underscores a long-standing problem with how organizations assess cybersecurity risks. He argues that the industry has become too reliant on the Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, which assigns numerical scores to security flaws on a scale of one to 10. PHP2Shell, a pair of vulnerabilities in the widely used PHP programming language that drew widespread attention, each received a score of 9.2.

Meyers says CrowdStrike examined the issue before its public disclosure and concluded that exploiting the vulnerabilities would be far from straightforward. Woodward agrees, noting that doing so required exceptionally precise timing and chaining vulnerabilities together. Still, the flaws weren’t merely theoretical: The Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, part of the U.S. Department of Homeland Security, added both to its catalog of known exploited vulnerabilities on October 26, calling for urgent action.

Woodward maintains that CVSS remains useful because it accounts for the potential impact of an attack. Meyers, however, argues that severity scores shouldn’t be the sole factor determining how defenders respond. Instead, organizations should also consider evidence of attacks taking place in the wild. “I like to think about things in terms of what’s being actively exploited,” he says.

Otherwise, the industry risks creating a boy-who-cried-wolf scenario. “The more people get burned out, the more they’re going to make mistakes,” Meyers warns.

Boiten, for his part, sees reason for optimism. He believes that the collaboration underpinning vulnerability identification could eventually extend to fixing flaws, with AI-assisted coding potentially reducing the number introduced in the first place. That’s a notable shift in his thinking. Just a year ago, he expected the rise of vibe coding to produce even more vulnerabilities.

Amid the growing focus on AI, however, it’s easy to overlook one of cybersecurity’s most persistent vulnerabilities: humans. For all the technological advances transforming cybercrime, attackers still frequently gain access by exploiting human weaknesses.

Who benefits from the AI panic?

So why has AI captured so much attention? Meyers believes the companies developing the technology have played a significant role. “There’s a lot of fearmongering going on, and I think the frontier labs have been stoking that fire for their own benefit,” he says.

Meyers suggests that frightening predictions about AI capabilities could serve the commercial and regulatory interests of frontier AI companies, particularly as they consider going public. By emphasizing the potentially catastrophic consequences of their own technology, these companies may also be reinforcing perceptions of their power and importance.

The danger isn’t simply that organizations might devote too many resources to the wrong threats. It’s that a steady drumbeat of warnings could make them less responsive when a truly serious threat emerges.

“I think we need to be careful not to overhype it or we’ll find people stop listening, and then there will be something they really need to take care of,” says Woodward.

#10594. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#10593. This former Facebook employee wants to show AI in all its glory—and all its flaws

Americans, in case you hadn’t heard, are growing weary of AI.

Just one in nine Americans supports AI data center construction in their community, according to a poll from the University of Massachusetts Amherst. A Pew Research Center survey found that 67% have little or no confidence in the federal government’s ability to regulate artificial intelligence effectively. And perhaps most tellingly, 54% of Americans say they’re simply tired of hearing about AI, according to a Talker Research survey. Amid the nonstop barrage of headlines about AI’s risks and hype, it’s easy to lose sight of what the tech is actually accomplishing.

Dave Goldblatt, a product engineer at Mission Control AI and former Facebook employee, wants to change that.

He’s the creator of Because of AI, an independent, unfunded research database that tracks documented cases in which AI has delivered measurable benefits in fields ranging from medicine and education to transportation and accessibility. Rather than tout the tech’s theoretical potential, the site focuses on real-world outcomes, evaluating each case based on the strength of its evidence, how directly the results can be attributed to AI, and when the impact occurred.

Crucially, the database also documents cases where AI has not delivered on its promise.

‘It can be a great thing, or it could be a terrible thing’

Goldblatt’s inspiration came from a subscriber chat for The Exponential View, a newsletter by the British tech entrepreneur Azeem Azhar. When another chat member asked whether anyone had compiled a dashboard of measurable improvements to human life attributable to AI, Goldblatt turned to Claude to assemble a list. The response was enthusiastic enough that he decided to build an entire website around the idea. (It took him just four hours.)

Since launching on October 23, Because of AI has operated largely autonomously; AI tools continually update its database. The site is attracting between 200 and 300 visitors a day, according to Goldblatt.

“It’s automated now, so I’m just gonna have my machines continue to update, continue to monitor, and slowly build up the audience,” Goldblatt tells Fast Company.

The site organizes its findings into three categories: “Because of AI,” “Not Because,” and “Not Yet.” Among its documented successes is an AI-assisted tuberculosis screening program in Ethiopia, which identified 1,660 cases between January 2025 and March 2026 in settings where no radiologists were available to interpret X-rays.

Not every deployment has been so successful. An AI-enabled stethoscope introduced at 205 National Health Service general practices in the U.K., for instance, failed to significantly improve heart failure detection after 12 months.

But the database also makes a point to say that a disappointing outcome doesn’t necessarily mean the AI is ineffective. Poor implementation, inadequate training, or difficulties integrating AI into existing workflows can all undermine its effectiveness.

“Everyone who is involved in tech and works in AI knows that it is tremendously powerful, and, of course, like anything that’s tremendously powerful, it can be a great thing, or it could be a terrible thing,” Goldblatt says. “At the very least, we can establish some sort of a ground truth inasmuch as ground truth exists anymore.”

A second project

Goldblatt is applying a similar approach to another project, albeit one with a more pointed focus. His second website, Pundit Claims, scrutinizes public statements by the prominent AI critics Gary Marcus and Ed Zitron, evaluating whether the available evidence supports their assertions.

Goldblatt establishes the criteria for evaluating claims, while AI models handle the drafting and fact-checking. Models from Anthropic and OpenAI review the findings before a verdict is published.

Unlike Because of AI, which is intended as a broad resource for understanding the impact of large language models, Pundit Claims is aimed at readers of Marcus and Zitron, as well as journalists and investors trying to assess how much weight to give the critics’ arguments.

Goldblatt says both projects serve the same fundamental purpose: “Take claims about AI, and then check them against the record.”

#10592. Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

#10589. The Rubik’s Cube just got its first major redesign in 50 years

The Rubik’s Cube is perhaps one of the most universally recognizable objects in the modern world. It’s the world’s best-selling toy ever, having sold more than 500 million units. It’s appeared on The Simpsons, in two Spider-Man films, and in Disney’s animated hit WALL-E. It’s been immortalized in the Museum of Modern Art and turned into a Google Doodle. And it just got its first major redesign in more than 50 years. 

The new 3×3 cube has gotten a top-to-bottom makeover, from the strength of its internal springs to the shapes of its tiles and the texture of its surface. As part of the product launch, the Rubik’s team has also invested in a redesigned webpage that includes an illustrated, eight-step guide to solving the cube, a quiz to help players find the “solve style” that works best for them, and a series of YouTube videos produced in-house that explain each style. 

Rubik’s (a brand that encompasses the iconic 3×3 cube as well as several other tactile puzzles) was acquired by the toy company Spin Master in 2021. Since then, Spin Master has been on a mission to modernize Rubik’s products and introduce them to new audiences.

In a moment when retro tech is making a comeback and parents are looking for creative ways to entertain their kids without screens, Spin Master wants to position Rubik’s as the analog brand that makes problem-solving skills fun.

The world’s most famous toy

Ernö Rubik never set out to create a toy. The Hungarian architect and design professor was instead trying to invent a 3D object that had movement on all axes. It was the mid-’70s, and Rubik wanted a fun task to give to his students at the Budapest College of Applied Arts. After making his first prototype out of wood, Rubik realized that he had created something special. When he added colors to the sides of the object and gave it a spin, it became clear that he’d stumbled on a puzzle. 

“It actually took him about a month to solve his first Rubik’s Cube,” says George Scholey, an associate brand manager at Spin Master and holder of multiple Rubik’s Cube-related world records (including most cubes solved while running a marathon). “That’s completely fair enough, by the way, because if you were to try and work out a Rubik’s Cube on your own for your first-ever solve, it isn’t that easy.”

In fact, Scholey says, the Rubik’s Cube can be arranged into more than 43 quintillon different formations—essentially, an infinite number of possibilities. Every time the toy is scrambled, there’s a strong possibility that the user is seeing a completely unique cube. The puzzle’s unbelievable variety, paired with its simple form, is perhaps what catapulted the Rubik’s Cube to international fame. 

“The fundamentals of the Rubik’s Cube can teach you so much—it’s not just about a party trick,”  Scholey says. “This is about learning how to solve something, learning how to push through something. I think it’s really powerful.”

Rubik launched the cube as a product in Hungary before expanding it worldwide. By the mid-’80s, it was a hit. It’s difficult to overstate the pop cultural oeuvre and staying power of the Rubik’s Cube. Multiple current artists use it as their sole medium. In 2022, the hashtag #rubikscube had 4.1 billion views on TikTok. As recently as this June, the cube made an appearance in the Disney film Toy Story 5. 

While the design of the classic 3×3 cube has been tweaked several times over the years, it’s never been rebuilt wholesale. The Spin Master team knew that they wanted to reimagine the cube’s design to bring it to new audiences—but they had to do it without compromising any of the design’s most recognizable features. 

How the Rubik’s Cube got a glow-up

The biggest change in the new Rubik’s Cube is the way it feels. Karen Harel, the design lead at Spin Master, says the main goal of the redesign was to improve the tactile experience for a modern audience of users that’s been familiarized with a wide breadth of fidget toys (we’re looking at you, fidget spinner). 

Solving the modern Rubik’s cube needed to feel easy, smooth, repeatable, and satisfying. And while the mechanism of the original toy has been beloved for decades, it has some significant shortcomings in those respects. Because of the original design’s sharp corners and compact form, every spin of the sides produces a lot of friction, meaning that the movement can feel labored and the cube’s pieces need to be in perfect alignment before they can be spun again. On the inside of the toy, tightly coiled springs make the whole construction extra rigid. 

So, Harel’s team started by rethinking the cube’s basic construction. On the inside of the cube, they worked with manufacturers to test a variety of different spring strengths, landing on an iteration with a coil that lets the cube flex a bit without coming apart. Then, they reimagined the shape of each piece of the cube, rounding out their edges so that only the very outer pieces retain their sharp corners. For speed cubers like Scholey, they also mattified the exterior tiles, to prevent light glare and give the fingertips a bit more grip.

With the new springs and reduced tile-on-tile friction, the cube spins much more freely, even when its sides are out of alignment. It’s a fun fidget toy for those who don’t even want to solve the puzzle. 

Scholey says the redesigned 3×3 cube serves as an entry point for customers to reengage with the Rubik’s brand. Since Spin Master acquired Rubik’s, it’s expanded the cube’s core principle to a multiplayer game called Rubik’s Rush and the board game Rubik’s Race. An upcoming partnership with Minecraft will aim to introduce Gen Alpha to the Rubik’s experience. 

“This, to me, is the epitome of Rubik’s—the movement toward becoming not just a 3×3 cube brand, but the most culturally relevant problem-solving brand that exists,” Scholey says. “Just as Lego is to building, Rubik’s is to solving.”

User reports of the problem keep mounting, as well, including from RTings staff and our own.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When shopping for an OLED TV, most prospective owners are concerned about burn-in, a problem that's been mostly mitigated, save perhaps for scenarios that involve leaving a TV on 24/7. This has been demonstrated by RTing's famous long-running test, but the site has found a new problem that seemingly pertains to pre-2023 LG WOLED panels: edge rot, where pixels progressively go dark around the edges, similar to a mold pattern.

RTings says that 14 out of the 16 LG televisions in its burn-in test have developed this problem in varying degrees, so 87.5% of a small test sample are affected. Those sets were all made between 2020 and 2022. The site says that several staff members saw the issue pop up on their personal televisions. Some searches indicate similar reports from users everywhere, and coincidentally, I learned about this just last week as a close friend sent photos of his 2019 LG TV.

The site says that the television sets bearing Samsung QDOLED panels in its burn-in test do not exhibit these symptoms, though it notes that it only has three such units in its test run. Additionally, since the test ran between 2022 and 2025, RTings says it can't assess whether LG's newer-generation WOLED panels have this issue, and notes that the manufacturing process for said panels reportedly improved in 2024.

Needless to say, most of the television sets in the study are going to be out of warranty. LG does have five-year panel warranty coverage, but only since 2021, and only covering higher-end units like the G, Z, and M series. It's still worth looking into this, in case you own a set from those lines and within the covered timeframe.

RTings asked for statements on the matter from both LG and Samsung, and interestingly, both firms discussed moisture directly or indirectly. LG stated that the issue "may occur in a very small number of OLED products under prolonged exposure to high-temperature, high-humidity, or other harsh environmental conditions."

It's not likely that RTing's test environment is especially humid, though, and the flurry of user reports with TVs sitting in their own living rooms doesn't lend much credence to LG's argument.

Meanwhile, Samsung is more specific, saying that it's implemented its proprietary Thin Film Encapsulation (TFE), a method for blocking ingress at the panel's edge. The firm says TFE "[eliminates] the root cause of border degradation and [ensures] long-term edge-to-edge uniformity where traditional structures falter."

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

RTings says that according to conversations with industry experts, "contamination during manufacturing, defects in the encapsulation of some OLED displays, or degradation of that encapsulation over time" may be at the source of the problem. The site's investigation also notes that some of its test units are affected by edge delamination.

The publication also believes the pixel-death problem may be related to power-on hours, while edge delamination could be dependent on the unit's age. It's unlikely that all user reports come in from people leaving their TVs on 24/7 or close to it, though. A sample size of one means little, but the TV is used sparingly at my friend's home, only around 30 hours per week — over nearly seven years, he's only seen the pixel refresh function come up twice.

For whatever it's worth, he notes that his set's dark spots mostly reside on the side closest to a portion of the wall with very minor humidity ingress from outside the building. The set is resting on furniture, not wall-mounted, though, so the relation between humidity and the edge rot is inconclusive at best.

#10586. The smartphone isn’t dead. But the battle to replace it is already on

The smartphone changed the world, but listening to some tech bosses lately, you’d be forgiven for thinking AI could threaten its dominance. 

The emerging debate about what comes after the smartphone is about much more than device sales. It’s about who controls the gateway to the customer, and who gets a cut of the money they spend.

And that battle has already begun. Amazon has blocked Meta’s AI assistant, Muse, from shopping on its e-commerce platform. The tech giants are already fighting over who gets between businesses and their customers.

Tellingly, Sam Altman, the OpenAI boss, has questioned the smartphone’s future. He argues that these gadgets are ill-suited to artificial intelligence.

I don’t think the smartphone is about to fall off a cliff. Apple’s iPhone pulled in $54.3 billion in sales in the three months to June 2026, a 22% rise on the year before. Unit shipments also shot up 15%, the tech analysis company IDC estimates. 

If the smartphone loses its dominance at all, it’ll be a slow burn, unlike its rapid rise, having overtaken PC shipments just three and a half years after the iPhone launched in 2007.

However, the smartphone’s stranglehold on how we access the digital world could be challenged by new technologies.

Smart glasses are jostling for pole position. But they come with drawbacks, including added weight and limited battery life. One independent tester burned through half the battery in Ray-Ban Meta smart glasses in just an hour of heavy video use.

And let’s face it: They look pretty dorky too.

For all the hype, they’re still David to the smartphone’s Goliath. Analysts forecast 15 million pairs will have shipped this year, a drop in the ocean compared to more than a billion smartphones.

So it’ll probably take a combination of technologies to knock the smartphone off its perch.

One is Apple’s AirPods wireless earphones. They already provide hands-free access to Siri, its voice assistant. But a recent AI upgrade now allows some users to hold chatbot-style conversations with them.

Apple is also reportedly developing AirPods with built-in cameras, to give Siri eyes as well as ears. You could look at ingredients and ask for a recipe, say, or get walking directions based on your surroundings, without pulling out your smartphone.

Facebook’s parent Meta, meanwhile, plans to launch the stand-alone device Muse Charm. It’s small enough to hang from a keychain; a screen and voice controls provide access to Muse, Meta’s AI assistant.

Muse itself has exploded in popularity since its September launch, quickly becoming the most downloaded app in the U.S.

The battle for control

The battle to control the next digital interface is likely to be fought on three fronts: hardware, distribution, and intelligence.

Apple is a heavyweight in hardware, owing to its $146 billion cash and investment pile, engineering prowess and 2.5 billion active devices. Huawei and Samsung are also contenders.

In the distribution layer, Amazon has upgraded Alexa with AI, enabling users to have natural conversations with the voice assistant, and for it to carry out tasks such as booking restaurants. Alexa is already connected to more than 600 million devices.

Google’s AI-powered search summaries reach more than 2.5 billion people monthly. And Google’s Android software powers more than 3 billion devices worldwide.

Meta is another contender. Beyond selling hardware, it could become a powerful middleman, taking a cut of transactions handled by its AI assistant, from shopping to travel bookings.

Amazon has likewise considered taking a cut of purchases handled by Alexa, even as it blocks Muse from shopping on its platform.

The third battleground is intelligence, where the frontier AI labs OpenAI and Anthropic are frontrunners. OpenAI is also muscling into hardware; it acquired Io, Sir Jony Ive’s startup, for $6.5 billion. The former Apple design chief and OpenAI are developing AI-powered devices, which could give ChatGPT a direct route to consumers instead of relying on other companies for distribution. 

The commercial prize

Hardware and distribution may be the biggest prizes of all, particularly as AI models are likely to become commodities: interchangeable. History suggests whoever cracks the next interface stands to make a fortune, at least in the medium term.

By the mid-1990s, IBM still controlled three-quarters of the mainframe market. Microsoft then built a monopoly in PC operating systems, nabbing some 90% of the market throughout the 1990s. Apple then raked in billions from iPhone sales, reaching almost $210 billion in 2025.

The wider effects are the hardest to envision. Who knew the smartphone would upend the taxi industry? Insurance, telecoms, and banking are at risk today, especially if AI agents start shopping around on customers’ behalf.

AI agents could kill the friction—the time and hassle of shopping around and swapping service providers—that keeps customers wedded to insurance policies, bank accounts, and phone contracts.

For business leaders, the biggest mistake would be trying to build the next interface themselves. Unless technology is your business, leave that battle to the tech giants. But don’t sit on the sidelines either. If AI agents become the new gateway to your customers, you could lose control of how they find, compare, and buy your products.

Companies should be probing whether AI agents can locate their products, compare them with rivals, and complete purchases for customers, without those customers ever needing to land on their websites.

The smartphone’s days are far from numbered. But companies that fail to prepare for what comes next risk ceding their customer relationships to the companies who control the next interface.

#10584. Google’s AI search fumbles leave schools in the lurch

Good morning, and happy Ocarina of Time day to all who celebrate. Oh, and speaking of celebrating: congratulations to the real winners of the midterms, YouTube and Twitch streamers.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

It’s hard to overstate how reliant US school districts are on Google. Almost every school board in the country purchases Chromebooks for their students, many of whom are able to take them home and treat them as personal laptops. IT and procurement managers like them because they’re relatively cheap and offer zero-touch enrollment, which lowers upfront and ongoing maintenance costs. Teachers also like them because Google Classroom is relatively intuitive and ChromeOS is optimized for most other web-based education platforms.

But this reliance on Google’s tech stack comes at a cost: school boards are forced to comply with the company’s capricious terms of service, in particular when it comes to the creeping proliferation of AI across its products.

Things came to a head earlier this week when the New York Times reported that changes to the Google Search experience in schools will require opting into Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and, perhaps most importantly, AI Overviews. The move actually comes after Google was pressured to make student AI usage opt-in. Google’s justification: search and AI are now “deeply integrated” in the tech stack, so disabling one means disabling the other.

Google’s alternative to AI Overviews is a new Guided Learning feature, which replaces search’s primary function — solving a problem — with a step-by-step guide that helps a student understand said problem.

Former FTC Chair Lina Khan took to X to decry the move: “Google is using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI,” she said. “This highlights the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service. Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened. As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

I have a feeling Google will need to walk back this decision in some form. As 9to5’s Ben Schoon points out, it’s comically easy to disable AI Overviews in the current version of Google Search, and it doesn’t seem to have a detrimental impact on the subsequent results. But Google is likely worried that if it is forced to carve out versions of the search experience for specific user bases, revenue from its burgeoning AI Overviews ad business may be impacted.

Even if Google sticks to its proverbial guns, the move exposes a rift between the public, particularly parents, and the tech giants pushing AI on their users. Furthermore, the AI boom has had a direct impact on educators’ ability to purchase new hardware and upgrade old Chromebook fleets, since component prices have increased by as much as 5x over the past year. Some lower-income districts are even recalling Chromebooks from students, reverting to a shared laptop pool to save money.

For a much less complicated relationship with Google Search, try its Ocarina of Time Easter egg. Search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” then tap the Master Sword to travel back to a 1998-style results page, complete with Link’s sound effects. It’s a trip down memory lane to a time when, as even Google acknowledges, things were a whole lot simpler.

Was AI on the ballot? According to Ben Thompson at Stratechery, it was in a big way. Democrats and Republicans both rejected data centers, but Democrats want to actually pass some regulation reining in the hyperscalers’ and frontier labs’ ambitions.

First we were short on compute, but that ramped; now we are short on memory, but that will ramp; we were coming close to being short on power, but that will be rectified. The final constraint is political, and that may be the most imposing constraint of all. Not only is it harder to solve, but it actually interferes with solving the other constraints. Many of the theories about AI assume that it becomes a commodity; I think it’s just as likely we are looking at scarcity for much longer than we expected, which will have ripple effects on everything.

Is it possible to pass broad AI regulation that will slow down an industry that has been allowed to run unchecked since Trump’s reelection in 2024? And is it in the best interest of these companies to support it, even if it means a short-term slowdown at a time when the two biggest AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, are about to IPO?

Some fascinating developments in the $6.3 billion Binance hack: turns out it was yet another case of social engineering. A Binance engineer was lured into a fake job interview, before which he downloaded malware that let hackers breach his computer and, eventually, others on the network.

After my discussion with Will last week about the death of browsers, it came as no surprise to learn that Brave is being sued by the New York Times for copyright infringement. Brave is accused of scraping paywalled material from sites like nytimes.com and selling access to it through a search API to AI companies, which use it as training data. Yes, it’s difficult to earn money as a browser maker (see Arc for another compelling example), but this is particularly egregious, even for a company familiar with shady business practices.

You can also support us by becoming a 9to5Google Pro member, starting at just $5/month. You get bonus podcast episodes, access to our members-only Discord server, and lots more.

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Tuesday.

#10582. Motorola Signature 27 goes official with true flagship specs and a $1,599 price tag

Motorola is officially back in the flagship space, with its new Signature 27 phone available for pre-order starting today, but it’ll cost a pretty penny.

Motorola Signature 27 is, in every sense of the word, a true and proper flagship smartphone, with specs that are more or less unmatched in the US and hold up even in international markets.

The full specs on Motorola Signature 27 include:

Add it all up, this is a true flagship like we’ve never really seen from Motorola, especially in the US.

Motorola also touts that Signature 27 is compatible with GrapheneOS, has an “ArcticMesh” vapor chamber cooling system with diamond-infused thermal gel, and reiterates just how bright that display is. The Tandem OLED technology in use is also mentioned to be 34% more energy efficient and have a 121% longer lifespan compared to traditional OLED panels. The speakers are also tuned by Bang & Olufsen, a name we’ve not seen in the Android space since LG was still in the game.

On the software side, Android 17 is available out of the box with the promise of 7 years of OS upgrades and security updates – no promises on how often those will be delivered, though. Motorola is also the first brand outside of Pixel to fully embrace Gemini Intelligence, with Gboard Rambler, “Create My Widget,” app automation, and Sign-To-Text ASL translation all supported on Signature 27.

Battery life should be excellent with a 7,000 mAh cell inside that Motorola says charges at 90W (wired) and uses silicon-carbon battery tech. Wireless charging speeds top out at 50W on Motorola’s charger and it sounds like it maxes out at 15W on other devices based on the company’s communication with us. There appears to be no Qi2 support either, sadly, even though Motorola supports the tech elsewhere in its lineup.

The cameras are not holding back either. The 50MP main shooter uses a Sony Lytia L910 sensor with LOFIC for improved color, brightness, and video, with two other 50MP sensors for the ultrawide and front-facing cameras. There’s also a 200MP periscope telephoto camera that’s only 3x optical quality, but goes all the way to 100x hybrid zoom. Motorola has also equipped the device with a dedicated EVS action sensor that is specifically used to improve clarity and sharpness on fast-moving objects while using an “Action Shot” mode.

Truly, a full flagship across the board, but oh boy it is not cheap.

Motorola Signature 27 is launching in the US for $1,599 on November 19, with pre-orders open today. That’s for the 512GB/12GB variant that’s also the only one coming to the US, with colors including “Coal Smoke” and “Capulet Olive.” There’s no availability coming to Canada at this time, but US buyers can get a free Moto Watch Ultra (the one with Wear OS) with their pre-order from Motorola.com for a “limited time.” That same offer also includes a free year of the “motocare” extended warranty. There’s no word of carrier launches yet, but Motorola says US units are “universally unlocked.”

What do you think of Motorola Signature 27, and would you pay $1,599 for it?

#10580. ‘I am a writer who wanted to be a painter’: Tobias Frere-Jones on the creative mash-up that led him to typeface design

Tobias Frere-Jones has designed typefaces for clients like Martha Stewart Living, the New York City Metropolitan Transportation Authority, Carnegie Hall, Stacey Abrams, Barack Obama’s 2008 campaign, Zohran Mamdani’s mayoral office, the American Civil Liberties Union, New York City Football Club, and many more. Years ago, a friend of his asked him what he thought what letters are made of. Frere-Jones’s answer came quickly and in a single word: negotiation. 

As a culture, we agree that letters take a certain shape and carry specific meaning, says Frere-Jones. Running his own typeface foundry in Brooklyn, he describes himself a translator, oscillating between a client’s idea of what their brand or project communicates and his own understanding of the shapes that make letters and what any given design of those shapes communicates to the people looking at it. 

Says Frere-Jones: “An important part of my process is to be able to either alternate between two modes—engineer or artist—or hold them in my head simultaneously so they can collaborate with one another.” 

The shortest version of my story is that I am a writer who wanted to be a painter. That’s how I came to this. The slightly longer version is that through high school, I was pursuing every kind of visual art I could get my hands onto: drawing and painting and photography. I also enjoyed writing. My brother, father, and great-grandfather were all writers. I came to settle on abstract painting. That’s where I thought I’d want to go. I thought I’d have to choose one and abandon the others. I discovered by accident through my father’s work in advertising that there was this thing called typeface design. I realized it was an intersection of working with abstract expressive form and language at the same time. I wasn’t even looking for it because I didn’t think it was possible. 

In 1992, no one was teaching this. I realized that I was not going to learn what I wanted to learn in school. At the time, typeface was too specialized to include in most design school curriculums. That has since changed. My first job was a junior designer at the Font Bureau in Boston. I was going to learn on the job, which is how designers centuries before me also learned. I was most acutely aware of all of the things I didn’t know. I was trying to soak up everything the designer I worked for gave me—a guy by the name of David Berlow. 

There are two schools of thought on how designers position themselves in the world. One is that they are engineers; they are solving problems and maximizing results and all of the vocabulary that comes through that more practical viewpoint. The other is that they are half a step removed from being an artist; they bring intuition and expression that can be this necessary color and animating force in what they provide for their clients. Both are true. An important part of my process is to be able to either alternate between these two modes—engineer or artist—or hold them in my head simultaneously so they can collaborate with one another. That kind of crossover is an important part of how I work. 

The two fields that I turn to most frequently are music and history. In a very amateur way, I play keyboard and synthesizer and make sound audio collages. I think that has a lot of overlap in typeface design and in programming—which is something that’s very close to typeface design. It’s really a very narrow palette of elements. There are only 12 notes, really, in Western music. Everything is a variant or a combination after that. The other place where I give my attention is history. I’m particularly interested in the history of New York. It’s my hometown, and it’s all around me. It feels parallel in the same way that the alphabet is this set of shapes that have meaning because we all agree they have meaning. The city and its streets have a similar aspect in being developed, handed down, and modified, and interpreted and handed down again, resting critically on consensus as to how this space works and what it’s for. 

The design and production of typefaces has always been close to the technology that delivers it. It’s involved right from the start, whether this is someone cutting punches for hand casting in the 15th century or making machine drawings for automated line casters or making digital fonts now. We need to be really, really close to the technology. There are designers who are also programmers; I can read code and see what it does, but I can’t write it myself. 

There is a lot of attention, and also anxiety, directed to the portfolio a student will have when they graduate. If they want to find a job they have to be showing something. But when I’m looking at designers, I’m looking less at the actual thing in front of me, but at how deeply they understood what they made. That’s hard to tell from looking at a résumé. That has to come from a conversation. I’ll ask plenty of questions I already know the answer to, but I want to see how they answer it and what it says about their priorities and how they think. 

My job is to be a translator. I translate between their [clients’] brand objectives and a set of shapes. I need to translate one into the other. It’s an important part of how I work with clients, though I won’t describe it that way. I will describe it in whatever terms that are native to them and their internal culture and their problems. I need to translate a cultural history into something that will be meaningful to the chief marketing officer, and so on. Often, that will mean speaking to different stakeholders in different ways. The legal team will care about one thing and the web developers will care about something else. The CFO will care about something else altogether. If I can speak to all of them, and make everyone feel like they are heard, that’s the basis of a successful project.

A long time ago a colleague of mine was writing an article, and asked his friends to give the quickest possible answer: What are letters made of? Negotiation. They are made of a designer negotiating between a design concept and the strict but weirdly difficult-to-express rules about what the alphabet is, what is a letter and what isn’t. The negotiation between one period of history and next about what’s normal and what’s weird about typographic style. 

One of the things I collect is children’s spelling primers, the first book a child would have. It’s fascinating to see what should be a starting point for a child to think what letters are. Turn-of-the-century America and 19th century Germany are going to look kind of weird to us now but that was their idea of normal. The way that has drifted over time is telling. It’s fascinating all by itself, but it is something as designers we need to keep in mind. In those forms that are presented as normal. I think there are assumptions about the context that they’re being seen in. Whether that’s a social context or technological context—and I think there is an unspoken connection to what has preceded it, that is not explicitly pointed to, but I think is still felt.

I find my best or clearest solutions when I’m talking something out. I find it valuable to work with people with whom I can have some meandering conversation and, sort of in a less guarded or less structured moment, see something I had missed before. I think being able to describe the problem in the clearest, most concise way is a really reliable way to figure out what the solution ought to be.

My days are difficult to have a really reliable routine. So much will depend on what kinds of conversations we are having with clients, what kind of proposals or pitches. I try to limit interruptions, or herd them into spaces in the day so the space in between can be spent in creative work and thinking. I’ll check headlines in the morning, look at lunch, and then again on the way home. I try to keep all of that clear for the rest of the day, and be conscious that this little technological marvel of a phone is so adept at shredding my concentration. 

I try to be aware of how much time and energy gets lost in switching gears—whatever mode it is—business planning or design concept. I try to stay there for at least half an hour or ideally a full hour before I turn to something else.

I think there’s a parallel between this field and, say, costume design for films. Even if we don’t explicitly consciously notice what a character in a film is wearing, it will still be there exerting an influence in the same way the color cast of a scene will quietly set a tone, the way the texture of a fabric and a suit jacket and the way its cut and fit will very quietly tell you, “This guy is the villain.” That’s the kind of space that typography can work in. It can also be much more overt and draw attention to itself and be very upfront about its characterization. 

More people have the experience of needing to choose what the font is. They can see for themselves what the effect is if they put something on Instagram and format the caption like this or like that. They can see how it affects what they are trying to say. 

This business has gone through a lot of changes in the last 10 or 20 years, but the public always seems to be getting more sophisticated when it comes to typography and aware of it and the work we do. I can remember when I would need some amount of time to explain to someone what my job is and how someone can make a living at doing that. It’s been years since I’ve had to explain my work like that to anybody.

#10579. Moto Buds Ultra 27 and Watch Fit 27 help complete the company’s new premium ecosystem

The duo of Motorola accessories will see a North American release in the coming months, featuring Motorola Qira integration.

If you’re planning on upgrading to the extremely ultra-level Motorola Signature 27 flagship phone this year, I have good news for you: you’ll have plenty of time to save up for Motorola’s accompanying accessories after you buy the $1,600 phone. Motorola isn’t announcing a specific release date or price for either the Moto Buds Ultra 27 or Moto Watch Fit 27, but here’s what you can look forward to when they eventually come out.

Starting with the Moto Buds Ultra 27, which Motorola calls its “most feature-packed pair of earbuds yet.” These sport Bose-branded ANC and SoundDesign tuning, plus six high-SNR microphones with two dedicated Voice Pickup Units in what’s now become a conventional wireless earbud design with tips. Motorola says it created the microphones not just for great sound quality, but also for content creation, which means they’re clearly confident in the quality of these mics.

The buds deliver around 8 hours of continuous playback on a single charge, and the included case adds another 37 hours of use. They’ve also got built-in Motorola Qira support, plus AI Live Translate, head gestures, dual connection and audio share (so you can pair them with two devices, or pair two sets of buds with one device), plus a wear detection auto-pause feature.

They also sport IP57 protection, support Google’s Find Hub, and feature 11mm dynamic drivers for crisp audio. Moto Buds Ultra 27 will debut sometime in the near future in two colorways: Pantone Reflecting Pond (dark blue), and Pantone Temple Gray.

The Moto Watch Fit 27 is the antithesis of the recently launched Motorola Watch Ultra, focusing more on daily health than anything else. That means nearly two weeks of battery life on a single charge (instead of a day or two as Wear OS watches have), plus a Polar-powered health engine that’s designed to get your health metrics right the first time.

The square design features a rotating crown and a customizable physical button below the crown, IP68 and 5ATM protection, Bluetooth connection, built-in Motorola Qira support, all in an aluminum frame. A 1.85-inch gOLED display sports ultra-thin 2mm bezels and Gorilla Glass 3 on top. The display supports always-on visibility, and the built-in Qira assistant gives you hands-free command of the watch’s abilities.

As for health metrics, this one supports SPO2 monitoring, stress insights, heart rate powered by Polar, menstrual cycle tracking, auto activity detection, step and distance counter, over 100 tracked workouts, and dual-frequency GPS for accurate tracking. The Polar engine also supports holistic tracking, including sleep, calories, inactivity, and active workouts. Moto Watch Fit 27 launches sometime in the future in three colorways: Pantone Stratified Sea, Pantone Pigeon, and Pantone Ballerina.
